Consideration of funding proposals – 
Addendum XII 
Funding proposal package for FP237 
Summary 
This addendum contains the following seven parts: 
a) A funding proposal titled "E-Motion : E-Mobility and Low Carbon Transportation";
b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal
point(s);
c)
Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;
d) Secretariat’s assessment;
e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment;
f)
Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's
assessment; and
g) Gender documentation.
It is noted that gender documentation has been updated.

Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template 
• 
Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc. 
• 
Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
• 
The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual 
service standard time. 
• 
The recommended font is Arial, size 11.  
• 
Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. 
Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.    
 
Please submit the completed proposal to: 
fundingproposal@gcfund.org 
Please use the following name convention for the file name: 
“FP-[Accredited Entity Short Name]-[Country/Region]-[YYYY/MM/DD]”

A.20. Executing Entity 
information 
• 
Agence Française de Développement (AFD) is a financing company and 
a public industrial and commercial institution, acting as financer, advisor, 
partner and implementer to mobilise finance and expertise for development 
projects in emerging and developing countries. It is the main implementing 
agency for France’s official development assistance to developing countries 
and overseas territories. Focusing on climate, biodiversity, peace, 
education, urban development, health and governance, AFD teams carry 
out more than 4,000 projects in France’s overseas departments and 
territories and in 115 other countries. AFD is both the accredited entity and 
the executing entity.  
 
• 
PROPARCO (société de Promotion et Participation à la Coopération 
économique). PROPARCO is a subsidiary of AFD, hence acts as agent 
in the name and on behalf of AFD, focused on private sector 
development, and provides funding (loans, guarantees, equity and 
quasi-equity) and support (technical assistance and expertise) to both 
businesses and financial intermediaries across Africa, Asia, Latin 
America and the Middle-East. The financing of infrastructure projects 
represents more than 30% of PROPARCO’s activities in these 
countries. PROPARCO will implement the financing activities and TA of 
the Programme where Project Owners (PO) belong to private sector. 
PROPARCO’s head office is located in Paris.  
 
• 
Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ). GIZ is 
the German Agency for International Cooperation. It is a public-benefit 
federal enterprise. GIZ’s sole shareholder is the Federal Republic of 
Germany, represented in Germany by the Federal Ministry for Economic 
Cooperation and Development (BMZ) and the Federal Ministry of Finance 
(BMF). GIZ is headquartered in Bonn and Eschborn but operates from about 
120 countries around the globe. 70% of its 20,000 employees are national 
staff.  
 
A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages) 
 
Climate Context 
Transport contributes almost one-quarter of the current global energy-related greenhouse gas (GHG) 
emissions and is growing faster than any other energy end-use sector. Limiting the global temperature 
increase to below 2 degrees Celsius requires changing this transport emissions trajectory, which involves 
the development of an integrated electromobility ecosystem encompassing various transport modes, 
coupled with the low-carbon production of electricity and hydrogen (Paris Declaration on Electro-Mobility 
and Climate Change & Call to Action, 2015). To achieve this goal International Energy Agency (IEA) 
modelling says EVs need to represent 35% of global sales in 2030.  
Avoid-Shift-Improve (ASI) has been a long-standing framework for classifying mitigation interventions in 
the transport sector. A new dimension needs to be added to this framework: EASI: Electrify-Avoid-Shift-
Improve. Electrification is a game-changer and a must if carbon neutrality shall be achieved. Shift and 
improve measures without electrification are not capable to reach carbon neutrality in the transport sector.  
Electrification coupled with a fossil-free grid creates this opportunity. Many Latin American countries have 
recognized this fact and have started to develop a regulatory framework to go toward this direction by 
introducing Electric Vehicle (EV) tax incentives, EV targets, and favourable regulatory systems e.g. for e-
buses (see Section B1 and Annex 2 Report 1). However, maximum efficiency is still required to reduce 
electricity demand and thus cost and challenges in the energy system. Additionally, other challenges in the 
sector, such as congestion, safety, access, health can be addressed through the use of more efficient

=== PAGE 11 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 5 
 
modes of transport, such as buses, and through non-motorised alternatives. Developing an electrified 
public transport as the backbone of urban transport is therefore key to a sustainable mobility system. 
Social and Environmental Context 
Air pollution is a large problem in most Latin American cities with levels affecting seriously human health. 
According to the latest urban air quality database, 98% of cities in low- and middle-income countries with 
more than 100,000 inhabitants do not meet World Health Organization (WHO) air quality guidelines. The 
WHO estimates that annually 4.2 million deaths result due to excessive exposure to fine particulate matter 
from ambient air pollution equivalent to 7.6% of all deaths. The transport sector is a major source of air 
pollution.  
The COVID’19 pandemic has created an unprecedented challenge for many Latin American countries, 
from the health and economic perspectives, but it is also opening new opportunities for an accelerated 
sustainable transformation of its energy, transport, and urban landscapes while contributing to economic 
recovery. A recent report prepared by the International Labor Organization (ILO) shows that fostering of 
electric mobility can have a significant positive job impact (ILO, 2020). 
Country Conditions and Ownership 
Latin America has strong enabling conditions in terms of clean transport and e-mobility. The region has 
one of the cleanest electricity generation portfolios in the world with the median of Program countries 
having a carbon grid factor of 0.252 kgCO2e/kWh. The adoption of an e-mobility strategy would allow the 
countries to move towards a decarbonisation pathway. The updated Nationally Determined Contributions 
(NDCs) of Program countries all include measures to reduce transport emissions. The majority of countries 
have included in the NDCs electric mobility as a core mitigation measure to reduce transport emissions 
and have formulated concrete electrification targets through regulations, policies or EV roadmaps. 
The main motivating forces to foster e-mobility in the countries included in the Program are (i) reduce GHG 
emissions; (ii) improve air quality and reduce health problems associated with air pollution; (iii) reduce 
dependency on imported fossil fuels and exposure to external price shocks; (iv) achieve positive economic 
impacts related to reduced health costs, reduced fuel import bill and job creation; (v) Participate in the new 
growth industry of EV and battery manufacturing; (iv) increase the attractiveness of public transportation 
vis-a-vis private motorized transport. 
This Program has been developed in close coordination with CAF whose FP195 has been approved by the 
GCF at B34 and which implements similar activities in Uruguay, Paraguay and Panama (private and public 
sector). The two programs will be closely coordinated by CAF and AFD and shall be implemented under 
the same label “E-Motion”.   
 
Intervention Strategy 
The Programs goal is to accelerate Electric Vehicle (EV) deployment and enable a large-scale regional 
transition towards electro-mobility in Latin America through financial and technical assistance.  The 
Program implements interventions to kick-start EV mass deployment significantly earlier than under a 
Business as Usual (BAU) scenario by reducing the risk profile of investments and by comprehensive 
technical assistance. The key strategic value of the Program is that it functions as market accelerator 
enabling a far faster uptake of e-mobility than under a BAU scenario avoiding a lock-in of long-lived assets 
in fossil technology. The Program fills the gap between initial pilots already existing in the region and long-
term targets. These interventions are made in a time where e-mobility is commercially not yet viable and 
thus require initial investment support -like is the case in all countries which have a significant uptake of e-
mobility.  
The Program focus is on electrification of public transport buses. Electrification of buses is not a stand-
alone measure but an important component to modernize and to upgrade the public transport system of

=== PAGE 12 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 6 
 
countries. New electric buses position the public transport system as modern, environmentally friendly and 
attractive. The investment in electric buses is linked with investments to improve the public transport 
system and non-motorized transport (NMT) in urban areas as well as transport demand management 
measures which result in increasing the attractiveness of low carbon forms of mobility. This results in a 
mode shift towards zero or ultra-low mobility systems. The Program directly modernizes public transport 
systems through establishing new business models, which increase the financial stability and economic 
efficiency of public transport systems e.g. through separation of bus ownership and bus operations and 
indirectly by realizing e-bus investments in cities which have programs to foster NMT and public transport 
financed nationally and/or internationally i.e. the complementarity of e-bus investments to such efforts is a 
selection criteria for investment projects of the Program. As example medium sized Colombian cities which 
are targeted by this Program have formulated urban mobility plans with the aim of modernizing and 
upgrading public transport whilst also fostering NMT and components of transit-oriented development. 
Program activities improve the grid resilience and the resilience of urban infrastructure. All investments in 
grid infrastructure and bus depot up-gradings will be made climate resilient. Used batteries from EVs have 
a second-life application where they can serve as storage units and thereby (i) increase the resilience and 
independence of the grid at critical spots such as hospitals or schools; (ii) increase the grid resilience and 
reduce costs of renewable grids by increasing storage capacity capable of taking up electricity during times 
of over-supply; (iii) act as peak shavers in urban settings.  
The program will also benefit from the activities implemented by GIZ and AFD in all program countries 
under the framework of the EUROCLIMA+ initiative whose purpose is to develop national policies for urban 
mobility and integrated multi-modal planning at the city level, in order to accelerate the transition of Latin 
American cities towards sustainable urban mobility with low carbon emissions. As a result, Euroclima+ will 
contribute to create the favorable conditions for the development of e-mobility in the framework of 
integrated and sustainable urban mobility plans. The TA of the FP will be limited to activities which are 
complementary to the ones already realized within EUROCLIMA. The Budgetary credit lines financed by 
AFD and supporting a high level political dialogue on transport decarbonization in Costa Rica and 
Dominican Republic also ensure that the projects implemented in these countries through E-Motion will 
have a key impact in public transport restructuration and decarbonization.  
Barriers to EV Deployment and Program Approach to Overcome Barriers 
Main barriers to EV deployment are (i) 2-3x higher Capital Expenditure (CAPEX) of EVs resulting in higher 
capital requirements, higher finance and insurance costs and higher risk exposure due to higher debt 
levels. The incremental CAPEX of e-buses result in incremental capital required from the investor and in 
additional finance costs compared to a fossil unit; (ii) lack of financial profitability for EVs in involved 
countries with long payback times; (iii) higher risk of EVs compared to fossil vehicles due to unknown 
performance and cost levels and potential income losses; (iv) governance structures and business models 
not conducive to EV deployment, especially in the public transport sector; (v) limited know-how on optimal 
EV ecosystem design, maintenance & operations; (vi) EV targets have been established by many 
countries but concrete and tangible support policies which are conducive to EV uptake are partially 
missing. All involved countries have a large interest in promoting e-mobility and various have also 
established fiscal incentives which are however targeted primarily to passenger cars. In all countries e-bus 
projects can be realized without requiring a change of the existing policy framework. The intervention 
strategy is to use concrete implementation projects to foster policies, regulations and frameworks 
conducive to a widespread adoption of e-buses. This can best be done by having concrete workable 
implementation cases e.g. of concession contracts or business models such as separation of bus 
ownership and bus operations conducive to e-buses. Such approaches together with high quality e-bus 
system design enables e-buses to be commercially competitive to fossil units. Only once it is apparent that 
e-buses can operate in a reliable manner and with comparable total costs to diesel buses will 
governments, city authorities, transport operators and other stakeholders be willing to establish and 
actually implement policy targets such as a ban of fossil buses. Stand-alone policy recommendations

=== PAGE 13 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 7 
 
without the proof of concept have limited impact. Stakeholders consulted by the Program have repeatedly 
stressed the point that investment projects involving significant fleets (not pilot projects) need to be 
implemented prior to taking critical regulatory decisions which affect public transport services. The proof of 
concept is thus critical and the sequencing of starting with investment projects which are then used as 
concept approach accompanied and followed by policy advice is therefore core to this Program.  Not all 
counties experience the same magnitude of barriers and in some, e.g. Colombia, at least in some cities 
innovative business models are already under trial. The experience of Colombia as well as Chile (not part 
of this Program) is however, that the business models and financial structuring for large cities such as 
Bogota or Santiago cannot be transferred to medium and smaller cities where for example private capital 
has much less interest to participate due to offering smaller markets, financially fragile transport operators 
and weaker local city authorities including limitations in legal structures such as concession contracts. 
Charging infrastructure, which is a core barrier for passenger car deployment, is not listed as barrier due to 
the Program focus on electric buses which do not rely on a public charging network but have proprietary 
chargers either at the bus depot or along/close to their routes. 
The Program tackles these barriers and offers a comprehensive e-mobility package including financial and 
technical assistance thereby improving the policy framework and the ecosystem and removing barriers that 
enable the mass deployment of EVs. The barrier of higher initial CAPEX and higher finance costs is 
tackled through concessional loans and through technical assistance (TA) which promote new business 
models with lower costs and risks for public transport (PT) operators and through an optimal e-bus system 
design which reduces costs and operational risks. The barriers of lack of financial profitability and high 
risks are overcome through concessional loans and TA which assists cities in designing optimal e-bus 
systems, which has a knowledge exchange platform and allows to learn from experiences of other cities in 
Latin America, and through training and capacity building of staff. The policy and regulatory work done with 
TA is also critical to increase profitability of e-bus investments e.g. through establishing concession 
contract lengths in line with the (longer) lifespan of e-buses or through tender documents which favour the 
participation of e-buses based on their positive environmental and health impact. As mentioned the policy 
and regulatory work builds upon the proof of concept investment projects and uses these to promote 
successful and workable policies based on concrete results. Stakeholders from the government and 
private sector are willing to establish and implement policies such as targets for EVs or ban of fossil 
vehicles but it needs to be demonstrated locally that this will not affect adversely the public transport 
system including its costs.  
Program Components 
For each country a market assessment has been performed analyzing (i) the current financial viability per 
vehicle category in terms of total cost of ownership, profitability, cash-flow and debt structure; (ii) financial 
instruments and support structures to overcome viability barriers; (iii) market readiness of EVs. 
Interventions are designed to accelerate market readiness and to foster widespread commercial 
deployment of EVs without further financial assistance.  
Financial assistance is targeted to around 4 e-mobility projects in different countries focusing on e-bus 
systems and PT upgrading. The selection is based on the criteria established in section B3. E-mobility 
system investments include vehicles, charging infrastructure, grid upgrades and bus depot upgrades and 
are linked to urban public transport and NMT improvements. At maximum an individual country receives 
35% of the total GCF funding. All countries receive technical assistance to enhance their e-mobility 
framework and policies. Investment projects can be executed in any of the participating country and are 
selected based on high quality proposals put forward on a running base to the AE. The available funds 
give an indication of investments in minimum 4 projects – for calculation and programming purposes the 
most promising and advanced projects have been used. In practice however, this might have variations 
and no country is included from proposing investment projects. During Program design city authorities, 
financial institutions and transport operators were consulted on upcoming investment projects for bus 
replacement or expansion of buse fleets and where interest would exist to purchase electric units if

=== PAGE 14 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 8 
 
technically and financially viable. The individual projects are listed in the country assessments included in 
Annex 3. For facility of review a seosrate excel sheet has been created listing all pre-identified investment 
projects (see Annex 3: List Investment Projects).  
Technical Assistance will focus mainly on achieving Output 1.1: Establishment of a sustainable mobility 
ecosystem encouraging electrification and a shift to public transport. Work will be done to overcome the 
barriers associated with this output through the implementation of a set of activities that were specifically 
selected and subsequently structured according to a differentiation of scope at regional, national or local 
levels. Following TA activities are implemented in Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican 
Republic, Mexico and Peru: (i) establishment of an e-mobility conducive ecosystem on the local, national 
and regional level by providing TA to create a policy and business framework conducive for massive 
deployment of EVs on a local level (project design, business model design and development, city EV 
policies, training etc.), national level (sectoral electrification roadmaps, support of national enabling policies 
for EV deployment, advice on battery re-usage, recycling and disposal) and a regional level (capacity 
building, knowledge materials, outreach and dissemination); (ii) Project sourcing, structuring and 
development; (iii) Program management. The Program has in addition a comprehensive and ambitious 
gender action plan targeting to improve gender equality and reduce sexual harassment in public transport. 
Program Impacts 
 
The Program supports a paradigm change to low-emission transportation systems. Impacts to be achieved 
are (i) GHG reduction: EVs have significantly lower lifecycle GHG emissions than fossil units in all 
countries included in the Program and mode shift triggered through program investments and technical 
assistance from modes with high levels of carbon emisisons per passenger-km to low carbon transport 
modes; (ii) improved air quality; (iii) reduced fossil fuel import dependency; (iv) increased energy efficiency: 
EVs are up to 4x more energy efficient than fossil vehicles.  
 
Program key figures are: 
• 
Total Program volume 210.98 MEUR and 103.89 MUSD 
• 
GCF investment 56 MEUR and 40.264 MUSD of concessional loan and 22.3 MEUR grant for TA  
• 
Co-finance ratio 62%  
• 
Direct mitigation impact over lifetime of assets 4,334,051 MtCO2e  
• 
Direct impact on reduced air pollutants and energy saved 
• 
Indirect large GHG impact due to accelerated uptake of EVs 
• 
Economic internal rate of 16% of the total Program 
 
Program Sustainability 
E-mobility is still in a learning stage in Latin America. It therefore requires at this stage FA and TA. 
However, due to the efforts of the Program this is only required for a transitional period. By 2025 the cost 
of e-buses is expected to be at par with fossil units. However, the experience is that even if total cost of 
ownership of e-buses is below those of fossil untis, this does not automatically derive in operators 
purchasing such units. The initial CAPEX including charing infrastructure remains factor 2-3 higher thus 
requiring also more equity of investors and risks associated with e-buses (including risks of adequate 
maintenance) prevent investors from purchasing such units as long as insufficient concrete implementation 
experience is available in the country which clearly show that e-buses have comparable reliability and 
availability rates as fossil units, that actual operational costs are as expected (perceived risk of much 
higher than promoised energy consumption of e-buses) and that maintenance and repairs can be 
managed (this is also related in many countries to concerns over purchasing buses from countries with no 
local maintenance structure in place). The investments in projects and the TA allow to overcome the 
barriers of profitability and high upfront CAPEX through successfully implementing new business models

=== PAGE 15 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 9 
 
and a regulatory and policy environment which favour E-mobility investments. Interchange of experience 
and knowledge management instruments allow to learn from experiences and to structure in an optimal 
manner new investment projects thereby reducing costs and risks. Thus market forces of decreasing 
prices work hand in hand with Program activities which create an enabling environment with lower risk and 
higher profitability for follow-up investors in e-mobility, which will not longer require further technical or 
financial support.  
Program Replicability and Transformational Impact 
The potential market for e-mobility is very large in Latin America. However, especially secondary cities are 
facing financial and technical barriers towards adopting e-mobility. Whilst pilot projects have been 
successful in reducing some barriers they have not resulted in widespread adoption. The approach of the 
Program to invest in large-scale application combined with TA to foster new business models and with 
knowledge management instruments which allow for optimal e-mobility system design, learning from 
experience, training and policy regulations which favour EVs allows to combine all ingredients which allow 
for a transformational impact towards mass deployment of commercial e-mobility in Latin America. A 
modelling of EV deployment with and without Program was realized for different vehicle sectors and is 
included in details in reports 3 per country attached under Annex 3 of this FP and also available in excel 
version under File per country.  
Program Pipeline 
The initial indicative Program pipeline includes 4 investment projects (among which 50% in secondary 
cities) to be realized in 2023/2024 covering the total available FA. The full list of initially identified potential 
investment projects is for 9.6 billion USD i.e. around 20 times higher than the investment size of the 
proposed Program. This is a clear indication of the demand for such investments. Many investments are 
for fleet renovation over various years where a first batch could be financed with future funding being 
based on local banks. Telling is also the fact that many of the projects initially listed in 2021/2022 with the 
planned implementation 2023 could not be implemented due to lack of adequate finance. Other operators 
e.g. in Peru have decided to purchase instead fossil buses. The program will select the investment projects 
based on fulfilment of all Program investment criteria, on being projects with a high replication potential 
and demand driven i.e. first-come first-served. The Technical Assistance activities of the Program 
concerning the regional, national and local level will be implemented mainly by GIZ, and AFD will 
implement Technical Assistance for Projects feasibility studies and to structure projects from an 
operational, financial and legal perspective.

 PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION 
B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
Climate Change Problem and the Relevance of Sustainable Transport (incl. Electric Mobility) 
Transport contributes almost one-quarter of the current global energy-related GHG emissions and is 
growing faster than any other energy end-use sector.  Latin America already accounts for 10% of global 
GHG emissions as of today and, transportation related GHG emissions from many countries in Latin 
America represent a higher proportion than the global average (16% excluding LULUCF), namely 
Argentina (13%), Brazil (18%), Colombia (16%), Costa Rica (37%), Dominican Republic (18%), Mexico 
(23%), and Peru (25%)5. Current transport demand per capita in developing and emerging economies is 
far lower than in Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) countries but is 
expected to increase at a much faster rate in the next decades due to rising incomes and development of 
infrastructure6. GHG emissions from transport are anticipated to rise from today’s levels by nearly 20% by 
2030 and close to 50% by year 2050 unless major action is undertaken. Limiting the global temperature 
increase to below 2 degrees Celsius requires changing this transport emissions trajectory, which involves 
the development of an integrated electromobility ecosystem encompassing various transport modes, 
coupled with the low-carbon production of electricity (Paris Declaration on Electro-Mobility and Climate 
Change & Call to Action, 2015). The Paris Declaration on Electro-Mobility calls for the deployment of 
electric vehicles (EVs) compatible with a 20% share of all road transport vehicles in 2030. To achieve this 
goal modelling performed by the IEA suggests that EVs need to represent 35% of global sales by 20307. 
Under a Business as Usual scenario World Data Lab calculations come to 13GtCO2 of transport emissions 
by 2050 which is 67% more than 2022 and reflects a 1.9% annual growth rate of transport emissions8. 
Taking into account that all policies announced by today of governments worldwide are actually 
implemented GHG emissions of the transport sector would continue growing albeit at a slower pace (see 
figure below).  
Figure 1: Projected World Transport GHG Emissions under a Stated Policy Scenario 
 
Source: IEA

=== PAGE 17 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 11 
 
 
 
9 Avoid in theory also has the potential of zero GHG emissions– however, avoiding 100% of motorized transport is unrealistic 
To achieve targets of decarbonization massive trabnsformational changes are still required in the transport 
sector (see figure below). 
Figure 2: Projected World Transport GHG Emissions with Net Zero Targets by 20250 
 
Source: IEA 
 
Avoid-Shift-Improve (ASI) has been a long-standing framework for classifying mitigation interventions in 
the transport sector. Electrification is part of the Improve dimension and a paradigm change and not just 
hybrid vehicles, eco-drive, increased vehicle occupation rates etc. i.e. typical efficiency measures. 
Electrification is a game-changer and a must if carbon neutrality shall be achieved9. No shift measures and 
no improve measures will be capable to reach carbon neutrality in the transport sector. Electrification 
coupled with a fossil-free grid creates this opportunity. Not surprisingly do carbon neutrality plans involve a 
fully electrified vehicle fleet. Already today electric passenger cars can result in less GHG emissions per 
passenger-kilometer than diesel buses, even including a life-cycle approach.   
Electrification of public transport buses is an important component to modernize and to upgrade the public 
transport system of countries. This results in new and attractive buses which incentivize passengers to use 
public transportation. E-mobility is a game changer as new technology and higher quality enables cities to 
rethink and plan there entire public transport system.  
The impact caused on modernization of public transport systems through establishing new business 
models which increase the financial stability and economic efficiency of public transport operators and 
public transport systems e.g. through separation of bus ownership and bus operations and the introduction 
of new investors with deep pockets to the sector. Both movements are spearheaded by the electrification 
of public transportation due to the different financial structure of e-buses with a high upfront investment 
and a long recovery time of investments. This shift of business models and development of public-private 
partnership incited by e-mobility can be clearly observed in Chile and in Colombia, the 2 countries in Latin 
America which have already embarked on electrifying their public transport fleet and both of which have 
within this process changed their business models how to deliver public transport services. In both cases 
large capital-heavy investors have entered the market (electric utilities, private capital funds and bus 
manufacturers) which results in public transport operations being financially more solid (in both countries

=== PAGE 18 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 12 
 
 
 
10 Global EV Data Explorer – Data Tools - IEA 
11 PR China, USA, Norway, France, Germany, and the UK 
12 See e.g. Electric vehicle trends | Deloitte Insights or McKinsey Electric Vehicle Index: Electric Vehicle Trends | McKinsey; 
See for an analysis per country per Program targeted vehicle segment Annex 2 
13 Global EV Data Explorer – Data Tools - IEA 
bankruptcy of traditional bus operators has been a major problem) and with better and lower cost access 
to capital.  
The change of the business model is directly linked to e-mobility as (i) new investors are competitive with 
traditional bus operators with e-mobility due to the high amount of capital involved (ii) the interest of new 
investors is linked to electric mobility due to their business background; and (iii) e-mobility will not float with 
the traditional business model as operators do not have sufficient capital, to high finance costs and vested 
interests with the repair and spare parts market which is a major business for fossil buses but not for e-
buses. This disruptive change of the business model solidifies public transport operations, reduces in the 
long-term its costs (due to lower financial costs) and allows for upgrading of the service offer with the 
separation of ownership and operations and investments not only in new buses but also in improving the 
network, fare integration, and increased usage of electronic means. All of these measures can be 
established better by a transport authority if transport operators are just operators and not system 
managers. This results in e-mobility being a game -changer not only in terms of fostering low-carbon 
vehicles but also in reorganization and modernization of the public transportation system increasing its 
attractiveness and thereby resulting in an important mode-shift towards public transport compared to a 
business as usual situation.         
Program activities will also improve the grid resilience and the resilience of urban infrastructure. Used 
batteries from EVs have a second-life application where they can serve as storage units and thereby (i) 
increase the resilience and independence of the grid at critical spots such as hospitals or schools; (ii) 
increase the grid resilience and reduce costs of renewable grids by increasing storage capacity capable of 
taking up electricity during times of over-supply: this feature is especially important for grids moving 
towards high rates of (supply unpredictable) renewables; (iii) increase grid resilience through acting as grid 
shavers, especially in urban settings, thus reducing peak impacts.  
Electrification is happening within all vehicle segments. However, long-range vehicles such as inter-urban 
buses or long-haul trucks are only at the start of electrification. Hydrogen technology might be, at least 
initially, a more appropriate technology solution for these vehicle segments. Battery Electric Vehicles 
(BEVs) are the most competitive and also have the largest environmental impact in urban areas. Hybrid 
and plug-in hybrid vehicle technologies have been outgrown by the fast development of battery technology 
and only offer a limited GHG impact. For commercial vehicle applications at least (buses, light-commercial 
vehicles) manufacturers have switched towards full electric vehicles. Hybrids as well as plug-in hybrids are 
therefore not considered in this Program (see for further details Benchmark Report in Annex 22). 
The Program focuses on bus investment projects. Urban buses are the vehicle segment closest to being at 
cost-par with fossil units, have a high mileage and are used in urban areas where most people live. 
Replacing such units thus has a high GHG as well as air quality and health impact. Fostering commercial 
vehicles also avoids a negative social impact which could result from subsidizing private passenger cars. 
Accelerating Transformation towards Low-Carbon Mobility 
By 2022, there were about 18 million full electric cars on the worlds’ roads10. 6 countries have nearly 90% 
of all electric cars in operation worldwide11. EV deployment is still at an infant stage in Latin American 
countries and lags far behind Asia or Europe. None of the participating countries have reached 15,000 EVs 
yet. Under a BAU Scenario prices of EV’s are dropping reaching cost parity or full commercial viability in 5-
15 years depending on the vehicle category and the country12. End 2022 700,000 battery electric buses 
were operating worldwide of which 670,000 in PR China13. In Latin America in total (including trolleybuses)

=== PAGE 19 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 13 
 
 
 
14 E-BUS RADAR - E-BUS RADAR (ebusradar.org) 
some 5,100 electric buses were operating early 202414. large fleets of electric buses are operating in Chile 
(in Santiago specifically with 2,000 out of in total 2,043 electric buses) and in Colombia (in Bogota 
specifically with 1,486 of in total 1,590 units). The other program countries had early 2024 the following 
number of battery electric buses (excluding trolleybuses): Brazil 142 buses; Mexico 129 buses; Costa Rica 
4 buses; Peru 8 buses; Argentina: 22 buses; Dominican Republic: 2 buses 
The Programs core function is to accelerate EV deployment in involved countries through financial and 
technical assistance (energy transition in transport).  The Program ensures an at scale transition towards 
e-mobility and closes the gap between the current pilot stage and commercial large-scale sustained 
growth.  The focus is on commercial EVs and therein on buses. The mass deployment is feasible if 
successful business models are established, if the government is willing to cover the viability gap and/or if 
creative financial structuring is done as is shown e.g. by Chile or Bogota. This linked with the experience 
gained in mass applications which reduces the performance risk of vehicles as well as decreasing costs of 
e-bus systems allows to ensure a mass replication of the interventions. 
Figure 3: Intervention Window of E-Motion Program 
 
The Program has a strong focus on electrification, especially in its financial component. It implements the 
required interventions to kick-start mass deployment of electric vehicles significantly earlier than with a 
BAU scenario by reducing the risk profile of investments and by comprehensive technical assistance. The 
Program is focused in countries and vehicle segments where this gap can be closed i.e. where conditions 
for promoting on a larger scale e-mobility are met and where simultaneously commercially sustainable e-
mobility deployment is feasible within 7 years by kick-starting the process and by eliminating barriers 
towards commercial uptake. This is the reason why the Program does not have the same sequencing and 
activities as well as support levels in all countries. The Program is thus not about pilots and initial technical 
assistance, which already exist in the region, but about accelerating market-based commercial EV 
deployment.  
Per country modelling has been performed to determine the projected EV deployment with and without 
Program intervention. This is detailed in the reports 3 of each country included in Annex 3 as well as the 
corresponding country excel files also included under Annex 3. The following graph shows the modelled e-
bus deployment with and without program for Mexico.

Strengthening Public Transport 
Electrification is an enabler for improving not only vehicles but increasing services quality and working with 
partners on policies for incentives to shift to public transport and increasing ridership. Introduction of EVs is 
complemented by technical assistance on the mobility transition and by investments in improving PT 
systems. Projects with e-buses are located in cities which want to upgrade their urban mobility system and 
modernize public transport to also increase its mode share.   
Electric mobility represents a lever to shift the underlying paradigm that private vehicles are the preferred 
mode of transport. Introducing a large number of electric vehicles into the fleet requires transport operators 
and cities to fundamentally redesign and improve the overall public transportation system. Accelerating 
market-based commercial EV deployment across Latin America cannot be guaranteed by electric buses 
solely achieving cost-parity with rival technologies. Perceptions of risk, operational practices, business 
models, user choices and how local governments plan for integrated, sustainable urban transport systems, 
including non-motorised transport (NMT), also must change. 
Most existing EV bus electrification initiatives focus only on the exchange of vehicles and the provision of 
the required charging infrastructure and training of those operating the new systems. Often only a few 
vehicles running on one or two lines are replaced. Initiating a larger-scale replacement of vehicles offers 
the unique opportunity to also re-think the overall mobility system to create an attractive alternative to 
private vehicles, utilising the exchange of vehicles to trigger a systemic change towards a comprehensive 
mobility environment centred around public transport and non-motorised and low emission modes.

Figure 2 illustrates the push and pull elements that enable a shift towards more use of public transport and, 
if well-connected, to walking and cycling. The goal is to enhance efficiency of public transport systems and 
make the sustainable options more attractive than personal motorised transport to counter the past trend in 
declining public transport ridership. Shifting to electric buses can contribute to this by providing more 
attractive vehicles. The programme will provide financial assistance to de-risk financial products and 
accelerate the market take-up of electric vehicles. Technical assistance will support the development of 
conducive policy frameworks, as well as the identification of innovative business models and contractual 
arrangements that safeguard the financial and operational viability of public transport services.  
The upgrade of the vehicle fleet is an opportunity for reforming the transport systems in cities. Electric 
mobility offers an unique opportunity to re-structure the PT sector, introduce new PPP and business 
models based on separation of asset ownership and operations, favour the entry of new financially 
stronger players, and all this leading to the structuring of a modern, attractive and integrated offer to 
potential users of PT.  This is why the technical assistance component goes beyond electrification and 
complements the direct impact of fleet renewal with indirect results in terms of shift from high-carbon to 
low-carbon modes. Customers might also appreciate an EV more due to less noise or due to a positive 
image and therefore ridership of electric buses could increase resulting in a positive mode shift towards 
EVs and therefore additional ERs. A report found for example that e-buses could attract 1.9% additional 
ridership compared to diesel units (Currie, 2018).  More important is however the impact caused on 
reorganization and modernization of public transport systems through establishing new business models 
which increase the financial stability and economic efficiency of public transport operators and public 
transport systems. This results in e-mobility being a game -changer not only in terms of fostering low-
carbon vehicles but also in modernization of the public transportation system increasing its attractiveness. 
The E-Motion program will also have a significant impact on the development of sustainable and low 
carbon mobility in the region. The Program is implemented in countries and cities supported by the 
EUROCLIMA+ program, implemented by AFD and GIZ, and that establishes all the enabling conditions to 
do this shift towards sustainable transport in the region through the identification of mitigation measures for 
the effects of climate change in the transport sector. Indeed, EUROCLIMA+ Program finances 2 Latin 
American national governments National Urban Mobility Plan (NUMP), 6 Sustainable Urban Mobility Plans 
(SUMP) approved by local governments fostering multimodal participatory integrated mobility planning and 
3 pilot projects. The Budgetary credit lines financed by AFD and supporting a high level political dialogue 
on transport decarbonization in Costa Rica and Dominican Republic also ensure that the projects

Local Context 
Latin America has strong enabling conditions in terms of clean transport and e-mobility. The region has 
one of the cleanest electricity generation portfolios in the world (high hydroelectric production and growing 
deployment of wind and solar power plants). Therefore, the adoption of an e-mobility strategy would allow 
the sector to move towards a decarbonisation pathway. The table below shows the carbon intensity of 
electricity generated of Program countries. With these grid factors EVs can reduce GHG emissions on a 
well-to-wheel base by 70-90% compared to fossil units15. 
Table 1: Carbon Grid Factor of Program Countries

Latin America is also well known for having developed innovative bus-based transportation systems 
(especially in Colombia and Chile) and cities like Bogotá, Medellin and Curitiba have become international 
benchmarks for innovation in this area. Finally, Latin America has a positive local manufacturing context: 
three large vehicle producers operate in the region (Argentina, Brazil and Mexico) and four countries have 
large lithium reserves (Argentina, Bolivia, Peru and Chile) – essential material to build EV’s batteries. The 
main motivating forces to foster e-mobility in the countries included in the Program are: (i) Reduce GHG 
emissions; (ii) Improve air quality and reduce health problems associated with air pollution; (iii) Reduce 
dependency on imported fossil fuels and exposure to external price shocks; (iv) Re-structure and 
modernize the public transport system to increase its attractiveness and mode share; (v) Achieve positive 
economic impacts related to reduced health costs, reduced fuel import bill and job creation; (vi) Participate 
in the new growth industry of EV and battery manufacturing; 
Air pollution is a large problem in most Latin American cities with levels affecting seriously human health. 
According to the latest urban air quality database, 98% of cities in low- and middle-income countries with 
more than 100,000 inhabitants do not meet WHO air quality guidelines16. The WHO estimates that 
annually 4.2 million deaths result due to excessive exposure to fine particulate matter equivalent to 7.6% of 
all deaths17. The transport sector is thereby a major source of air pollution. The most vulnerable population 
is disproportionately affected by air pollution, as they tend to live and work closer to its sources18. Children 
and the elderly are particularly vulnerable. Recent studies show that women are more affected by poor air 
quality than men19.

=== PAGE 23 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 17 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
22 Registered vehicles 14 million; operating 8.5 million 
The COVID’19 pandemic has created an unprecedented challenge for many Latin American countries, 
from the health and economic perspectives, but it is also opening new opportunities for an accelerated 
sustainable transformation of its energy, transport, and urban landscapes while contributing to economic 
recovery. A recent report prepared by the ILO shows that fostering of electric mobility can have a 
significant positive job impact primarily due to the induced impact of savings of consumers on fossil fuels 
and vehicle maintenance resulting in increased spending on goods with a high income elasticity which tend 
to be labour intensive service-goods (ILO, 2020). 
AFD has conducted  a feasibility study of the E-Motion Program including a  diagnostic and market study 
of all countries involved (see Annex 2). Core information on each country given below is complemented 
with information on the countries NDC and transport sector in Section D5.

Argentina is one of the largest economies in Latin America. It is also, along with Brazil, Mexico and 
Colombia, one of the main producers of automobiles and auto parts in Latin America. Lithium reserves 
represent a unique opportunity for the transition towards electric mobility. The National Action Plan for 
Transport and Climate Change represents the set of initiatives that Argentina has planned to do to reduce 
GHG emissions in the transport sector. It is based on strategic intervention axes and mitigation and 
adaptation measures. Argentina launched the development of its National Electric Mobility Strategy in May 
2018 with the support of UN Environment. Argentina is currently formulating specific legislation on electric 
vehicles, establishing the conditions for the installation or operation of charging centres, and at the same 
time, exploring possible options for the local development of the electric mobility industry. Various pilots 
are running with electric buses in Argentina.

The Brazilian automotive industry is among the ten most important in the world. The government promotes

and biofuels to achieve mitigation targets. Brazil has already established various tax incentives for the

NDC strategy of the country and 21 bills are being discussed in parliament (tax reductions for fuel

Colombia has an automotive industry dedicated mainly to vehicle assembly, auto parts production and 
motorcycle assembly. The NDC contemplates for the transportation sector amongst others to achieve 
600,000 registered electric taxis, buses, light commercial vehicles including small trucks and official 
vehicles. The Government has developed the National Strategy for Electric Mobility, which aims to 
promote the electrification of the transportation sector. Bogota has purchased as of end 2020 nearly 1,500 
electric buses, whilst Cali and Medellin have pilot fleets of e-buses. However, all e-buses purchased to the 
moment are 9-12m units not operating on bus-only lanes. Initial pilots of e-taxis have also been deployed. 
Law 1964 requires that all buses purchased from 2035 onwards be electric. Colombia has established a 
good business model for e-buses. However, this model still requires a subsidy from the municipality (16% 
additional lifespan cost expressed in 16% higher cost per km paid to e-bus operators). Large municipalities 
such as Bogota are willing to do this and have the financial means. Smaller municipalities have different 
bus operation systems (no BRTs) and do not have these financial means. Also e-buses purchased to the 
moment are only for 8-12m feeder buses. For high-frequency trunk routes operated with 18-26m buses the 
e-bus technology would need to be different e.g. based on ultra-fast charging which requires a different 
business set-up due to large investments required in electric infrastructure for charging with different 
ownership structures (investments at bus stops and not bus depots). The program therefore focuses for 
Colombia on these areas and will not finance standard 8-12m feeder buses for cities with BRT systems.

Costa Rica reaffirmed its aspiration of becoming a Carbon Neutral economy and aims for a decarbonized

categories such as motorcycles targets and measures shall also be developed to migrate towards EVs. 
Costa Rica has published a national plan for electric transport which includes concrete steps towards 
electrification of vehicles and has approved 2018 the law on incentives and promotion of electric 
transportation which includes targets for EV penetration, the establishment of a public charging 
infrastructure as well as important tax incentives for private EVs.

as a 5% share of total sales of new electric or hybrid vehicles by 2030. The Management Program to

Peru has very low levels of Compressed Natural Gas (CNG) prices due to having abundant national gas 
reserves. EVs cannot compete with these prices due also to the fact that the electricity price is relatively 
high. Thus the Program will not realize activities in areas connected currently or in the near future to the 
gas pipeline system. However. various cities such as Arequipa or Cuzco are and will not be connected in 
the medium future to the pipeline system and depend on diesel buses thereby suffering from high pollution 
levels. In these cases EVs make commercial sense. Therefore the Program will focus on these areas. 
In none of the program countries diesel fuel is as of February 2024 being subsidized. Prices for 
diesel are, with exception of Colombia, over 1 USD per litre being virtually identical to the diesel price paid 
in the USA. Only Colombia had a lower price with 0.61 USD/l but also in this case this does not involve a 
fuel subsidy. Not all external costs are included in the fuel price, which is the case for all countries 
worldwide. In this sense all countries currently subsidize fossil fuels due to not including external costs fully 
(health, environmental costs etc). The current fossil fuel prices make the usage of electric buses in general 
profitable from a total cost of ownership perspective for all involved countries. However, payback times are 
long and risks potentially high. Risks are related to reliability of the technology – this is also linked to usage 
of buses basically from China with limited experience of operators in Latin America with Chinese buses 
and with partially negative experience with such suppliers including negative experience of post-sales 
service which for e-buses is critical to maintain acceptable bus availability ratios. Risks are also related to 
the lifespan of the battery and actual electricity usage as well as risks related to non-fulfilment of daily 
operational requirements due to range issues. Range issues are also related to the choice of the battery 
technology and the charging system – optimal choices require expert knowledge not widely available in 
Latin America. Many of these risk factors cannot be addressed well contractually or with guarantees. The 
risk appetite of public transport operators on the other hand is limited as they do not want to jeopardize 
their core business and revenue source namely transporting passengers. Therefore, technical assistance 
for high quality system design, advisory services in contract formulation, business models which allow for 
reducing risks and exposure and concessional finance are critical to initialise large fleets of e-buses. 
Summarized (i) fuels are not subsidized and (ii) the current fuel prices are adequate from a financial 
viewpoint and make investments in e-buses in general profitable but not sufficient to overcome the risks 
associated with the technology. 
Barriers addressed by the Program 
1.Limited commercial attractiveness of e-mobility investments: 
• 
A core barrier to EV deployment is the 2-3x higher CAPEX of EVs including associated 
infrastructure. The higher CAPEX results in higher capital requirements, higher finance and 
insurance costs and higher risk exposure due to higher debt levels. Higher CAPEX can be partially 
or fully recovered with lower Operational Expenditures (OPEX), primarily lower energy and 
maintenance costs and a higher lifespan of EVs. However, the profitability of EVs is only in the 
long-term and insecure. In the majority of involved countries the differential investment in an e-bus

=== PAGE 26 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 20 
 
 
is not recovered during its lifespan, due also to having to re-invest in batteries and chargers during 
the asset lifetime. Municipalities and transport operators in developing countries therefore prefer to 
invest scarce capital resources in a larger number of new fossil vehicles although electric units 
have potentially lower total costs of ownerships (TCO). This barrier is addressed by the Program 
with concessional loans reducing financing costs, with the promotion of business models which 
reduce capital requirement of bus operators and which reduce finance costs and with TA which 
allows to identify the most convenient e-bus system approach which allows to reduce upfront 
investment and operational risks.  
• 
The low financial profitability of EV investments reflected in low FIRR and very long payback times,  
constitutes an important barrier that guides investment decisions related to electromobility.  
• 
Another barrier related to commercial attractiveness of EVs is the higher risk of EVs compared to 
fossil vehicles as the technology performance level in the respective country is unknown, the long-
term profitability of EVs depends on long-term energy price development and cities and operators 
lack know-how in EV system design, maintenance and operations. This results in a high-risk profile 
of EVs. This barrier is primarily addressed by the Program through TA in the optimal design and 
management of EV systems and the information and capacity building efforts which allows to learn 
from experiences of already implemented projects. 
2. Business models and policies non-conducive to sustainable urban mobility and EV deployment:  
• 
This barrier is prevalent in various countries as governance structures and business models are not 
conducive to EV deployment. Innovative and localized business models that redefine the roles and 
the risk allocation among involved private and public actors are required. Local regulations, 
contract structures, payment systems and fare structures are frequently not favourable for e-buses 
(e.g. concession periods which are too short to recover the high investment in e-buses). Adjusting 
such structures can be a game-changer for e-mobility allowing to apply innovative business models 
adapted to the eco-system of e-mobility. This barrier is addressed by the Program through TA 
designing and promoting e-mobility appropriate business models and through outreach showcasing 
successful business models such as those established in Santiago de Chile or in Bogota. 
• 
Non-conducive policies: Whilst many countries have established general targets or policies these 
have not yet trickled down to concrete and tangible support policies such as a reform of the public 
transport sector which increases the demand for attractive public transport, improve the framework 
conditions and allow for private sector investment and participation to reach scale. This barrier is 
also tackled with TA, in a regional manner establishing know-how platforms and interchange of 
information. 
3. Limited know-how and experience with e-mobility systems and lack of experience in the design of 
effective interventions: 
This barrier refers to a lack of know-how on optimal EV ecosystem design (identification of type of EVs to 
use, fast/slow/opportunity charging etc.), maintenance & operations, as well as on used battery 
management policies including options of 2nd-life usage, recycling and disposal. This barrier is addressed 
through TA of the Program including studies on best practices in different countries, assistance in the 
design of appropriate regulations and policies and with outreach activities to ensure that experiences learnt 
in countries are also taken into consideration in other countries. GIZ has for example worked in these 
different areas in various countries already and can use that experience and know-how applying it to other 
countries and regions. 
The Program tackles these barriers and offers a comprehensive e-mobility package including financial and 
technical assistance thereby improving the policy framework and the ecosystem and removing barriers 
which allows to kick-start mass deployment of EVs. It further tackles the link between increasing service 
quality (PULL) and encouraging shift from cars to public transport (PUSH). The activities and instruments

=== PAGE 27 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 21 
 
 
 
23 https://www.changing-transport.org/about/  
used by the Program to remove these barriers are detailed in section B3. Currently many cities and 
countries have pilots deployed with mixed success. Pilot projects have the large drawback of resulting very 
expensive (bus prices are much higher if less than 50 units are purchased and the infrastructure cost gets 
very expensive) and with multiple problems related to maintenance, repair and problem solving. 
Manufacturers will not deploy appropriate after-sales services for few units. The step between pilots and 
full-blown implementation has not been made by transport operators as latter requires a high-quality 
technical design and a large investment which is considered as too risky by operators and by conventional 
finance institutions. As example Costa Rica has already made 2 pilots of 2 and then 3 buses – the first 2 
decades ago and the second with GIZ funds finished 3 years ago. The last pilot had large-scale technical 
assistance developing business models etc. but no financial instrument and no transport operator nor the 
government hopped on. The same is truie e.g. for pilots in Peru, Argentina or the Dominican Republic and 
even for countries like Mexico or Brazil where no large fleets of e-buses are operating. The project 
overcomes this first mover problem with concessional finance and targeted high quality technical 
assistance to ensure high quality buses, charging infrastructure, an optimal combination of charging 
system and buses. It also builds on an adequate business model distributing risks and purchase contracts 
which include multi-year service, maintenance and repair plus training and capacity building to be provided 
by the manufacturer.  
Related projects/interventions  
Multiple actors are engaged in promotion of e-mobility. AFD group and  GIZare important actors in e-
mobility in various Program countries. The E-Motion Program is implemented by AFD in 7 countries of the 
region ( Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Peru, Mexico and Dominican Republic).. The AFD FP 
and the GCF approved CAF FP (FP 195, approved at B34, no overlapping countries) are part of the same 
E-Motion program and approach aiming to promote e-mobility in the region.  
UN Environment is implementing the GCF Readiness Program “Advancing a regional approach to e-
mobility in Latin America” in thirteen countries in the region. The Program focuses on enabling strategies 
and policies to scale up electric mobility. The Global Environment Facility (GEF) is also implementing 
through UNEP a Global E-Mobility Program at the world level including Costa Rica. The individual TAs per 
country have been designed so that a complementarity exists with on-going and planned initiatives of other 
actors.  
The Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) is also an active e-mobility player in various countries e.g. 
financing a pilot fleet of e-buses in the Dominican Republic.   IDB also has an approved GCF e-
mobilityprogram (FP189) for the Latin American region, with complementarities with the E-Motion Program. 
The IDB program also includes Colombia and Costa Rica whilst all other countries covered by this 
Program are not included in the IDB program. The IDB program is broader in scope including also pilot 
hydrogen projects as well as electric vessels with potential electric vessel projects having been identified 
for Costa Rica as well as for Colombia. The three programs of CAF, IDA and AFD will collectively have the 
potential to define a transformational change in the transportation system of the Region.  
Projects/programmes in the region implemented by intergovernmental organizations and multilateral 
development banks focusing on the introduction of electric mobility include: 
1. GIZ – Changing Transport23: Supporting partners in defining low carbon transport strategies, 
implementing climate actions to reduce emissions and harnessing sustainable transport benefits 
through the Transfer project in Colombia, the PROMOB-E platform in Brazil, the Mi-Transporte 
Program in Costa Rica and other initiatives in all the countries concerned by the Program. GIZ is 
also the organization in charge of delivering the TA activities of the e-mobility Program.

=== PAGE 28 ===

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 22 
 
 
2. Euroclima+ program is a program implemented by AFD and GIZ with activities in the Program 
countries Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Mexico and Peru. Its objective is to develop 
national policies for urban mobility and integrated multi-modal planning at the city level to 
accelerate the transition of Latin American cities toward sustainable urban mobility low in carbon 
emissions. As a result, EUROCLIMA+ contributes to create the favourable conditions of the 
development of e-mobility in the framework of integrated and sustainable urban mobility plans. This 
is therefore an important link to achieve the structural change of the urban transportation system 
towards low carbon transport and links up e-mobility promotion with low carbon urban 
development. More specifically, with a total allocation of 10 million euros, Euroclima+ has the 
following goals: (i) Support the National low carbon Urban Mobility Policy in Ecuador and promotion 
of the National strategy of Electric Urban Mobility in Uruguay; (ii) Support the elaboration of  the 
following 7 Sustainable Urban Mobility Plan (SUMP): Regional Plan for Mobility and Logistics of 
Bajada Santista (Brazil), Mobility and Sustainable Urban Transport Plan for the Provincial 
Municipality of Arequipa ( Peru),  Sustainable Urban Mobility Plan for Córdoba (Argentina), 
improvement of the Integral Plan for Sustainable Urban Mobility  of the Guadalajara Metropolitan 
Area (Mexico); (iii) Support the implementation of 3 pilot projects: Mobility Observatory to improve 
the performance of public transport in Teresina (Brazil), Pilot Project on soft mobility in Cordoba 
( Argentina) and development of bike parkings in BRT stations  in Puebla (Mexico); (iv) A regional 
platform/community of practice is strengthening exchanges and capacities of cities and national 
Latin American governments for NUMPs and SUMPs. 
 
The E-Motion Program will leverage on ongoing programs of GIZ and Euroclima+ specifically trough the 
following aspects: 
• 
TRANSfer Colombia, being a "project preparation facility", designed a national program, including 
the structuring of a financial fund that could allow for major private investments, but also national 
guarantees and subsidies. Furthermore, an MRV system was developed, training curricula for 
business models, technical operation and planning were developed and a government mechanism 
was established, that E-Motion can build on.  
• 
MiTransporte: the project implemented an electric bus pilot, including data collection, which 
enabled that local bus operators gained interest and trust in the new technology. E-Motion can 
build on this to accelerate the deployment. The project furthermore reduced barriers for the uptake 
of e-buses (e.g. preferential tariff for charging) through a National steering committee on e-mobility, 
has trained different actors (drivers, maintenance personnel, firefighters, electricians, etc.), 
implemented a pilot project with commercial light duty EVs with the Institute of Technology Costa 
Rica and companies, evaluated operator models and financing mechanisms for e-buses that can 
be implemented under E-Motion and hast established an eco-system and a plant for battery 
recycling with a private company (develoPPP FORTECH) 
• 
PROMOBE: This project created the framework for electric mobility in Brazil by promoting a multi-
stakeholder dialogue in the National Platform for Electric Mobility (PNME), the introduction of 
standards and regulations for charging infrastructure and the support of pilot projects in urban 
public transport and urban logistics. 
• 
Euroclima+: The NUMP in Uruguay will set a long-term vision regarding sustainable mobility that 
will enable different sectors to engage. The NUMP will generate capacity at the city level to develop 
mobility plans, those plans expect to be financed in private public partnership. Consideration for 
future financing models are welcome. Uruguay is building a working group that will set the agenda 
for fleet renewal as for energy production. Considering their almost 100% renewable electric grid, 
they are exploring Hydrogen and Bio-fuels. Governance capacity is suitable to pilot energy 
transition as to work on innovative multi-actors networks to accelerate the transition and to 
outreach lessons learned to other countries.
