TUMI E-Bus Mission: This project is supporting 10 Deep Dive Cities in Brazil, Colombia and Mexico 
to set ambitious targets for e-bus deployment, and to elaborate and implement a work plan for 
tendering electric buses. 
3. UNEP - Global Programme to Support Countries with the Shift to Electric Mobility24: The aim of the 
Global Programme (covering Antigua & Barbuda, Chile, Costa Rica, Ecuador, Grenada, Jamaica, 
Peru and St. Lucia in LAC) is to support low and middle-income countries in overcoming these 
barriers through a combination of technical assistance and investment. Technical assistance on the 
Country Child Project level includes awareness raising campaigns, capacity building and the 
development of instructional structures to support the introduction of electric mobility. It furthermore 
includes laying the ground for large-scale market introduction of electric mobility through 1) The 
development of policies, including fiscal, regulatory and local measures; 2) The establishment of 
adequate business models and finance mechanisms; and 3) The development of plans and studies 
to ensure environmental sustainability. The investment component of the Country Child Projects 
will be used for electric mobility demonstration projects to allow for the creation of local experience 
to de-risk the technology and to attract investors to upscale electric mobility in the respective 
countries. 
4. UNEP GCF Readiness Programme on E-Mobility in Latin America: The UNEP Regional Office in 
Panama currently implements a GCF Readiness Programme on E-Mobility with the title “Advancing 
a regional approach to e-mobility in Latin America” including the Program countries Argentina and 
Costa Rica. The project aims at building capacity, development of e-mobility policies and business 
models, identification of strategies to finance the shift to electric mobility in the region and will be 
implemented combining a national with a regional approach, whereby close collaboration is sought 
for with the GEF Global E-Mobility Programme. 
UNEP - MOVE Electric Mobility in Latin America25: In 2016, UNEP and Mario Molina Centre Chile 
launched a regional platform to accelerate deployment of electric mobility in Latin America. Since then, 
MOVE has provided (1) capacity building to public technical officers from almost 20 countries in the region 
through online webinars and face-to-face meetings, (2) knowledge creation on the uptake of electric 
mobility in Latin America and the Caribbean, (3) technical assistance, through the elaboration of National 
Electric Mobility Strategies in the Program countries Colombia and Argentina and (4) resource mobilization 
to enable countries to transition to electric mobility – with a special focus on electric road mass transit. 
The ongoing and planned project / programmes are addressing the current inexistence of socially and 
economically viable alternatives to fossil fuel road transport. However, despite those ongoing initiatives, 
barriers to the broad adoption of EVs continue to exist in many countries. This is related to a significant 
extent to the projects focus only on technical assistance lacking financial assistance instruments and not 
allowing to implement actually new business models. For an accelerated shift of the transport sector 
towards e-mobility, overcoming the investment barriers will be key in the Program countries. Gap finance 
and financial incentives are still missing to a larger extent. The E-Motion Program will contribute 
significantly in closing the funding gap of higher up-front cost, while providing business model support and 
complementary TA elements for commercial fleets and general enabling activities, which will help 
accelerating the transformation towards low-carbon mobility in the respective countries.           
B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus 
diagram) 
 
IF low carbon urban mobility systems are implemented and an enabling environment for e-mobility can be 
established THEN the population will have access to affordable, attractive and convenient urban transport

=== PAGE 30 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 24 
 
 
 
26 New $1 billion dollar commitment from investors to deliver zero-emission buses in Latin America - C40 Cities 
and investors will be interested in pouring resources into EV projects which helps the involved countries to 
meet their GHG reduction targets BECAUSE transportation systems will have a more gender balanced 
and comprehensive mobility offer using low carbon EVs. 
The potential paradigm shift of the Program in terms of scale is a shift from the current reliance on fossil-
fuel powered transportation towards electrified low-emission transportation means. The Programs 
technical and financial assistance in the design and implementation of business models in line with the 
specific needs of electric mobility are decisive factors to foster this paradigm shift. The development and 
implementation of business models which tackle the barrier of high upfront investments pulls in other 
investors and allows for massification at scale in a commercial manner. In terms of replicability the 
Programs investments in electrified urban transport combined with the market trend of strongly decreasing 
prices of EVs allow for solutions to be replicated across other cities in Program countries. This is enabled 
through knowledge management and outreach instruments and the implementation of at-scale 
implementation projects which reduce decisively the risk levels for follow-up investors. The paradigm shift 
is sustainable due to the promotion of government policies favouring investments in EVs and resilient 
infrastructure combined with a market where EVs will achieve price-parity with fossil units within this 
decade. The interventions make cities more liveable resulting also in a behavioural change towards public 
transport. The Programs technical assistance in the design and promotion of policies which enable a 
commercial mass-uptake of e-mobility and the interaction with the private sector in the implementation of 
projects are critical for achieving this target. The main contribution of the GCF is that this paradigm shift 
towards low-emission electric mobility happens faster and at a much larger scale than under a BAU 
scenario. It is expected that commercial investors will crowd in to the e-bus area once the framework 
conditions (e.g. guarantees, concession contracts) and the performance risks of e-buses are manageable. 
This has been the case in Santiago de Chile as well as Bogota. A coalition of investors announced end 
2021 the intention to invest 1 billion in e-buses in Latin America26. The Programs TA creates conditions 
which are favourable to actually materialize such investments and the investment component of the 
Program reduces the risks to investors and operators. The Program is thus critical to unlock the private 
investment potential. 
The expected outcomes of the Program are (1) improved public and private sector capacity and a more 
favourable enabling policy framework for low carbon transport; (2) reduced GHG emissions from the 
transport sector due to a shift from private to public modes of transport and due to a shift from fossil to 
electric means of transportation resulting in 4.33 MtCO2e decreased GHG emissions and reduced energy 
usage estimated at 48,343 TJ of energy saved due to EVs being on average factor 3 times more energy 
efficient than fossil units and PT being more energy efficient per passenger-km than other modes of 
transport 
Expected co-benefit outcomes are (i)   Reduced economic & social cost of air pollution due to an 
improved air quality resulting in environmental co-benefits of 41 tons less PM2.5 and 4,842 tons lower NOx 
emissions; (ii) Increased use of public transport resulting in lower costs for mobility as economic benefit 
and gender co-benefits due to implementing measures which favour a safe usage of public transport by 
women; (iii) increased climate resilience of urban transport infrastructure.  
Outputs of the Program are (1.1) Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging 
electrification and shift to public transport. (2.1) E-mobility is combined with PT measures to foster mode 
shift to low carbon transport. This is made to strengthen attractiveness and convenience of PT including 
measures such as exclusive bus lanes, improved pedestrianization and public transport accessibility, 
transit-oriented development, fostering of electric micro-mobility and gender sensitive interventions 
realized in 2 countries. (2.2.) Electric bus fleets are deployed with innovative business models to enable 
their mass application in 4 countries.

=== PAGE 31 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 25 
 
 
Activities related to the Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and 
shift to public transport are (1.1.1) A sustainable mobility local ecosystem encouraging electrification and 
shift established with activities related to the local environment such as business model design and 
development, local policy advice, advice on concession contracts for bus services (e.g. concerning 
concession length as this is important for e-bus deployment), capacity building and training of operators, 
safety staff, maintenance staff etc.; (1.1.2) A sustainable mobility national ecosystem encouraging 
electrification and shift  established with activities such as design and divulgation of sectoral roadmaps, 
support of national enabling policies for EV deployment, advice on battery re-usage, recycling and disposal 
and capacity building at a national level  e.g. in public financial institutions for the generation of financing 
mechanisms and in insurance companies; design of business models to facilitate the scaling up of e-buses 
deployment in medium size cities; (1.1.3) A sustainable mobility regional ecosystem encouraging 
electrification and shift  established which is core for learning and experience exchange between Latin 
American countries with activities such as the realization of different knowledge materials including 
publications, webinars, benchmark and best-practice studies, preparation of capacity building guidelines 
and curricula, dialogue with EV suppliers and outreach events including trainings and workshops; (1.1.4.) 
Implementation of the Gender Action Plan; (1.1.5) Project sourcing, structuring, design and monitoring. 
Activity related to the integration of e-mobility with PT: (2.1.1) Implement urban mobility measures such as 
pedestrianization, bus lanes & route restructuring.  
Activities related to the deployment of EV fleets linked with innovative business models are (2.2.1) 
deployment of 850 electric buses; (2.2.2) Charging infrastructure for e-buses installed; (2.2.3.) Bus depot 
upgrades realized for e-buses; (2.2.4) Upgrade for grid connections for e-buses realized Finance covers 
the entire e-bus system of buses, charging infrastructure, grid connection and bus depot upgrades to 
accommodate e-buses. This needs to be designed individually per project as the optimal technology 
choice (e.g. overnight charging, fast/opportunity/at boarding station ultra-fast charging) is dependent on 
route and operational characteristics as well as local costs.  
The barriers identified and which shall be overcome with this Program are (i) limited commercial viability 
of e-bus investments related to high upfront costs, low or negative profitability, long payback times and 
possible loss of income if assets cannot perform at the equal level as fossil units e.g., due to range 
limitations; (ii) Business models and policies which are not conducive to a mass EV deployment including 
e.g. atomized public transport operators with limited capital access, contracts for public transport services 
which have a too short duration to be viable for EVs, policies favouring investments in roads and private 
means of transport or transport policies not taking into account gender aspects. This barrier is linked to 
limited experience with and knowledge on innovative business models, E-mobility system designs and on 
effective policy instruments (iii) limited know-how and experience in e-mobility systems and supportive 
policies; and (iv) lack of know-how on the effective design of e-mobility investments. The identified major 
risk is technological, operational and performance risks of EVs linked also with a limited availability of 
skilled maintenance staff. 
Core assumptions are (i) strong negative COVID-19 impacts on public transport usage are of temporary 
nature; it is expected that system will recover gradually from the strong demand reduction experienced 
during COVID-19; (ii) the critical financial situation of various involved countries limiting the uptake of new 
loans, can be overcome.

Adaptation co-benefit: This program’s main focus is on mitigation of climate change impacts and 
reduction of GHG emissions through fleet replacement. As such and due to a necessary investment 
prioritisation, adapatation to climate change impacts was not selected as a core infrastructure component 
of the program and considered as a co-benefit only. Measures related to adaptation will be addressed 
through the large technical assistance included in the program. This TA will look at aspects such as 
resilience of the local grid and charging infrastructure to flooding and other climatic events, promotion of 
non-motorised transport (NMT) along and around mass-transit corridors, improvement of drainage capacity 
through the selection of porous materials for NMT infrastructure, inclusion of bioswales, etc.    
B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages) 
Program Description & Intervention Strategy 
 
The progam aims to stimulate the operation of more electric buses and increase their ridership. It plans to 
achieve this by creating an enabling environment for electric mobility, embedded in a policy and system 
environment that enhances the efficiency and attractiveness of public transport and through this make 
electric buses financially viable. 
The Program has a comprehensive approach and includes implementation-oriented activities such as 
targeted policy advice for an integrated sustainable urban mobility and transport demand management, 
business model development and capacity building to ensure a favourable e-mobility environment 
combined with financial instruments to ensure deployment of large-scale fleets. The establishment and 
operation of large-scale EV fleets works as proof of concept of the commercial viability of EVs and fosters 
a mass replication without further concessional finance. The Program can reduce risks through the 
investment interventions combined with the implementation of business models appropriate for EVs (e.g., 
asset and operation separation; leasing and PPP schemes)  and measures that increase ridership, thus 
enhancing the financial viability of public transport sytems.. 
For each country a market assessment (see for details section below) has been performed analysing (i) 
the current financial viability of commercial EVs in terms of total cost of ownership, profitability, payback 
period, cash-flow and debt structure; (ii) required financial instruments to overcome current financial 
viability barriers; (iii) market readiness of EVs in the specific country based on energy and vehicle prices 
and future expected EV costs. Interventions are designed to accelerate market readiness and to foster 
widespread commercial deployment of EVs without further financial assistance. Therefore interventions in 
each country are targeted to vehicle segments where (i) existing conditions are favourable for EVs (in 
terms of policies, energy prices, interest of investors ); (ii) limited financial support can make a decisive

=== PAGE 34 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 28 
 
 
 
27 See Annex 2 
28 This is the reason why plug-in hybrid buses and not full electric buses were purchased for the BRT Peshawar. 
change and (iii) market conditions allow in the medium term (by end of the Program) for a sustained 
uptake of EVs based on commercial conditions. 
A power sector assessment has been made in detail per country. This includes power availability, 
electricity generation, and expected grid factors based on renewable energy (RE) trends27. All countries 
have trends of increasing electricity demand and supply and an increase in RE shares resulting in a 
decrease of the carbon grid factor. Commercial EVs will only result in marginal incremental electricity 
usage (<5% even with the high growth EV scenario by 2030 – see Annex 2 market studies). Especially for 
commercial vehicles the issue is much more on localized grids and e.g. supply of sufficient electricity and 
updated grid connections and substations for bus depots. At design stage various options how, when and 
where buses can be charged are assessed to find localized solutions per investment project. 
The Program is for commercial EVs and charging infrastructure related to the commercial EVs only. 
Private passenger cars and motorcycles are not financed. Commercial vehicles result in a much larger 
impact in terms of reduced GHG emissions and improved air quality and have significantly lower GHG 
abatement costs than private vehicles. With a small number of vehicles and a limited investment a large 
impact and a high visibility can be achieved. Concessional finance for private vehicles is considered to be 
socially inequitable and could result in a mode-shift from public to private transportation. Commercial EVs 
targeted are electric buses which have attracted the most attention from stakeholders in the involved 
countries. 
The Program only includes 100% BEVs. Hybrid electric vehicles (HEVs) and plug-in hybrid electric 
vehicles (PHEV) are not included. HEVs and PHEVs were an intermediate technology as long as BEVs 
were not yet technologically mature (see for details Annex 22). Hybrid buses or taxis can recover their 
incremental investment but only have a limited environmental impact of around 20% of emission 
reductions. A review of >50,000 plug-in hybrid buses deployed in PR China revealed that the 
overwhelming share of plug-in hybrid buses are never re-charged and thus only result in 20% energy and 
GHG savings (ADB, 2018). Exceptional cases where such buses, can make sense are in countries or 
cities with electricity supply problems and frequent prolonged black-outs28 or on very demanding routes. 
PHEV taxis are not a commercially attractive option. The battery size of PHEVs is small resulting in an 
electric driving range of only 30-50km. Most PHEVs models can only use AC slow chargers, which means 
charging of more than 1 hour to re-charge their already small batteries. A recent study of ICCT revealed 
that commercial PHEVs only run 20% of real-world driving on electricity (ICCT, 2020). In addition, PHEVs 
are more complex and expensive to maintain due to having both electric and internal combustion 
powertrain. The limited operational savings are insufficient to recover incremental investments of PHEVs 
compared to fossil or hybrid units. Hydrogen or fuel-cell vehicles are not included in the Program. They use 
on a life-cycle base 3-4x more electricity than BEVs due to highly energy intensive hydrogen production. 
They therefore result in most countries in an increase of GHG emissions compared to fossil units, except if 
hydrogen supply is exclusively based on electrolysis using to an overwhelming extent renewable energy. 
In such cases, hydrogen can be of interest especially for long-haul vehicles or large vessels. However, this 
is not the scope of this Program. 
The Program focus is on electrification of commercial vehicles, especially public transport buses. As 
outlined in Section B.1, electrification of buses is however not a stand-alone measure but an important 
component to modernize and to upgrade the public transport system of countries. New electric buses 
position the public transport system as modern, environmentally friendly and attractive. The investment in 
electric buses (PULL) is linked in 2 cities (potentially in Coilombia nd Brazil) with investments to improve 
the public transport system as well as non-motorized transport (NMT) in urban areas which result in 
increasing the attractiveness of low carbon forms of mobility. Together with transport demand 
management policies (PUSH) this results in a mode shift towards zero or ultra-low mobility systems. The

=== PAGE 35 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 29 
 
 
Program directly modernizes public transport systems through establishing new business models, which 
increase the financial stability and economic efficiency of public transport systems e.g. through separation 
of bus ownership and bus operations. Indirectly various urban mobility projects fostering NMT and public 
transport financed nationally and internationally are realized in the cities where investments in e-buses 
take place (the complementarity of e-bus investments to such efforts is a selection criteria for investment 
projects of the Program). As example medium sized Colombian cities which are targeted by this Program 
have formulated urban mobility plans (SUMPs) with the aim of modernizing and upgrading public transport 
whilst also fostering NMT and components of transit-oriented development. 
To achieve the program goal of mass deployment of EV and increase share of sustainable transport, an 
intervention logic has been designed that combines TA and FA efforts to promote the implementation of 
large-scale EV projects and contribute to the efficiency and attractiveness of public transport for both users 
and investors, generating an important mode-shift,. The combination of TA and FA will allow to orient the 
available resources in projects structured under innovative business models and provoke the necessary 
transformation of the sector by working on strengthening the current regulatory and normative framework, 
the creation of necessary promotional public policy instruments, the development technical capabilities 
and other aspects that will allow addressing the barriers that the development of electro-mobility faces in 
the region (See Section B.2 Theory of change. Activities). 
 
There are many ongoing activities to foster electrification in the transport sector in Latin America. 
However, most either concentrate on defining policy frameworks, without practical application and often 
with a focus on passenger vehicles. Projects aiming to replace conventional buses with electric 
alternatives only cover a small number of vehicles out of the total fleet in the specific urban context and do 
not address the wider system sustainability.   
 
The program is designed to bridge this gap and combine local and national framework creation with large-
scale implementation in model cities. Operating in medium-sized cities and converting a large share of the 
existing diesel-based fleet, supported by the technical assistance will enable the envisaged system 
change. This will not only allow to demonstrate the functionality of technologies, but also how business 
models and policy frameworks can work hand-in-hand with technology to deliver environmentally and 
commercially sustainable systems.  
 
a. Linking policy design with on-the-ground large-scale implementation also allows to ensure that   
b. Policy frameworks address actual challenges faced by cities in introducing electric bus systems;   
c. The investment in vehicles and infrastructure is not hampered by existing policy frameworks;  
d. Business models don’t need to be exclusively structured around existing policy, but can influence 
policy design to enable more favourable concepts.  
 
The Program will benefit from activities implemented by AFD and GIZ in the Program countries under the 
Euroclima+ initiative, whose objective is to develop national policies for urban mobility and integrated multi-
modal planning at the city level to accelerate the transition of Latin American cities toward sustainable urban 
mobility low in carbon emissions. As a result, EUROCLIMA+ will contribute to create favourable conditions 
of the development of e-mobility in the framework of integrated and sustainable urban mobility plans. 
Market Assessment 
A market viability assessment has been made for each for urban buses, taxis and Light Commercial 
Vehicles (LCVs) in each country based on local prices, costs and operating conditions (See Annex 2). This 
reflects the current market situation for EVs which is then expanded, based on projected future selling 
prices of EVs until 2030 (again per vehicle segment and country) to assess commercial viability of vehicles 
without financial support. Financial parameters used for the assessment are primarily TCOs, the FIRR

=== PAGE 36 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 30 
 
 
 
29 This rate is higher even than the total investment capital as FIs in general only finance a percentage of the vehicle cost 
and not the associated charging infrastructure, grid connection or bus depot upgrades which are considered non-marketable 
assets. 
compared to the weighted average capital cost (WACC) of the transport sector, the dynamic payback, 
cash-flow and debt levels and required equity investment levels (see Annex 2). Whilst results differ 
between countries and vehicle segments core take-aways are: 
• 
Non-discounted TCOs of e-buses as well as e-taxis are in most countries lower or at-par with fossil 
units. However, this depends on various critical assumptions including life-time of EVs versus fossil 
vehicles, maintenance costs, energy usage and energy costs and battery replacement costs. A 
critical part is thereby the bus availability rate which depends on the reliability of the vehicle itself, 
maintenance and availability of qualified maintenance staff and repairs respectively availability of 
spare parts. This has been a critical issue with e-buses, especially with pilot fleets, with availability 
rates being as low as 60% whilst diesel buses have, well maintained, 95%. This results in huge 
cost increases as well as difficulties to comply with the time-table and service offer. The reason for 
low bus availability rates is lack of experience with the technology, low quality bus suppliers, lack of 
adequate maintenance and trained staff and lack of spare parts. Issues related with poor after-
sales service of some providers have resulted in many transport operators being sceptical about 
changing bus providers and bus technology. These barreirs or problems can be overcome with 
adequate technical specifications, contracts including guarantees, maintenance and service 
contracts requiring on-site manufacturer staff and training. However, this is not feasible in financial 
terms for small or pilot fleets and most public transport operators are not willing to bet on a new 
technology with fleets of 100 or more buses at one go. They thus wait to see what other operators 
are doing which shows the relevance of having established in the country initial large fleets of e-
buses to prove their commercial viability. 
• 
The differential FIRR (profitability of the incremental investment in an EV versus a fossil vehicle due 
to operational expenditure savings) is in some cases below the benchmark WACC which means 
the investment is not profitable and in others is insufficient to warrant for the extra risks involved in 
changing the propulsion technology.. 
• 
The dynamic payback time of EVs is long  and in general exceeds the concession periods of 
transport operators and in some cases also exceeds the asset lifetime. 
• 
EVs require 2-3x more investment capital, 3-4x higher equity investment29 and higher loan volumes 
resulting in a balance sheet laden with debt and high consequential risks. Most investors prefer to 
purchase more fossil vehicles thus renewing their fleet more rapidly or increasing revenues instead 
of investing in costly EVs. 
• 
EV investments result in prolonged periods of cumulative negative cash-flow which can result in 
liquidity problems of investors. 
• 
The lack of large fleets results in scepticism concerning the reliability and performance of EVs and 
a lack of know-how on EV maintenance. Also, spare parts are scarce in the country without 
deployment of large fleets. No party wants to be the first-mover and take the risk, although many 
acknowledge that this might be an interesting investment. 
• 
Regulations are often not conducive for EV investment e.g. the length of concession contracts is 
too short for long-term investments or concession payment systems are not conducive for other 
ownership structures. 
Future market development has been modelled based on expected future price development of EVs per 
sector per country based on international trend forecasts of EV cost parity and battery prices (see Annexes 
2 and  23 for a description of the modelling approach).  
Business models in many countries are not conducive for EV deployment. New business models (see for 
more details section below) based on separation of asset ownership and operations or leasing models also

=== PAGE 37 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 31 
 
 
 
30 Phase II had no subsidies for e-buses and all contracts were awarded to fossil bus systems as e-bus offers were non-
competitive 
31 See Benchmark Report in Annex 22 
require risk instruments to be in place e.g. fare systems managed by third parties with automated debiting 
to the vehicle asset company to ensure payments, vehicle transfer agreements in case of operator default, 
vehicle insurance systems or bankable concession contracts. 
Financial Support Instruments 
The market assessment identified that without initial support commercial EVs are not yet viable in Program 
countries. Concessional finance is required to promote EVs. No investment grants are given. Concessional 
loan conditions of GCF for public sector projects and private sector projects are 0.75% interest rate with a 
maximum loan tenure of 20 years.  
The Program will give loans in Euro or USD. The maximum USD requested loans from the GCF under this 
Program will be 44.264 MUSD, and the maximum EUR requested loans from the GCF will be 56 MEUR.  
In absence of concessional loans investment in fleets will not take place. Initial financial support for large-
scale fleet deployment of electric buses, taxis as well as LCVs is critical and the international experience in 
this area is clear: No country worldwide has been able to deploy on a large-scale e-bus fleets without initial 
financial support (see Benchmark report in Annex 22).

Countries consistently subsidized 80-100% of the entire e-bus system incremental cost for the initial 
phase. Countries decreased their subsidy rates significantly within few years (e.g. China, India, UK, Chile). 
The importance of upfront investment subsidies – independent of TCOs – to kick-start deployment of e-
buses is thus clear. It also shows however that once a large fleet is established subsidies can be reduced 
gradually and even be eliminated due to more competitive prices of e-buses combined with the experience 
of mass operations of e-buses. Concrete examples are31:

=== PAGE 38 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 32 
 
 
 
32 Source: Grutter Consulting; contract with ADB for monitoring of e-bus fleets in 18 cities with 60,000 buses financed by 
leasing funds with support by ADB 
33 Grutter Consulting, personal communication with TransMilenio 
• 
In PR China, the average subsidy level per 12m urban e-bus decreased within 5 years from 
150,000 USD per bus to 30,000 USD per bus and is expected to  be  fully eliminated by 202232. 
• 
In India, under FAME I 2017-2019, e-buses where subsidized with 60% of incremental costs. This 
was reduced to 40% under FAME II 2020-2021 (40% of a lower absolute value meaning absolute 
subsides where reduced by more than 50% within 2 years). 
• 
In the UK, the 1st scheme for low carbon buses (until 2017) subsidized 90% of the incremental cost 
and the 2nd scheme, since 2018, 75% of the incremental cost of e-buses (including charging 
infrastructure). 
• 
Chile subsidized for the first lot of 200 electric buses 100% of incremental costs. The next lots 
received no more investment gap subsidies but some additional benefits for e-buses relative to 
fossil units in tenders which is translated into higher payments per distance driven or per service 
unit for e-buses (TransMilenio, Colombia analysed the tenders of Santiago and estimates the 
subsidy to amount to more than 16% of total costs per km33). 
• 
Uruguay subsidized for the first lot of 30 buses 100% of the incremental cost, through a National 
law that was intended to be up to 150 buses for the whole country.      
Business Models 
Two components stand out that can improve in a decisive manner the attractiveness and commercial 
viability of commercial e-bus projects: (i) bulk purchase –that will be promoted through the TA-, and (ii) 
separation of asset ownership and operations/management. Bulk purchase is important not only due to 
significant price discounts but also as this allows to optimize the e-mobility system in terms of charging 
infrastructure and bus types allowing e.g. to use fast-charged buses with common usage of chargers (up to 
12 buses per charger). Separation of asset ownership & management is critical due to high investment 
costs of buses which require strong financial players with access to cheap long-term finance. Private bus 
operators typically have a weak balance sheet and are financially not preferred clients of FIs. Business 
models with asset separation have been successfully implemented in Chile with e-buses involving initially 
electric utilities and also bus manufacturers as well as private investment companies. To thrive such 
business models also need the regulatory framework defining payment schemes, procedures in case of 
bankruptcies of parties, clear long-term concession contracts etc. Separation of ownership and 
management can be done with public led delivery systems (public asset companies) or with private led 
delivery vehicles (special purpose vehicles within a private public partnership PPP). This needs to be 
adjusted and structured per country based on national and even local requirements, priorities and 
conditions. It is also essential to work on standardized bidding documents to acquire EVs in order to 
strengthen the ability to replicate this business model in Latin American cities.  One of the main features of 
the Program will thus be to design, establish and implement such business models which can work as 
delivery vehicles for widespread commercial replication of e-buses without further need of subsidies. 
This shift of business models incited by e-mobility can be clearly observed in Chile and in Colombia, where 
in both cases large capital-heavy investors have entered the market (electric utilities, private capital funds 
and bus manufacturers) which results in public transport operations being financially more solid (in both 
countries bankruptcy of traditional bus operators has been a major problem), more efficient and with better 
and lower cost access to capital. New business models promoted by the Program separate asset 
ownership and asset management and allow for large investment firms with solid balance sheets and 
access to cheaper finance to enter the game. This has been the case for example in Chile or Colombia. 
This results in e-mobility being a game -changer not only in terms of fostering low-carbon vehicles but also 
in modernization of the public transportation system increasing its attractiveness and thereby resulting in 
an important mode-shift towards public transport compared to a business as usual situation. Unbundling

=== PAGE 39 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 33 
 
 
ownership and operation can also include the provision of power, chargers and bus depots. Leasing by 
fleet owners can be limited to the vehicle or include also maintenance and energy. Leasing can effectively 
reduce the barrier of upfront investment. E-buses can have lower TCOs and leasing models can bring this 
advantage to bus operators. Various models are used separated in the type of leasing, the type of leasing 
company or components leased.  
Figure 6:  Business Model e-Buses, Chile (2020)  
 
Source: AFD; dotted lines contracts; fixed lines: fund flows 
Examples of Chile and Colombia in e-Bus Promotion 
Chile and Colombia have been particularly successful in promoting e-buses. Why that? What was the game-
changer? 
Chile 
The case of Chile is especially interesting due to the introduction of new business models separating bus 
ownership and bus operations. The first batch of 200 electric buses was based on investments of electric utilities 
leasing e-buses to operators, in order to boost their core business (energy sales and the installation of charging 
infrastructure). Critical for the first batch of e-buses was not only the investment of electric utilities but also a 
government subsidy for the entire incremental investment cost of e-buses versus fossil units as well as changes in 
concession contracts reducing risks for investors in vehicle assets and ensuring payments from vehicle operators. 
In subsequent tenders ownership of e-buses is mixed with some offers based on JVs of bus operators with bus 
suppliers and others being JVs of bus operators with investment firms. Electric utilities are no longer engaged or 
have teamed up with capital investment firms as they do not consider vehicle investments to be a part of their core 
business (e.g. AMP Capital and NEoT Capital have entered as investors). The government no longer pays for 
incremental investment costs but still offers other advantages for e-buses such as longer concession periods and 
additional points in the bidding process representing effectively a subsidy to e-buses. Critical take-aways from the 
Chilean case are: (i) separation of ownership and operations can be an effective instrument to promote e-buses 
and can bring in new financially strong players into the industry; (ii) for kick-starting the process investment 
subsidies are critical; (iii) e-buses still require incentives to be competitive with fossil buses; (iv) required subsidies 
can be reduced significantly after an initial fleet of e-buses and after having established a working business model.

=== PAGE 40 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 34 
 
 
Colombia 
Large Colombian cities already run successfully since many years BRT systems with private operators and a 
public system manager paying companies based on distance driven cum service delivery targets. E-buses have 
been introduced for secondary routes based on local e-bus targets. The initial batch of 500 e-buses (2019) was 
based on a tender which only allowed for the provision of e-buses. A subsequent second tender which allowed for 
any technology resulted in all winning offers to be fossil buses, thereby clearly showing that e-buses are not yet 
commercially competitive (end 2019). The third tender (2020) included significant additional points for e-buses as 
well as longer concession periods. This made electric buses competitive with fossil units but also resulted in 16% 
higher per-km costs of buses which is being paid by the municipality. The experience of Colombia reveals two 
important points: (i) e-buses can be inserted into public transport operations and will be offered by private 
operators if concession contracts are sufficiently attractive and if e-buses receive benefits compared to fossil units; 
(ii) without financial incentives e-buses are commercially not yet competitive. However, some large cities such as 
Bogota are willing to pay for these incremental costs due to e-buses improving air quality and reducing GHG 
emissions. 
 
The importance of these business models for e-buses relies in their ability to reduce upfront CAPEX 
barriers which are larger than for fossil vehicles. Difficulties of leasing models for e-buses are the long-
payback period resulting in long lease terms required to make e-buses competitive. This again results in 
difficulties as concession contracts might be for a shorter duration than the payback time for e-buses and 
question concerning the actual lifespan of buses as well as the re-sale value of a leased e-bus arise (there 
is only a very thin market currently for used e-buses). Without proper estimate of the re-sale value of used 
e-buses and the actual lifespan a determination of the lease fee is complex. Long payback periods and a 
high CAPEX also result in high finance costs and the importance of low interest rates. Asset separation 
can allow for new, financially stronger companies, to enter the market and act as SPV leasing vehicles as 
they can have access to less costly financing sources.  Supporting these business models will add to the 
additionnality of GCF resources by de-risking the sector and providing incentives to new actors to invest in 
the sector. The Program will work with municipalities, the national government, transport operators and 
companies willing to invest in urban transport schemes in concession contracts, regulations and the 
structuring of business models conducive for e-bus deployment. The delivery scheme for component 3 of 
the Program (deployment of EV fleets) can thereby be public or private led (see figures below).     
 
Public-Sector Led Delivery 
 
In the case of Sovereign Investment Loans or Grants to directly finance Projects under this Funding 
Proposal, the AFD eligible borrower, acting as EE (EE 1st level) or a designated EE (EE 2nd level) (if 
different from the eligible borrower) will directly execute all project activities following AFD rules including, 
detailed specifications and prepare bidding documents, manage procurement processes under AFD rules, 
perform financial management and reporting of results.  
 
Accordingly, the most simple execution arrangement corresponds to the structure presented in the Figure 
below. In this structure, Subsidiary Agreements (Sovereign Investment Loans and Grants) are entered into 
between the AFD and an AFD eligible sovereign borrower. The AFD eligible sovereign borrower will act 
either as EE or transfer proceeds to a separate EE (in this case a project execution agreement is normally 
required). The EE will be in charge of the execution of the Project and will carry-out any procurement. AFD 
as AE will have an agreement with each EE, as required pursuant to the AMA.

The indicative structure presented in the figure above includes a private-public special vehicle (SPV) -instead

inventory. OEMs can also be involved directly in the SPV or team up with SPVs for tenders.The financing 
modality for PTOs will in general be through leasing which can include the vehicles plus charging 
infrastructure only or it can also include service and maintenance.

In case of a private sector led delivery the GCF loan is  a public sector loan (e.g. to the MOF) with 
sovereign guarantee or either a loan without sovereign guarantee directly to the private SPV or a public 
sector loan to the municipality without sovereign guarantee which on-lends it to the private SPV or directly 
to a PTO based e.g. on a leasing arrangement  
OEMs can also be involved directly in the SPV or team up with SPVs for tenders as has been the case e.g. 
in Colombia or Chile. Also in Brazil, the PROMOBE project has established a Working Group on Electric 
Buses within National Platform for Electric Mobility (PNME). Private and public national banks will be co-
financier of many projects of the program e.g. as PPPs and public development banks can also be 
financed by the program (e.g. national banks like FDN in Colombia or NAFIN in Mexico can finance e-bus 
projects). In some countries, e.g. Costa Rica or Colombia, national banks are already involved or have 
established lending facilities for EVs which are however not being used by clients due to lack of 
attractiveness (example credit line for purchase of electric passenger cars, taxis, buses or LCVs 
established by various banks in Costa Rica which is not being used with exception of passenger cars as 
the purchase of EVs is not profitable even with slightly more attractive loan conditions). Therefore, to 
ensure an active participation of national development banks in the Program and allow them to gain 
experience and de-risk the sector, the Program will propose technical assistance and financial assistance- 
which are key to ensure  that the credit line proposed by these banks are disbursed.    
Objectives, Impacts and Outcomes 
 
The Program supports a paradigm change to low-emission transportation systems. The Program shall 
overcome the barriers identified to kick-start the mass deployment of commercial EVs and increase 
ridership in public transport. The following impacts shall be achieved: 
• 
GHG reduction: EVs have significantly lower lifecycle GHG emissions than fossil units in all 
countries included in the Program. Public transport mode shift results in additional GHG reductions. 
AFD/ Proparco Agent 
GCF 
PTOs 
 Eligible borrower   
Loan 
Agreement 
Lease payments  
AMA/FAA 
$

availability

=== PAGE 44 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 38 
 
 
 
34 Multiple other factors difficult to separate from the Program activity influence the impact on mode shares 
• 
Improved air quality: EVs have no combustion emissions and are a crucial instrument to achieve 
clean air in cities. Commercial vehicles are a major source of PM2.5 and NOx emissions in cities. 
Replacing fossil diesel powered commercial vehicles with EVs improves air quality significantly. 
Public transport mode shift results in additional reductions of pollutants. 
• 
Reduced energy dependency: EVs use domestic resources and reduce reliance on imported fossil 
fuels. They thereby also increase the resilience of the country’s economy to external oil price 
shocks. 
• 
Increased energy efficiency: EVs are up to 4x more energy efficient than fossil vehicles. In addition, 
public transport is more energy efficient than private motorized modes. Electricity consumption 
even if pursuing an ambitious EV penetration level such as proposed by the EV30@30 target 
supported by the International Energy Agency (IEA) is marginal compared to national production 
levels – however, localized grid problems need to be addressed. Public transport mode shift results 
in additional energy efficiency impacts. 
 
The expected GCF fund-level impacts are reduced GHG emissions through increased access to low-
emission transportation resulting in direct emissions reductions of 4,334,051 tCO2e over the asset lifetime 
of investments co-financed by the Program excluding benefits from mode-shift towards public 
transportation which are induced by the Program and the introduction of innovative business models in 
public transportation but are considered an indirect and non-quantified Program impact34. The potential 
impact of de-risking EV investments has been calculated in the country reports. Investments are previewed 
in 4 countries only whilst TA is delivered in all countries. The GHG reduction calculations are only based 
on the 4 countries with investment projects.  Investment projects can be executed in any of the 
participating country and are selected based on high quality proposals put forward on a running base to 
the AE. The available funds give an indication of investments in minimum 4 countries with 1 project each – 
for calculation and programming purposes the most promising and advanced projects have been used. In 
practice however, this might have variations and no country is included from proposing investment 
projects. 
 
Technical Assistance 
 
To enable the envisaged system change, technical assistance is an essential element of the program, 
supporting and enabling the financial assistance. The exact activities and themes addressed in each city 
and country will depend on the existing frameworks and an initial gap analysis. Gaps will be based on the 
analysis already conducted during the preparation of the program, updated at the start of the program to 
reflect recent developments. 
All activities developing strategies, policies or concrete system design will be conducted using a multi-
stakeholder approach supported by technical inputs generated within the program. Relevant stakeholders 
will be determined in close collaboration with the responsible government agencies and will be involved 
through workshops, dialogues and other forms of interaction. 
Program Components/Outputs 
 
The Program is structured in 3 components: 
 
• 
Component 1:  Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and 
shift to public transport (TA) 
• 
Component 2: Investive measures for PT mode shift (FA) 
• 
Component 3: Deployment of EV fleets (FA)

B

35 Indicative 4 cities; the activity 1.1.1 is realized in all cities where investment projects take place 
 
Additionally Program Management is put as separate activity. 
Component 1:  Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and 
a shift to public transport 
The outcome of this component is (1)  Improved public & private sector capacity & regulatory environment 
for e-mobility. 
Component 1 is linked with Output (1.1.) Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging 
electrification and shift to public transport.  
The activities of component 1 are (1.1.1) Implement a local e-mobility conducive framework; (1.1.2) 
Implement a national e-mobility conducive framework; (1.1.3) Implement a regional e-mobility conducive 
framework; (1.1.4) Interventions realized with a gender perspective.  
This component is TA only and realizes the TA activities for components 2 and 3 which are FA only. 
This component provides technical assistance to create a policy and business framework conducive for 
sustainable mobility and massive deployment of EVs. Includes a set of activities aimed at overcoming the 
barriers faced by the development of sustainable mobility in general and multiplication of electromobility 
projects in particular.  
 
Experience has shown that the introduction of new technologies can only follow a sequential path of 
applying TA and then FA, when the barriers that the market faces are only partially explained by financing 
aspects. Only under these scenarios is it understood to adopt an intervention strategy that starts with TA 
to create the required enabling conditions (development of normative and regulatory framework, 
development of the required technical capacities, develop of required public policy instruments, etc.). This 
work paves the way to the entry of the necessary resources that will allow the implementation of projects. 
 
In the case of the market of EV for public transport, the main barriers that were identified are associated 
with financial aspects (high investment costs, problems of access to financing, risks associated with a high 
debt exposure, etc.). This is caused by atomized ownership structure, financially weak transport operators, 
often high levels of subsidies and lack of an integrated attractive offer for clients, resulting in declining use 
of public transport and a steadily increasing motorization rate. The Corona pandemic has further 
decreased the attractiveness of public transport. Therefore the required strategy must be comprehensive, 
contemplating simultaneously both the generation of the enabling conditions that this market requires and 
the effective implementation of projects that will drive the expected development. The market needs to see 
projects implemented and learn from them. 
 
Through the development of details feasibility studies in each of the proposed country, extensive 
information has been obtained to know and understand the electromobility market in the region. The starting
situation of the transport sector in general and the electro-mobility market in particular was described and 
made it possible to understand the main barriers that the electromobility faces in the region (Section B.2 
Theory of change. Risks and barriers). Based on this information, the TA component was structured,.  
 
Activity 1.1.1 Implement a local e-mobility conducive framework. TA supports the FA components by 
working with the cities35 in which FA activities will take place to create the conducive environment for 
sustainable mobility based on electric buses, as described above. This includes required local policies and 
frameworks for electric vehicle deployment and support to cities to develop the system in a gender 
sensitive manner and catering to a diverse set of users. It further supports cities to optimise public 
transport systems, including route planning, depot design and location, and city planning, including the

=== PAGE 46 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 40 
 
 
integration with active mobility. TA targets all stakeholder groups involved in public transport planning and 
operation in the selected cities, as well as utilities and grid operators. 
Sub-Activities will support cities to: 
• 
Develop or, as appropriate, revise sustainable urban mobility plans (SUMPs) in selected cities to 
provide the strategic context for policies and investments, incorporating opportunities through the 
electrification of bus systems; 
• 
Better understand available technology options;  
• 
Develop or adjust demand policies, including planning, system optimisation for public transport and 
integration with walking and cycling, and instruments discouraging private vehicles use; 
• 
Develop locally appropriate business models, financial structuring and concession contracting; 
• 
Identify national policies and frameworks that are required to enable cities to implement electricity-
based, sustainable public transport systems. This will directly feed into component 1.2 and support 
activities at the national level. 
• 
Enhance capacity: Technical training in the operation, management and maintenance of EV buses 
and charging infrastructure to public and private service providers of the selected cities. Enabling 
south-south learning on gender-responsive planning and design of mobility systems and the design 
and implementation of gender responsive transport projects and planning at the city level. 
Identifying and disseminating lessons learned and experiences from the implementation of projects. 
 
Activity 1.1.2 Establish a national e-mobility conducive framework. TA supports all seven countries in 
developing a national EV ecosystem conducive for electrification of buses in cities throughout the country 
in the context of an overall sustainable urban transport system that aims to enhance demand for public 
transport in a gender sensitive way. This means including gender from the perspective of users and as 
parts of the supply chain, for example the role of women as drivers, technicians and planners. TA targets 
transport and energy ministries and other institutions potentially involved in or responsible for urban 
planning and energy in the respective countries. 
 
Sub-Activities will support city and national stakeholders to: 
• 
Develop or, as appropriate, revise national urban mobility programmes (NUMPs), sectoral 
electrification strategies or related sections of national transport strategies; 
• 
Develop or adjust policies, regulations and guidelines that will support the deployment of electric 
buses within the entire country, such as minimum requirements for electric bus fleets, procurement 
guidelines or contractual regulations impacting concession contract design. This also includes 
policies that foster the integration of electric buses into the energy system, for example through 
standardisation of equipment or flexible electricity pricing; 
• 
Develop or adjust policies, regulations and business models that address the re-use, recycling and 
disposal of batteries at the end of their useful lifetime in vehicles, especially those that support grid 
stabilisation and resilience through second-life use as grid-integrated storage; 
• 
For countries without a financial assistance component: The experiences of countries with FA 
interventions shall be used also in other countries localizing them based on country and citiy 
circumstances. 
• 
Enhance capacity: Transfer of knowledge on available technology options for government officials 
and national stakeholders. Development of general training courses and curricula for vocational 
training for technicians and drivers with a gender inclusion perspective. Assess implications of e-
mobility at project investment level and at a national level on jobs and skills required to enable a 
just transition. This entails for investment projects to identify areas of job losses (e.g. vehicle 
maintenance) and identify new job opportunities and skill sets required including the development 
of a transition program. On a national level this includes working with relevant government entities, 
including Minisitries of Education on approaches to capture benefits of new jobs and opportunities

=== PAGE 47 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 41 
 
 
given by e-mobility through having flexible workforces and skills. This shall be integrated for 
example in e-mobility roadmaps or as stand-alone roadmaps for just transition in e-mobility. 
 
Roadmaps are an important strategic tool to support the implementation of long-term goals and underpin 
these with intermediate targets and action areas. They can be developed at different levels (regional, 
national & city) and can address the overall sector or individual transport segments. For individual 
countries, they ideally build on an integrated national transport strategy or National Urban Mobility Policy or 
Programme (NUMP), that outlines the overall vision for the sector and empower cities to implement 
sustainable mobility planning. But they can also be specific to urban transport, as for example in city-
specific Sustainable Urban Mobility Plans (SUMPs). Some countries and cities also prefer to develop 
specific electrification roadmaps. In any case, roadmaps provide guidance for policy design and can 
provide clarity and predictability for investors, IF they are underpinned with the actual implementation of 
policy and it can be demonstrated that envisaged goals are practically feasible. The program design aims 
to ensure that these crucial conditions are met and that actual policies are implemented that address 
actual challenges faced on the ground while demonstrating technical and commercial feasibility in the 
context of improved urban mobility systems. GIZ has developed detailed tools and processes to develop 
national and local urban mobility plans (available at https://changing-transport.org/toolkits/) which will 
support the program activities. A range of countries and cities are currently in the process of developing 
NUMPs and SUMPs, some with support from GIZ and AFD. To provide an example, the English summary 
of the SUMP of Santo Domingo in the Dominican Republic can be found here: 
https://www.mobiliseyourcity.net/sites/default/files/2021-
03/Santo%20Domingo%20SUMP%20Summary_final.pdf   
 
Activity 1.1.3 Establish a regional e-mobility conducive framework: Capacity-building, knowledge 
sharing and experience exchange are conducted at the regional level. Activities include exchange and 
training between countries as well as between cities. The program will utilise existing platforms and 
institutions where available to disseminate information and experiences generated within the program.  
 
Sub-Activities include: 
• 
Dialogue with EV suppliers on the strengthening of regional equipment and support industries. If at 
the moment local manufacturers mainly focus on assembly of e-vehicles, this sub-activity will also 
look at initiating a platform for increased transfer of know-how and footprint of local manufacturers 
in the region; 
• 
Dissemination of knowledge materials developed in the city and national components; 
• 
Sharing of training courses and curricula developed in other components; 
• 
General outreach activities. 
 
Through its capacity-building activities, the program will be in a unique position to also identify potential 
candidates for further financial and technical support, which may be provided by consortium partners or 
external funders.  
 
Activity 1.1.4 Implement a Gender Action Plan: The technical assistance component will work to ensure 
that: 
• 
The design and revision of roadmaps and policies includes a gender perspective and adequately 
considers the gender equality gaps and opportunities for enhancing the participation of women in 
related jobs; 
• 
The design of transport projects and investments considers gender equality; 
• 
Gender-disaggregated data on urban transport is collected to enhance the knowledge base; 
• 
Protocols against sexual harassment are promoted through communication campaigns.

Component 2: Investive measures for PT mode shift 
 
The outcome of this component is (2)  Reduced GHG emissions from the transport sector 
 
Component 2 is linked with Output (2.1.) E-mobility is combined with PT measures to foster mode shift to 
low carbon transport. Component 2 is only FA. 
The activities of Component 2 are  (2.1.1) Implement urban mobility measures to foster PT in 2 countries. 
Urban mobility measures are implemented by the city. They are measures to increase the attractivity of 
public and NMT and can include demand as well as supply measures. Declining PT shares are a fact in 
many countries. One of the reasons is the lack of attractiveness and convenience of PT. The program will 
work in the involved cities to increase PT attractiveness through measures such as bus only lanes thereby 
reducing travel time and making PT faster than private means of transport, route restructuring, tariff and 
mode integration, transport policies which favour in cities PT and de-incentivize private means of transport 
e.g., through parking policies. E-bus projects are embedded in urban transport projects which include 
measures such as bus route restructuring, exclusive bus lanes, integrated fare systems, or inter-modal 
integration to increase the attractiveness and usage rate of PT. Projects are also integrated with measures 
to increase the usage of NMT including segregated cycling lanes, increased pedestrianization and 
improved accessibility, and improved last-mile mobility services including (electric) micro-mobility (see 
component 1).  Common urban spaces and new urban mobility services facilitate the independence and 
mobility of all residents and are key to promote sustainable, competitive cities while increasing the quality 
of life of citizens taking into account the different mobility patterns in women and men.  
For the advancement of low-carbon sustainable mobility, this component will finance (i) the allocation and 
improvement of supporting urban spaces and infrastructure for public e-transport; (ii) the creation of urban 
mobility plans and policies; (iii) public transport improvements. The Program will prioritize solutions that 
integrate into the urban fabric, are compatible and conducive to modes of sustainable mobility 
guaranteeing universal access. This can include the infrastructure connecting public transport systems to 
alternative modes of urban mobility (electric and non-motorized) such as cycling lanes, pedestrian streets, 
etc., and/or providing the infrastructure conditions to facilitate these alternative modes of urban mobility as 
bicycle parking, park and ride, waiting areas, charging areas etc.  
This component will only be implemented by AFD and not by PROPARCO. 
Component 3: Deployment of EV fleets 
 
The outcome of this component is (2)  Reduced GHG emissions from the transport sector. 
Component 3 is linked to the Output (2.2) E EV fleets are deployed with innovative business models to 
enable their mass application. 
 
The activities of Component 3 are (2.2.1) Deployment of e-buses; (2.2.2) Deployment of charging 
infrastructure for e-buses; (2.2.3) Implement bus depot upgrades for e-buses; (2.2.4) Implement grid 
upgrades for connecting e-bus chargers. 
 
This Component is FA only.

=== PAGE 49 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 43 
 
 
 
36 Any country can propose investment projects – the listed projects are those which are currently the most advanced 
37 Includes PT investments 
38 Includes PT investments 
This component addresses the barrier of limited commercial attractiveness to invest in e-buses (barriers 
initial CAPEX barrier, profitability barrier and performance risk barrier). 
 
It is expected that this component will be applied in 4 countries with investment projects (Brazil. Colombia, 
Peru and Mexico) with an indicative number of 850 e-buses deployed of different sizes. The goal is to 
identify, design and implement localized innovative business models in public transport which modernize 
and increase the attractiveness of public transport and which deploy large-scale fleets of electric buses to 
showcase their technical and commercial viability to operators. Technology-wise this includes different e-
bus technologies (slow-, fast-, opportunity and ultra-fast charged buses), different bus sizes (8m to 26m 
buses) and different operational structures (mixed traffic buses as well as units operating on bus-only 
routes). The Program will not finance pilot projects but mass application linked with innovative business 
models. Buses are operated by municipal transport operators or private parties (see above for pre-
identified business models).  
E-bus systems always include 4 components: (i) the vehicle itself which accounts for around 70% of 
system costs; (ii) the charging infrastructure; (iii) grid upgrades to accommodate the charging infrastructure 
especially at bus depots but also, if on-route charging or opportunity charging is used, in the areas of 
chargers (this involves the installation e.g. of mini-substations) and (iv) bus depot upgrades which include 
e.g. roofs over chargers and increased space or layout changes to the bus depot as buses cannot be 
stacked equally if the are charged with plug-in. Charger network types and numbers depend on each city 
and the final feasibility study. This can be e.g. ultra fast charging at stations whilst passengers board, end 
of route opportunity charging, intermediate fast charging or overnight charging. This changes the number 
of chargers, the charging system, the output power of chargers and also results in different bus depot 
layout changes as well as different grid impact and grid upgrades. The feasibility studies realized per city 
will define the optimal e-bus system configuration per city. The system design is critical as bus features 
including battery types and battery size needs ot be optimized with the charging infrastructure and with 
financial and technical criteria concerning the grid to not create to much grid stress. There is no one-
solutions fits all for e-buses and an assessment needs to be made per city based on operational 
conditions, bus size and characteristic, grid conditions, climatic conditions etc. Batteries will need to be 
changed after 8-10 years (guarantees are today available for 8 years with 80% SOH of batteries and in 
some cases and some battery types even longer). Howevr, this investment will not be that critical in 8 
years from now due to falling battery prices and is in the range of a complete engine overhaul and bus 
refurbishment.

Program Management 
Activity 1.1.5. Project sourcing, structuring and monitoring. This  includes technical, financial and 
institutional advisory and the full feasibility of the project including economic, environmental, social, gender 
and legal.  This includes the definition of a project contractual scheme, ensuring a proper risk allocation 
amongst key stakeholders and assuring the bankability of the project and securing a proper allocation of 
GCF concessional resources. This support is key as innovative business models are often needed to bridge 
the cost gap between EVs and  internal combustion engines (ICEs). Project preparation can also support 
the verification at the suppliers’ of the conformity of e-buses with the bidding documents and test 
developments. Projects sourced and designed under this activity will be financed within components 2 and 
3 and result in the Outputs (2.1) and (2.2.) Project monitoring is also part of this activity. 
This activity will contribute to a proper structuring of the Projects, an adequate risk allocation and 
bankability and to the deployment of innovative business models. 
 
This activity will be implementented by AFD only. 
 
Project Selection Criteria 
All investments must be compliant with the GCF investment framework and follow the principles, terms and 
conditions agreed by AFD group and the co-financers, including project eligibility criteria, governance, 
implementation arrangements, environmental and social management systems, gender consideration, 
monitoring & evaluation and verification. The Program assists in identifying climate change related projects 
and maximizing their GHG mitigation benefits and contributes to the bankability of projects by introducing 
catalytic funding. The Program applies to all projects a standard GHG accounting methodology to 
determine GHG benefits going beyond the baseline. 
 
Project Eligibility Criteria 
Eligible projects must fulfil the following minimum criteria: 
1. 
All investment projects financed under Components 2 or 3 must reduce GHG emissions relative to 
the baseline and have a positive sustainable development impact. The approach to be used is 
determined in Annex 22a and is based primarily on UNFCCC methodologies registered under the 
Clean Development Mechanism (CDM); 
2. 
All investment projects financed under Components 2 must be linked to transport demand policies 
encouraging shift to public transport; 
3. 
All investment projects financed under Components 2 or 3 must demonstrate a transformational 
potential and increase ridership;

=== PAGE 51 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 45 
 
 
4. 
All investment projects financed under Components 2 or 3 must comply with economic internal rate 
of return of minimum 12%; 
5. 
All investment projects financed under Components 2 or 3 must be in compliance with relevant 
national and local laws and regulations and have country ownership; 
6. 
Investments in e-buses financed under Component 3 are realized in cities which are planning to or in 
the process of modernizing and upgrading their public transport system including initiatives to foster 
NMT and electric micro-mobility; 
7. 
All investment projects financed under Components 2 or 3 shall have a gender action plan taking 
into account guidelines developed in Annex 8; 
8. 
All investment projects financed under Component 2 will have a climate risk and vulnerability 
assessment to identify potential risks to transport infrastructure financed under the Program and to 
incorporate actions to mitigate climate risks if required; 
9. 
All investment projects financed under Components 2 or 3 shall strive to generate employment and 
local economic development, and therefore shall demonstrate a maximised share of local content; 
10. Only full-electric battery-electric vehicles are eligible for financing under Component 3. Hybrid 
trolleybuses (trolleybuses with battery) can also be financed if they proof to be a more cost-efficient 
option than usage of battery-electric buses considering also infrastructure replacement and 
maintenance costs; 
11. No projects with less than 30 EVs are financed under Component 3; 
12. The focus is on financing e-bus deployment in intermediate cities under Component 3. 50% of GCF 
FA resources shall be invested in intermediate cities. Cities which are not eligible under this criteria 
are: Colombia: Bogota, Barranquilla, Bucaramanga, Cali, Cartagena, Medellin, Pereira; Brasil: Belo 
Horizonte, Brasilia, Curitiba, Fortaleza, Manaus, Recife, Rio de Janeiro, Salvador, Sao Paulo; Peru: 
Lima;  Mexico: Mexico City; 
13. All investment projects financed under Components 2 or 3 have a co-finance level (non-GCF funds) 
of minimum 40%. Minimum co-financing rate of 60% over total Program (all components); 
14. In minimum 4 countries (indicative Brazil, Colombia, Mexico, and Peru) minimum 1 investment project 
with e-buses under Component 3 and in minimum 2 countries (indicative Brazil and Colombia) 
investments in upgrading PT and/or NMT infrastructure under Component 2 will be realized; 
15. No investment grants are given for e-bus investments under Component 3; 
16. The target is to finance one e-bus investment project which will be realized by the private sector or 
as a PPP (indicative in Mexico) under Component 3; and 
17. The interest rate to the private sector cannot be lower than the one for the public sector. 
 
Not more than 35% of GCF funds shall be invested in one specific country. Investment projects will be 
determined on a “first come, first served” basis, depending on their level of maturity at the time of 
availability of GCF proceeds.  
 
EVs are purchased instead of new conventional vehicles i.e. the old unit is at the end of its lifespan and not 
retired or phased out prior normal replacement. The Project Owner (PO) has the option at that moment to 
purchase a fossil or an electric bus. In both cases (baseline fossil as well as project case electric) a new 
unit enters and the old unit is also the same in both cases. There is thus no increased number of old units 
with the project case versus the baseline case. Therefore, the project in no case results, with or without 
scrapping, in additional emissions due to the purchase of NEW vehicles but only accounts for emission 
reductions due to purchase of a vehicle with a different drive technology. The intervention of the Program 
is that instead of a new fossil vehicle a new electric vehicle is purchased. What happens with the old 
vehicle is the same in the project case or in the baseline case. The difference is not the old vehicle but the 
BAU new vehicle (fossil) against the Program new vehicle (electric). All emission impacts and financial 
calculations are also a comparison of a new fossil against a new electric unit. The Program does not 
promote earlier replacement or scrapping of vehicles. Scrapping Programs in any case have limited merits

=== PAGE 52 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 46 
 
 
as for example in the case of buses old municipal buses could still be used to replace even older rural or 
private transport buses with low mileage.  The Program does not change the number of vehicles but the 
vehicle technology. Instead of new diesel buses (BAU) the Program will support new electric buses. What 
happens with the old buses being replaced by new diesel or electric units is the same in the baseline and 
project case. Therefore to be very clear: the Program does not add vehicles. In absence of the Program 
the exactly same amount of vehicles would be purchased, just fossil instead of electric units. See for 
further details chapter 6.2. of Annex 22. 
 
Finance Instruments 
 
The Program uses loans and grants. Grants are only used for technical assistance in component 1 chanelled 
mainly through GIZ, the implementing agency for TA (34 M€ grants of which 21 M€ from the GCF) and for 
project sourcing and development managed by AFD (6.5 M€ grant of which 1.1 M€ from the GCF). The 
investment components 2 and 3 are purely loan financed.  
 
B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams) 
GIZ has a strong technical expertise in the development of large TA programs and in the sustainable 
mobility sector. GIZ finances together with AFD the National Urban Mobility Policy (NUMPs) of Ecuador 
and Uruguay (Euroclima+ program). Moreover, GIZ is particularly active in the promotion of e-Mobility in 
the region and supports the design and implementation of e-Mobility policies through several grant-funded 
projects: support to introduce specific criteria for electric buses within the renewal fleet program 
REFROTA, integration of bus systems and electrification in Florianopolis (FELICITY program with EIB) and 
technical assistance to strengthen conditions to consolidate electrical mobility in Brazil (PROMOB-E 
program), development of a National E-Bus Promotion Program in Colombia, and support to the 
operationalization of e-buses in Costa Rica (MiTransporte program). Through the Cities Finance Facility 
(CFF) program, GIZ is also developing two bankable projects for new electric buses on 2 routes in Quito 
and providing assistance for new electric bus corridor and bicycle lanes in Mexico City.  
The current regional Program aims at completing these multiple initiatives, which are currently being 
implemented across Latin America, with a common ambitious financing and technical assistance proposal 
specifically targeted towards the commercial EV segments, which are now able to reach the scale and 
become commercially viable in the short to medium term. 
Implementation Arrangement 
 
E-Motion will be implemented jointly by the following institutions:  
 
• 
AFD, as accredited entity, executing entity and co-financier for the Financial assistance targeting 
public sector project owners (Components 2 and 3), for the technical assistance dedicated to 
Projects’ preparation, and for the gender action plan activities related to the investments projects. 
• 
PROPARCO, as executing entity and co-financier for the Financial Assistance targeting private 
sector project owners; 
• 
GIZ, as executing entity for technical assistance activities; 
 
AFD project teams will be responsible for the identification, appraisal, implementation and evaluation of 
eligible investments in the public sector. The projects’ appraisal process will follow AFD’s procedures. In 
particular, task team leaders will carry out due diligence and appraisals on each project in accordance with 
AFD Group standards and procedures.

39 In order to secure consistency with the FA preparation process, and to maximize synergies between TA and

PROPARCO will be in charge of E-Motion implementation within the private sector. As per AFD projects, 
PROPARCO project teams will be responsible for the identification, appraisal, implementation and 
evaluation of eligible investments in the private sector and appraisal process will follow PROPARCO’s 
procedures,  which are in line with AFD Group standards and involve AFD at decision taking stage. 
 
GIZ will be responsible for the implementation of activities 1.1 (majoritarily39), 1.2 and 1.3, and 1.4 (i) to (v)
