consultants. GIZ will report to the E-Motion Steering Committee and to the AFD Programme Manager. 
AFD and PROPARCO will have the responsibility to apply GCF proceeds in the framework of the financed 
sub-projects. 
 
Contractual arrangements 
 
The GCF and AFD will sign a Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) and all GCF funds, whether reimbursable 
or non-reimbursable funds, will be transferred from the GCF to AFD according to the provisions set in the 
FAA. The GCF loan proceeds will be provided to AFD in the form of a sub-participation, and the GCF grant 
proceeds will be provided in the form of a grant to AFD. 
 
AFD will sign a grant subsidiary agreement with GIZ and will channel GCF Technical Assistance funds to 
GIZ for the purpose the implementation of the majority of the activities 1.1, 1.2 and 1.3 and 1.4 (i) to (v) of 
component 1; 
 
AFD will sign a subsidiary agreement with PROPARCO, which will define the fiduciary and implementation 
arrangements between AFD and PROPARCO and PROPARCO will have a mandate to act on the behalf 
of AFD, with AFD being the lender. A grant agreement might be signed to delegate part of TA project 
preparation funds to PROPARCO; 
 
 
Financing agreement between AFD or PROPARCO and the beneficiaries 
 
Eligible projects within AFD’s scope (sovereign and non-sovereign public sector) and PROPARCO’s scope 
(private sector) will benefit from financial support extended by AFD and PROPARCO through the Financial 
Assistance on a project-by-project basis. For this purpose, PROPARCO and AFD will sign financing 
agreements (grant agreement and loan agreement) with the respective implementing bodies (developers, 
ministries, etc.). Financing agreements will describe the provisions and modalities for disbursing and using 
the funds for the purpose of implementing the projects, and will be developed and negotiated according to 
AFD Group templates and procedures. 
 
A detailed financial assessment will be realized by AFD and PROPARCO in the framework of the appraisal 
process of the project to ensure that the borrowers answer to the required criteria to sign a Credit Facility 
Agreement with AFD and PROPARCO (risk profile, revenues etc.). 
 
Service contracts between AFD and consultants  
 
AFD will hire consulting companies to implement the TA dedicated to the preparation of the projects and to 
support the PMU activities. In order to implement the Projects’ preparation Facility of E-Motion, a call for 
tenders will be launched to hire a pool of consulting firms that shall be mobilised to meet the specific needs

of each project.  This will allow flexibility in the TA activities and speed in the mobilisation of consultants for 
the projects.  
 
AFD will also hire external consultants as part of the Program Management Unit. r the Project

For the Technical Assistance Component, a co-financing by the German Government (of up to 1 MEUR) is

Safety.  The co-financing will enable GIZ to support the program management unit and link to other 
German Government funded projects. 
 
In addition, the following TA activities,  already committed and being implemented between 2020 and 
2026, are part of the Funded Activities of the Program: 
• 
Sustainable Urban Mobility support program for Dominican Republic through the Caribbean 
Investment Facility (CIF) comprising technical assistance activities implemented by AFD and 
whose purpose is notably to support the implementation of the National Urban Mobility Policy, the 
ongoing reform of urban transport sector, and the implementation of the Santo Domingo 
sustainable mobility plan including e-mobility development. The amount dedicated to activities 
facilitating the development of e-mobility is estimated at 4.6 MEUR.

Euroclima+ technical assistance activities, financed by the EU and implemented by AFD and GIZ, 
whose purpose is to develop national policies for urban mobility and integrated multi-modal 
planning at the city level, in order to accelerate the transition of Latin American cities towards 
sustainable urban mobility with low carbon emissions. Euroclima+ will contribute to create the 
favourable conditions of the development of e-mobility in the framework of integrated and 
sustainable urban mobility plans. The amount of the Euroclima+ activities dedicated to the E-
Motion countries is 8 MEUR. 
 
For the Program Management Unit, AFD and GIZ will mobilize 4.3M EUR (65% of the total cost of the PMU)  
with an in-kind contribution of staff of each institution. 35% of the PMU costs will be financed by the GCF. 
Governance and Program Management 
 
An E-Motion Steering Committee (SC) will be constituted for the whole E-Motion Program. It will meet on 
a quarterly basis and be responsible for making the strategic decisions required for the execution of the 
programme. Its members are representatives of the executing entities (AFD,  GIZ, and PROPARCO). The 
SC shall notably review and approve programme annual budget and work plans, discuss implementation 
issues and identify solutions, and ensure coordination and communication between executing entities. 
 
The following additional committees will be constituted to ensure a proper implementation of the Program:  
• 
A Technical Assistance coordination committee to ensure the consistence of the TA activities 
implemented at local, national and regional level.  Its members are representatives of the executing 
entities (AFD, GIZ,and PROPARCO). 
• 
For each sub-program (driven by AFD): 
o An investment committee to pre-validate Concessional GCF loans proposed to be mobilized 
on the Financed Projects.  Its members are representatives will be PROPARCO, GIZ and 
AFD.   
o A Project Preparation Committee to coordinate and validate the mobilization of the TA 
dedicated to Projects’ preparation.  Its members are representatives will be PROPARCO, 
GIZ and AFD.

=== PAGE 55 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 49 
 
 
Considering the large geographical scope targeted by E-Motion and the diversity of financial and technical 
assistance tools managed by the Program, it is essential to have a strong Program management Unit 
(PMU) to ensure the coordination between the partners and a proper implementation of the Program.  
 
The PMU staff will be composed of professionals from AFD group and GIZ that will be in charge of 
handling the respective components of the Program. The PMU will be chaired by an AFD Task Team 
Leader, part time dedicated to the Program and based in Latin America. A Task Team Leader from GIZ will 
also be dedicated part time to the Program Management Unit.   
In addition, the PMU will be composed of external consultants: an e-mobility expert, an Environmental, 
Social and Gender expert, a communication Expert and a Project Management support officer. AFD will be 
in charge of contracting the PMU Consultant team.  
The PMU will be in charge of: 
• 
The general coordination and management of the Program, which will include the animation of the 
governance bodies of the Program, the technical coordination and animation, and the 
administrative and financial follow-up for structuring;  
• 
The management of the Technical Assistance for the Projects’ preparation; 
• 
The preparation of the reporting and monitoring reports due to the GCF and the supervision of the 
evaluation process; 
• 
The implementation and the follow up the E-Motion communication plan, supporting the 
organization and implementing of events, seminars and conference related to the program and 
preparing all the communication support (web content, press release etc.); 
• 
The implementation and maintenance of a knowledge and records management system.  
B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
 
Rationale for and Additionality of GCF Funding 
The Program introduces a concessional financing mechanism that addresses barriers to the uptake of 
innovative public transport business models with EVs and supports a transformative shift to low carbon 
transportation. Currently fossil vehicles dominate the market  and the fleet renewal. In all involved 
countries no large fleets of commercial EVs operate. The paradigm shift towards electric mobility not only 
entails new vehicles but a new fuelling/charging infrastructure, changes in vehicle maintenance and 
operations and frequently also requires changes in the business model. Actors in the commercial vehicle 
sector fear such disruptive changes as it can affect their core business. Governments on the other hand 
are keen on promoting e-mobility as this can reduce emissions and dependence on (often imported) fossil 
fuels whilst also creating long-term sustainable jobs. They therefore often announce political targets or 
objectives to promote e-mobility coupled with stand-alone EV pilots. The missing link is however to kick-
start EV massification with a first batch of at-scale implementation projects. Such initiatives are dependent 
on concessional funding to make them financially and from a risk perspective feasible. These projects are 
the foundation for meaningful, practical, realistic and action-oriented roadmaps and for developing policies 
which are cost-effective and reduce barriers towards e-mobility and encourage shift to public transport. 
Stand-alone pilots or technical assistance has proven to be insufficient to kick-start mass deployment of 
EVs. GCF concessional financing is critical to ensure this comprehensive e-mobility approach – in absence 
of concessional GCF finance, such projects will not materialize in the short or medium term.    
The major financial barriers for the potential beneficiaries which prevent alternative financing options are (i) 
the low financial profitability of EV investments reflected in a low FIRR and long payback times (ii) the high 
risk profile of EV investments due to performance risks, asset risks (e.g. unclear resale value of assets), 
and replacement investments (batteries) and (iii) high capital and investor’s capital demand resulting in 
high debt and risk exposure levels of the investing entity. Commercial funding is thus not willing to enter

=== PAGE 56 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 50 
 
 
this market. Alternative funding vehicles with a higher risk appetite are potentially interested but are critical 
of the lack of profitability and/or the high risk profile. A good example for this case is Chile where traditional 
banks were reluctant to finance commercial EVs and private electric utilities entered the market with a 
leasing scheme for buses thus creating a new business approach (see Benchmark report Annex 22). 
However, also these entities were only willing to kick-start the process when the government recognized 
and paid for the incremental investment cost of e-buses plus guaranteed payment of the leasing fees i.e. 
without concessional finance and risk mitigation instruments the deal would not have taken place.  
The main barriers why investors, cities or PTOs do not want to purchase e-buses is the lack of profitability 
and the higher upfront CAPEX resulting in higher financial costs. Guarantees do not resolve this problem 
and will increase the total cost. Potential investors as well as FIs are only interested in guarantees if these 
incur no cost. They however also mention that the guarantee will change the decision to purchase or not 
EVs as guarantees do not tackle the problem of profitability and high upfront costs. Only very high 
guarantee levels (100%) would be result in slightly lower interest rates. For some concession contracts in 
certain countries guarantees could be useful. However, no specific case could be identified where the 
guarantee instrument would be relevant. Highly concessional finance is critical for deployment of initial 
commercial EV fleets. Concessional finance is not required in replication projects which happen at a later 
stage as (i) EV market prices decrease: EV prices are decreasing in all vehicle segments due to 
decreasing battery prices and increasing competition and supply of EVs. TCO aloneis not a sufficient 
argument to move for EVs if the upfront incremental investment is still significantly higher and the risks are 
still perceived to be higher. However, decreasing vehicle costs are a very important ingredient to make 
EVs commercially viable without need for further financial assistance. (ii) Reduced risks and costs for 2nd 
and 3rd projects due to showcasing of successful implementation projects and business models.  
Risks which are reduced by large fleet applications are (i) vehicle reliability and vehicle performance risks; 
(ii) improved information and therefore lower risk on assessing the re-sale value of used EVs important for 
introducing business models based on leasing; (iii) less risk concerning maintenance & repair capabilities 
and availability of spare parts including secondary spare parts markets; (iv) reduced risk on the workability 
of new business models in practice. The Program assists in this process by designing and implementing 
business models and by enabling with concessional finance the uptake of large fleets which are critical to 
reduce these risks. (v) Policies which are more conducive for e-mobility and restrict fossil vehicle usage -if 
it is an objective set by the country. This allows EVs to be more competitive. Lower costs, reduced risks, 
and improved framework conditions all create favourable conditions for investors to pour capital into this 
market as profit rates improve and risks get down. This will allow for widespread replication based on 
commercial terms in 5-10 years if the country has gained experience with initial fleet deployment. The 
concessional finance is required as kick-off. The market development and experience of countries shows 
that at the end of the Program such support is no longer required.  
Concessional finance is not only needed to compensate the higher upfront costs of electric vehicles but 
also to reduce and share the risk of investing in this type of technology. Indeed, there are several barriers 
beyond ‘just higher upfront costs: associated with this, there are higher capital requirement, higher equity 
requirement and higher interest burden and guarantees (also due to uncertain resale value) with usually 
limited debt framework. Moreover, the construction of charging infrastructure and depot infrastructure 
needs to be considered as a  sunk cost. Finally, there is a higher risks in comparison: TCO calculations 
and savings potentials are based on assumptions on electricity price development and usage, 
development of diesel prices, maintenance costs and operational readiness of e-vehicles, lifetime of 
batteries, operational risks due to lower flexibility (e.g. e-buses cannot be used on all routes). 
Conventional financial institutions are also not willing to enter the market due to the novelty of the 
technology. A pilot project will not dispel their concerns as these are associated basically to the lifespan of 
the technology including batteries, associated replacement costs, the quality and reliability of vehicle 
providers (basically Chinese bus manufacturers which are not all well known in the market), resale value of 
vehicles, and potential savings in terms of maintenance and repair costs. Financial institutions perceive

=== PAGE 57 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 51 
 
 
these risks and are thus reluctant to finance EVs. The establishment of new technologies and new 
business models also entails significant additional transaction costs. Concessional GCF finance including 
grants can provide for technical assistance to design and structure e-mobility investments with a reduced 
performance risk whilst the financial instruments of the Program reduce risk exposure and increase 
profitability of the investment. Increasing the ridership of public transport strengthens operators and 
transport providers and reduces the risk with e-mobility investments.  
The multi-country approach is critical for a transformative and accelerated shift towards e-mobility, which is 
again required to achieve the target of limiting the global temperature increase to below 2 degrees Celsius. 
Multi-country approaches are critical for learning and experience exchange between Latin American 
countries with activities such as the realization of different knowledge materials including publications, 
webinars, benchmark and best-practice studies, preparation of capacity building guidelines and outreach 
events including trainings and workshops. The interchange of experience is included in component 1.3 and 
is realized within the program. Through participation of various countries with a similar context the learning 
effect can be much larger helping to get to replication. Therefore, outreach and learning from experiences 
of other countries is critical. If the program is realized only in one country, the replication will be basically 
on a national level. National programs are always important but experiences from other countries can be 
brought in much easier if the Program covers multiple countries. A multi-country programmatic approach is 
also necessary given the scale of climate change challenges facing countries and the critical need to 
deliver transformative change at scale as quickly as positive as vehicle replacement rates are low thus 
resulting in long lock-in times of investments. The GCF involvement is critical to support low-carbon 
transport investments across multiple countries and vehicle sectors. With a multi-country programmatic 
approach, resources can be used efficiently to highest priority investment projects – this cannot be 
achieved with a piecemeal, project-by-project approach. Individual projects are in most cases too small to 
warrant a Funding Proposal to the GCF with all transaction costs involved. AFD would thus refrain from 
financing electric mobility in most cases and not integrate it into its transport investments. The Program will 
also capitalize on synergies and lessons learned across countries and vehicle sectors and can effectively 
ensure transfer of knowledge and capacity building between stakeholders.  
Concessionality of GCF Funding 
GCF concessional finance including grants are used for TA and for FA. GCF assistance enables linking 
finance with technical and policy assistance. Investments alone are insufficient to achieve the 
transformation in climate action needed by the transport sector. Strategic sectoral planning and policy 
reform are critical aspects to enable the transformation towards electric mobility. The GCF, with the goal of 
supporting paradigm shifts in climate action, is critical for sufficient funding of the policy and technical 
assistance component of the Program. The Program involves private transport operators, national FIs, and 
potentially SPVs of PPPs for vehicle ownership. The private involvement is stimulated by involving in 
projects national FIs and by having PPPs as the major delivery agent for projects. 
PTOs are in most involved countries private companies. FIs are included in the national finance part. Also 
SPVs for private led delivery in a separation of ownership and operation of assets are private companies. 
In some countries however public sector led SPVs might be more appropriate depending on country 
circumstances.  
FA concessional finance are critical to reduce upfront CAPEX, to make investments commercially viable 
and to compensate for risks from initiating climate investments in a new technology and in new business 
models. GCF’s ability to offer concessional terms in investments enables prospective clients to invest in e-
mobility which would otherwise not be able to do with AFD finance alone. The benchmark report (Annex 
22) clearly shows that all countries with large fleets of commercial EVs (>100 units) have used, at least 
initially, financial support instruments in the form of grants to kick-start mass EV deployment. Incremental 
investment costs of e-buses was grant financed by countries by 80-100%. The magnitude of subsidies can 
be reduced with adequate policies and regulations (e.g. concession periods) and business models per

=== PAGE 58 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 52 
 
 
vehicle sector.  The experience of countries is also that once a large fleet is established subsidies can be 
reduced gradually and even be eliminated within 5-10 years due to more competitive prices of EVs 
combined with the experience of mass operations. This reduces for investors in EVs primarily performance 
risks related to energy usage and costs, battery replacement and maintenance costs plus reduced income 
risks related basically to the availability of a fast charging infrastructure.  GCF concessional finance is 
critical for sustainable market uptake linking initial pilot projects with commercial EV viability by inserting 
the missing part of initial large scale fleets combined with business models, policy advice and technical 
designs which reduce risks related to EV investments and allow to close the gap between political 
aspirations and commercial EV uptake. 
Long tenures and low interest rates are critical for the affordability of such loans as the fiscal situation of 
countries and of operators has tightened in LAC drastically with COVID-19. Longer tenure and low interest 
rates reduce the debt service levels significantly allowing countries to take up new loans without creating 
significant additional fiscal imbalance. Also, the public transport sector has been hit hard by COVID 19 with 
additional subsidies required by government to maintain service levels. Without such subsidies the public 
transport system will decline in importance due to bankruptcy of multiple private operators. Concessional 
loans with long tenure and low interest rate increase the fiscal space of governments to provide subsidies 
to the public transport sector which increases the sustainability of the services and thus of the program. 
They are thus absolutely critical for Program success. 
 
Grants are used for technical assistance such as new policies or business models, battery regulations, 
workshops, trainings etc. They result in no revenues. Therefore reimbursable systems will not work. TA is 
considered as absolutely essential for the paradigm shift. Only with changing policies, business models 
and with trained and informed people will this happen. Without this effort one-time projects and 
interventions will occur which will not result in a transformational change. 
The Program will benefit from technical assistance that will be made available to accompany each 
project and will ultimately serve to enhance the viability of the projects.  The TA package has been 
designed for each country individually based on the FA interventions, a demand analysis and an 
assessment of other actors in this area in the same country and other regional readiness programs to 
avoid duplication of effort. TA will establish asustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification 
and shift on a local, national and regional level with activities structured from the local to the regional level. 
The Program will provide direct technical assistance to projects that will potentially receive financial 
assistance. TA includes pre- and full feasibility assessment, legal, institutional, financial and technical 
advisory as well as advisory on supportive transport demand policies as well as possible business models 
and financial structuring. This includes charging networks and the interface between vehicle, charging, and 
the grid.  TA includes also: 
• 
Capacity building, training and monitoring: TA is used to ensure delivery of adequate training from 
equipment suppliers or third parties for EV and charger operations and maintenance, safety 
trainings and capacity building in optimal management of EV fleets. This activity also includes 
performance monitoring of EVs in technical (energy usage, emissions) and financial terms 
(operational expenditures, vehicle availability rates), which is core to reduce the perceived 
performance risk of EVs and to create a solid base of actual costs and benefits of EVs in the 
country.  
• 
Support of enabling public policies for shft to public transport and EV deployment: Technical 
support will be provided to the relevant national and local authorities in the countries and cities 
where the Program will support investments in order to set-up, improve and/or enforce enabling 
public policies and legal, regulatory, fiscal and/or normative frameworks for e-mobility. The 
Program intends to establish in all countries where not yet available roadmaps for electrification of 
public transport and roadmaps for taxis and urban freight. Sectoral and specific roadmaps are a 
tool to steer climate investments and initiatives beyond the Program’s scope  and will help to inform

=== PAGE 59 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 53 
 
 
 
40 Implementation period refers to the period of development of the TA and contractualization and disbursement 
of the credit lines. 
and/or further detail the countries long term mobility strategies and their NDCs. Policy advice is 
also performed for battery handling, re-usage, re-cycling and disposal. TA in policy also includes 
advice on concession contract issues, electricity pricing (e.g. demand charge for public charging 
networks).  
• 
Development and advisory services on optimal business models and financial structuring which 
relate to the peculiarities of EVs. This is targeted for public transport to identify business models 
e.g. for atomized urban transport providers, models which allow for increased influx of private 
capital and which lower financing costs and risks for operators, leasing models, inclusion of electric 
utilities etc.  
• 
Inclusion of gender aspects in all projects to be financed as well as on a national and regional level. 
• 
Knowledge management: The Program will develop knowledge products and share experiences 
and cases through channels such as webinars, electronic reports, case-studies, and workshops to 
disseminate experiences and build a base for replication thereby accelerating EV uptake on a 
regional scale. The Program will not create own platforms or channels for information dissemination 
but use existing channels such as the one created by UNEP/MOVE. An interchange of experience 
shall also take place where experiences can be shared from countries more advanced in specific 
issues, e.g. Colombia on bus systems. 
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
 
Exit Strategy 
 
Repayments under concessional loans provided with GCF funds under the Program will be flown through 
the AFD-GCF Special Account in accordance with repayment schedules set forth in AFD or Proparco’s 
loan agreement. It is envisaged that tenors will not exceed 15 years. All loans will be monitored by AFD 
group. GCF resources will be reflowed back to the GCF in accordance with the terms of the FAA. 
Program Sustainability 
At the end of the Program (based on an implementation period40 of 7 years this would be 2032) there are 
operational large fleets of e-buses in all Program countries. This forms the base for a large scale 
commercial replication without grants. The projections of dropping E-bus prices point to cost based parity 
(TCO) around 2025 and strongly decreasing upfront price tags. The market analysis performed per country 
shows that a significant share of investors would then be willing to purchase electric buses without 
subsidies given that experience is available in the country with an initial large-scale fleet which conforms 
core assumptions concerning energy usage, maintenance cost, vehicle reliability, and system technology 
choice (types of chargers, charging method and site, battery type and battery size on buses).  
Whilst the investment cost is still expected to be higher for an e-bus system the significantly lower OPEX 
(basically energy plus maintenance cost) make the TCOs significantly lower than for fossil bus systems (in 
many countries the TCOs are already today lower but not sufficient i.e. the payback period is still long and 
sometimes longer than the concession period of PT operators thus not making the investment financially 
attractive under a risk viewpoint).

=== PAGE 60 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 54 
 
 
 
41 Source: Grutter Consulting; contract with ADB for monitoring of e-bus fleets in 18 cities with 60,000 buses financed by 
leasing funds with support by ADB 
 
 
 
Figure 11: Projected Electric and Diesel Investment Cost per Urban Bus (real constant USD of 2020) 
 
Source:  Grutter Consulting 
 
It is expected that by 2030 e-buses will be close to sticker price parity with diesel units, whilst total system 
costs per e-bus are still expected to be around 40-50% higher than of a fossil unit. However, the 
significantly lower OPEX of e-buses together with the technology and market maturity will make purchases 
of e-bus the standard, without necessity of further concessional finance. TCO costs will be much lower 
than of diesel buses and payback periods of incremental e-bus investments under this investment price 
trend will be less than 5 years. This commercial uptake will only be realized if the investment risks of e-
buses are reduced. This is possible through the establishment of new business models and through 
operations of initial large fleets which allow for performance monitoring thereby reducing uncertainty for 
follow-investors. Countries which originally subsidized 80-100% of the entire e-bus system incremental 
cost for the initial phase and have purchased or subsidized large fleets have been able to decrease their 
subsidy rates significantly within few years. The importance of significant upfront investment subsidies – 
independent of TCOs – to kick-start deployment of e-buses is thus clear. It also shows however that once 
a large fleet is established subsidies can be reduced gradually and even be eliminated with 5-10 years due 
to more competitive prices of e-buses combined with the experience of mass operations of e-buses. 
Concrete examples are:  
• 
In PR China the average subsidy level per 12m urban e-bus decreased within 5 years from 
150,000 USD to 30,000 USD per bus and it is expected that it will be eliminated fully by 202241. 
• 
In India under FAME I 2017-2019 e-buses where subsidized with 60% of incremental costs. This 
was reduced to 40% under FAME II 2020-2021 (40% of a lower absolute value meaning absolute 
subsides where reduced by more than 50% within 2 years). 
• 
In the UK the 1st scheme for low carbon buses (until 2017) subsidized 90% of the incremental cost 
and the 2nd scheme since 2018 75% of the incremental cost of e-buses (including charging 
infrastructure). 
• 
Chile subsidized for the first lot of 200 electric buses 100% of incremental costs. The next lots 
received no more investment gap subsidies but some additional benefits for e-buses relative to 
fossil units in tenders which is translated into higher payments per distance driven or per service

=== PAGE 61 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 55 
 
 
 
42 Grutter Consulting, personal communication with TransMilenio 
43 New $1 billion dollar commitment from investors to deliver zero-emission buses in Latin America - C40 Cities 
unit for e-buses (TransMilenio, Colombia analysed the tenders of Santiago and estimates the 
subsidy to amount to more than 16% of total costs per km42).    
 
The establishment of a conducive EV framework, local business models which match the EV situation (e.g. 
separation of ownership and usage of assets) as well as reduced risks of operating EVs due to the 
experience gained with large fleets all make it feasible that at the end of the Program implementation 
period EVs are taken up massively and at a far larger scale than in absence of the Program.  
Based on the market studies performed the Program expects that a commercial uptake of EVs without 
further support from the GCF is feasible in the targeted countries at the Program end. This is feasible due 
to the intervention of the Program accelerating in a decisive manner the commercial uptake of EVs and 
fast-forwarding their financial feasibility. The sustainability is given by (i) improved framework conditions 
where the Program intervenes with difrent policy actions including roadmaps; (ii) attractive business 
models which are implemented with the projects and which provide investors with confidence of the 
attractiveness of investing in this market; (iii) reduced risk for investors due to clarity on performance of 
buses with the projects implemented and monitored by the program plus training of mechanics and support 
staff thus reducing the risk of stranded assests for investors and (iv) the market development which results 
in reduced costs of e-buses and system components resulting in increased profitability. Thus the 
combination of factors which create better framework conditions, clarity on investment models combined  
with reduced investment risks by knowing better the product and not being a first mover and increased 
profitability due to reduced CAPEX all work hand-in-hand to create a sustainable future for e-mobility 
investments.  
It is expected that commercial investors will crowd in to the e-bus area once the framework conditions (e.g. 
guarantees, concession contracts) and the performance risks of e-buses are manageable. This has been 
the case in Santiago de Chile as well as Bogota. A coalition of investors announced end 2021 the intention 
to invest 1 billion in e-buses in Latin America43. The Programs TA creates conditions which are favourable 
to actually materialize such investments and the investment component of the Program reduces the risks 
to investors and operators. The Program is thus critical to unlock the private investment potential. For 
commercial inestors to crowd in the TA is crucial to establish a conducive framework including e.g. 
concession contracts adn business models as well as clear government policies. Just as important is that 
large-scale fleet deployment with such business models take place as proof of concept and to reduce 
performance risks for oeprtors and financiers. Both TA as well as concessional finance for FA are however 
not required anymore in the future once the framework conditions are established and once the risks are 
reduced as lower costs of EVs make these profitable already in the short to medium term under the 
mentioned conditions of an adequate e-mobility environment. 
The technical assistance supports long-term sustainability through a number of crucial factors: 
Roadmaps at the national or city level are developed and are underpinned with actual policies that 
strengthen the EV ecosystem and make public transport more attractive. These frameworks will support 
additional investment in other cities by providing the right incentives, reducing risk through enhanced 
attractiveness of buses, and reduce uncertainty for investors. Coupled with the demonstration effect of 
large-scale deployment of new technology and business models in the selected four cities, this will 
enhance the commercial feasibility of future investments.  
Countries within the program have very different starting points. Those with already existing roadmaps will 
serve as role models implementing all components at both national and local level. Others will focus on 
developing their roadmaps and maybe implement some policies mostly at the local level, where 
components 2 and 3 are implemented. For countries without actual implementation components, the policy

=== PAGE 62 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 56 
 
 
 
 
frameworks will be essential to trigger enhanced bus electrification and increased use of public transport. 
Experiences from on-the-ground implementation in other countries can help shape better policies and 
enable a more ambitious strategy. 
Enhanced understanding of business models in the specific context of Latin America, including for second-
life usage of batteries, combined with a proof-of-concept on the ground will reduce the risk of investors and 
motivate additional action. 
Capacity-building activities ensure that there is a broader understanding of technology options and their 
suitability to different Latin American contexts at the national and city level in all countries covered by the 
program. The development and dissemination of experiences with business models as well as financing 
strategies will further add to the commercial attractiveness of EV buses after the program period. The 
development of general training and curricula for vocational training of drivers and technicians will support 
the availability of skilled personnel for enhanced deployment of electric buses in the region. 
In addition and under the TA component of the Program, specific studies will be undertaken on e-vehicles 
batteries’ replacement, disposal and recycling issues, to ensure environmental sustainability of this new 
business model (refer to Annex on Technical Assistance, where this is further detailed in activity 1.2).

C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words, approximately 
0.5 page) 
C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building 
activities? 
Yes ☒    No ☐ 
C.3.2. Does GCF funding finance technology 
development/transfer? 
Yes ☒    No ☐ 
 
Capacity Building 
 
Capacity Building is delivered under Component 1. This includes specifically: 
• 
Project specific training during design and planning stage specifically the transfer of skills for 
optimal technology choice, design of charging infrastructure, preparation of tender 
documents, design of relevant business models, financing plans, risk sharing mechanisms.  
• 
Project specific training and capacity building per implemented project on technology related 
issues such as maintenance of EVs and charging infrastructure, ecological driving skills with 
EVs, optimal vehicle dispatch, planning and design as well as technology choice of EVs,

=== PAGE 66 ===
C 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 60 
 
 
 
 
safety and risk avoidance with EVs, battery management and disposal policies and 
regulations  etc.  
• 
Capacity building and policy advice specifically for the establishment of sub-sectoral 
roadmaps for electric mobility in the fields of public transportation, taxis and LCVs.  
• 
Capacity building with knowledge products and their dissemination based on the best global 
practices and the experiences in the different projects. This is realized through different 
products and channels to reach a wider audience in Latin America.  
• 
Capacity building on gender mainstreaming 
• 
Training and CB of service providers including maintenance and repair staff for buses as 
well as charging infrastructure to minimize the risk of low vehicle availability rates. 
 
The purchase contracts with equipment providers (buses, chargers) will include specific training 
and CB courses which the equipment providers must realize for operators personel including 
drivers, maintenance staff, service staff and transport operators. The procurement contracts will 
also include on-site maintenance services to be provided by the equipment provider for buses and 
chargers during minimum 2 years in collaboration with the staff of the equipment owner cum 
operator.  
 
Technology Transfer 
 
Technology Transfer be facilitated through identification and design of appropriate e-mobility 
technologies and projects and their subsequent funding. Technology providers such as bus and 
charging equipment providers are tied with purchasers into 2–3-year service provision contracts 
where the supplier must provide for on-ground expert staff to train local maintenance and repair 
staff. At the same time this extended service contract includes guarantees on vehicle availability 
and spare parts availability which ensure smooth operations and high vehicle availability rates. The 
supplier must therefore ensure that the vehicle technology is understood by the local operators and 
staff in charge and ensures this through training programs which are also specified in the bidding 
documents for suppliers.

D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 61 
 
 
 
 
 EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out 
in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.  
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
 
Area of emission reductions are (i) impact of replacing fossil with electric vehicles and (ii) increased 
ridership in public transport.  
Impact of E-Buses 
GHG emission calculations have been made per for potential investment projects for e-buses per country. 
Whilst baseline emissions per vehicle category are comparable for different countries (variations are due to 
vehicle mileage, fuel type used and to a lesser extent due to different specific fuel consumption values) the 
carbon grid factor per country influences the emissions of the EVs and therefore the emission reductions.  
The GHG impact is determined based on a well-to-wheel approach. This includes for EVs the carbon 
emissions from the grid including production, as well as transmission and distribution losses. With the 
average grid factors of the countries included in the Program electric vehicles targeted by the report 
reduce GHG emissions by 70-90%. The methodology report on GHG reductions of the Program also 
includes a life-cycle or cradle-to-grave approach to determine emission reductions to assess the 
magnitude of leakage (see Annex 23). Inclusion of upstream emissions (vehicle manufacturing including 
batteries) only changes the picture marginally. For the grid factor the IFI database last version 3.1 of 
January 2022 is taken for electricity consumption. The assumed lifetime of measures is 15 years in 
accordance with e-bus lifespan with 1x battery replacement. 
Impact of Public Transport Measures 
This includes GHG reductions from Program investments in public transport measures to increase its 
patronage. The methodology used for calculation GHG reduction is based on the UNFCCC methodology 
ACM0016 and the UNFCCC methodological tool “Baseline emissions for modal shift measures in urban 
passenger transport”. GHG reductions are due to additional passengers which in absence of using the bus 
would have used other modes of transport with on average higher emission factors. Estimations of the 
incremental patronage of PT systems is thereby based on monitored results of already implemented 
projects. Measures which increase the PT usage include infrastructure investments in bus lanes and bus 
stations, smart ticketing, improved connectivity and intramodality, modern electric bus fleets and 
preference for PT systems in urban settings. The emission factor of private means of transport is per 
passenger-kilometre factor 2x higher than for PT. The assumed lifetime of measures is 25 years in 
accordance with infrastructure lifespan. 
The total projected GHG direct impact of the Program is estimated at 4,334,051 tCO2e over the lifetime of 
the project based on the initial pipeline of projects46. This includes only the impact of the 4 countries. Actual 
Program emission reductions will depend on which projects are actually implemented and will be 
monitored by the Program.  
The important aspect of the Program is however that it creates the conditions for an accelerated uptake of 
EVs. This has been modelled based on an investor-risk model which per country and vehicle sector 
calculates the annual FIRR of investing in EVs (the FIRR increases over time due to decreasing relative EV 
prices), the investment risk level and the target WACC47.  
 
 
46 Expected impact per country: Brazil 440,000 tCO2; Colombia 285,000 tCO2; Mexico 195,000 tCO2 and Peru 95,000 
tCO2 (rounded values) 
47 See for a detailed description Annex 23

=== PAGE 68 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 62 
 
 
 
 
Market assessments made in the different countries show that electricity demand for EVs even with a high 
penetation rate as targeted by the IEA scenario EV30@30 (30% of new registration of vehcles are electric 
in 2030) represent 1-digit figures of demand and less than the annual growth rate experienced in the 
countries (see figure below as example from one country). In no country the electricity demand by 2030 is 
more than 5% of the current supply even if the highest growth strategy of EVs of IEA would be achieved. 
This is due to the fact that vehicle turnover rates are low i.e. the change of the vehicle stock takes long (this 
is also true in other countries e.g. in Norway nearly 90% of vehicles sold in 2022 were electric but the total 
vehicle stock of EVs is still below30%). The limiting factor by 2030 is in general not electricity supply or new 
demand created by EVs. The problems which grids must face are of limited localized power supply, limited 
additional capacity at substations and peak power demand. Therefore design of adequate charging 
methods for e-buses is for example critical to avoid localized problems and to avoid increasing peak 
demands. 
 
Figure 12: EV Electricity Demand versus Electricity Supply Projections Brazil 2020-2030 
 
Source: AFD, Country diagnostic report Brazil 
 
D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
 
The paradigm shift of the proposed Program is achieved by having as long-term outcome a EV conducive 
ecosystem which results in investors purchasing EVs on a commercial base and a modal share of 
sustainable transport increased. Barriers towards mass EV deployment have been successfully eliminated 
or significantly reduced through Program interventions including FA and TA. Accelerated EV investments 
take place due to experiences made, business models established, reduced entry barriers, lower risks and 
costs and new market players. The transformation of public transportation into an attractive and efficient 
system is achieved by increasing services quality and working with partners on policies for incentives to 
shift to public transport and increasing ridership. Both approaches, the energy transition in transport and 
the mobility transition, together lead to a paradigm change towards sustainable mobility. The 
transformative shift is possible through the uptake of commercial EVs and the improved ecosystem for EVs

=== PAGE 69 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 63 
 
 
 
 
resultant of the concrete interventions as well as of policy assistance and capacity building. The 
transportation sector is transformed to a sustainable low-carbon system with a dominance of public 
transportation and NMT. Multiple countries in the region are establishing Green Recovery Plans due to the 
COVID-19 crisis as a vehicle to transform the society by investing in profitable infrastructure which creates 
economic, environmental and social benefits, short-term jobs and a long-term greening of the economy. 
Electrified and sustainable public transportation systems are thereby a core feature. Coutnries are 
interested in shifting to e’mobility ‘ this is expressed in updated NDCs as well as in activities on’going in all 
countries in the realm of e’mobility. What lacks is clarity on policies and an enabling environment for EVs, 
appropriate business models especially for e’buses and experience with initial large fleets, especially also 
in Colombia outside the large cities with established mass transit systems. The Program activities can 
remove these barriers and can showcase successful and commercialy attractive implementation of large 
fleets. This combined with the falling costs of EVs of all categories allows to fast’start EV deployment on a 
mass scale and move from a fossil’based transportation system to an electric one. The willingness of the 
public sector is there - the interest of the private sector is also manifest if the implementation has 
manageable risks and an acceptable profit rate – what can bring these components to spur e-mobility 
deployment is the design and implemetnaiton of adequate policies, framnework conditions, business 
models and performance experience of large fleets. 
 
In absence of a program E-mobility will also be the norm in Latin America as in the rest of the world – just 
at a later stage. The key issue is about accelerating mass transformation at a faster pace and at an earlier 
stage. An e-mobility program in this context is not different than any other GHG mitigation project – the 
question is not so muchi IF it will happen but if we can accelerate with GCF investments WHEN and to 
what EXTENT it will happen. The figure below shows a modelling made for Colombia with and without 
Program showing the projected deployment of EVs. The same modelling was made for all involved 
countries for e-buses, LCVs and taxis. The results including the model assumptions and modelling 
approach are detailed in the country reports per country (country reports number 3 (E-Motion Country 
Intervention Strategies) and the respective Excel Files per country included under Annex 3. 
 
The initial pipeline developed in 2021/2022 and updated thereafter showed that the majority of investment 
plans to purchase e-buses have not been fulfilled. The stakeholders contacted mention the lack of 
concessional finance and the prevailing risks as core reason. The program country which has progressed 
strongly in e-buses is Colombia but focusing to an overwhelming extent on Bogota plus to a much smaller 
extent in Cali and Medellin. Investments in smaller cities, targeted by this Program, have not taken place. 
The need for the Program is thus obvious and the delay in approving this Program is one of the reasons 
why Latin American cities have not deployed more e-buses resulting in retarding dearly needed initial 
investments in this technology to reduce with speed GHG emissions.

=== PAGE 70 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 64 
 
 
 
 
Figure 13: Projected E-Bus Prices, Bus Demand and Uptake of E-Buses with program and under BAU 
 
Source: AFD (2021), E-Motion Country Intervention Strategy Colombia 
 
The potential for scaling up and replication is ensured at the national and the international level through 
capacity created in the E-mobility ecosystem, proven cases of successful business models, improved 
profitability and reduced risks of EV investments and knowledge products. The experience of various 
countries shows that once fleets of EVs are operational and not only pilot projects, the technology is taken 
up quickly if the business environment is conducive. The projects financed under this Program function as 
trigger projects demonstrating the commercial viability of EVs. Performance risks are reduced greatly and 
business models have been introduced which make the uptake of EVs on a purely commercial base 
viable. This is linked with decreasing investment costs of EVs reaching cost-parity to fossil units in the 
medium term. Scaling up and replication is also enabled through TA at policy level including the 
development of sectoral roadmaps, design and establishment of appropriate policy incentives and 
knowledge management instruments as well as capacity building. 
It is expected that commercial investors will crowd in to the e-bus area once the framework conditions (e.g. 
guarantees, concession contracts) and the performance risks of e-buses are manageable. This has been 
the case in Santiago de Chile as well as Bogota. A coalition of investors announced end 2021 the intention 
to invest 1 billion in e-buses in Latin America48. The Programs TA creates conditions which are favourable 
to actually materialize such investments and the investment component of the Program reduces the risks 
to investors and operators. The Program is thus critical to unlock the private investment potential. For 
commercial inestors to crowd in the TA is crucial to establish a conducive framework including e.g. 
concession contracts adn business models as well as clear government policies. Just as important is that 
large-scale fleet deployment with such business models take place as proof of concept and to reduce 
performance risks for operators and financiers. Both TA as well as concessional finance for FA are 
however not required anymore in the future once the framework conditions are established and once the 
risks are reduced as lower costs of EVs make these profitable already in the short to medium term under 
the mentioned conditions of an adequate e-mobility environment. 
Potential for knowledge sharing and learning: The TA Component includes capacity building, training 
and monitoring. Technical assistance is used to ensure delivery of adequate training from equipment 
 
48 New $1 billion dollar commitment from investors to deliver zero-emission buses in Latin America - C40 Cities

=== PAGE 71 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 65 
 
 
 
 
suppliers or third parties for EV and charger operations and maintenance, safety trainings and capacity 
building in optimal management of EV fleets. Training materials, reports and knowledge products 
elaborated under this header in the different countries will be shared through multiple channels under the 
knowledge management activity. This will be realized at a national level thus removing one of the barriers 
to more widespread deployment of EVs in each country and also on an international level by sharing 
documents and experiences between countries and also with countries outside the Program. The Program 
will also develop professional knowledge products and share experiences and cases through channels 
such as webinars, electronic reports, case-studies, and workshops to disseminate experiences and build a 
base for replication. The Program will use existing channels within GIZ, AFD, CAF and KfW as well as 
those of other platforms for information dissemination. 
Contribution to the creation of an enabling framework: The TA Component includes the support of 
enabling policies for EV deployment: Technical support will be provided to the relevant national and local 
authorities in order to set-up, improve and/or enforce enabling public policies and legal, regulatory, fiscal 
and/or normative frameworks for e-mobility. One of the main instruments used thereby are sector 
roadmaps for electrification of public transport, roadmaps for electrification of taxis, and roadmaps for 
electrification of LCVs. Whilst various countries have general EV roadmaps or targets these remain at a 
macro-level. Sectoral roadmaps combined with investment projects perform a base for more specific 
targets, intervention instruments and actions allowing to take steps towards actual implementation. 
Sectoral and specific roadmaps are a tool to steer climate investments and initiatives beyond the 
Program’s scope and will help to inform and/or further detail the countries long term mobility strategies and 
their Nationally Determined Contributions. 
Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies: The TA Component includes activities such as 
roadmaps (see above) which outline an agreed-upon set of pragmatic and realistic measures to achieve 
sustainable mobility targets. Regulatory activities realized by the Program will tackle transport demand 
policies but also include aspects such as battery management and disposal regulations, charging 
infrastructure standards, pricing policies for EV charging, public transport regulatory aspects including 
aspects such as concession contracts (concession length, vehicle turnover guarantees), separation of 
vehicle ownership and vehicle operations, tariff structure etc. Technical assistance in this area is linked up 
with investment projects making it more credible and pragmatic. Roadmaps are developed in collaboration 
with all stakeholders and have clear short and medium term targets, means to achieve these targets to be 
realistic and implementable and to go beyond a vision statement. An example of a recenet and clear 
roadmap which helps to accelerate a transition is the Transit zero emission vehicle roadmap of the State of 
Colorado, USA which outlines the pathway for full electrification of buses49. An interesting roadmap for the 
region is also from Chile50. 
Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national 
climate change adaptation strategies and plans: Transport contributes almost one-quarter of the 
current global energy-related GHG emissions and is growing faster than any other energy end-use sector.  
Limiting the global temperature increase to below 2 degrees Celsius requires changing this transport 
emissions trajectory. The Paris Declaration on Electro-Mobility and Climate Change & Call to Action, calls 
for the deployment of EVs compatible with a 20% share of all road transport vehicles in 2030. All countries 
have however expressed their keen interest in embarking on this transformational shift towards e-mobility. 
Without significant GHG reductions in the transportation sector countries will not be able to achieve their 
decarbonization plans. Fostering e-mobility and kick-starting EV deployment is thus consistent with 
relevant national climate -resilient development pathways.

D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Sustainable Development Goal alignment 
 
The Program has as main target to reduce GHG emissions. It contributes significantly to sustainable 
development goals (SDG) 3 (“good health and well-being”), SDG goal 7 (“affordable and clean energy”), 
SDG goal 9 (“industry, innovation and infrastructure”), SDG goal 11 (“sustainable cities and communities”), 
and SDG 13 (“climate action”). 
 
Environmental co-benefits 
 
Major environmental co-benefits are reduced emissions of pollutants and reduced noise emissions. The 
major concern for air pollution in the cities is PM2.5 and NOx emissions. The impact of the Program on 
these pollutants has been quantified based on combustion emissions of new fossil baseline vehicles 
(compliant with vehicle emission standards of the country) using the EU COPERT model i.e. this is a 
conservative approach as emission reductions are not based on comparing the old replaced with the new 
EV but on comparing a new (BAU) fossil with a new EV. For the methodology used, the database as well 
as calculations see Annex 23. The following table shows the projected reduction of pollutants of the 
Program. 
 
Table 4: Projected Lifetime Pollutant Reductions (in tons lifetime) 
Projected tons of PM2.5 reduced  
Projected tons of NOx reduced 
41 
4,842 
Note: Total Program impact calculated with the same approach as for GHG reduction 
Source: Annex 23 
 
Social co-benefits including health impacts 
 
The major social benefit is improved air quality (plus reduced noise). Air pollution is a major problem in 
most Latin American cities with levels affecting seriously human health. According to the latest urban air 
quality database, 98% of cities in low- and middle-income countries with more than 100,000 inhabitants do 
not meet WHO air quality guidelines51. The WHO estimates that annually 4.2 million deaths result due to 
excessive exposure to fine particulate matter equivalent to 7.6% of all deaths52. The poor are 
disproportionately affected by air pollution as they tend to live and work closer to its sources53. Children 
and the elderly are particularly vulnerable. Recent studies show that women are more affected by poor air 
quality than men54. The transportation sector is thereby a major source of urban air pollution. Commercial 
fossil vehicles are a major source of air pollution in cities. Although they do not represent a majority of 
vehicles they are extensively used (large mileage), have high emission factors and are basically diesel 
powered. The diagnostic reports realized in all Program countries shows that the targeted commercial 
vehicles contribute in general more to air pollution than private passenger cars or motorcycles (see Annex 
2). Replacing such vehicles with EVs thus has a significant impact on air quality and health of people.  
The COVID’19 pandemic has created an unprecedented challenge for many Latin American countries, 
from the health and economic perspectives, but it is also opening new opportunities for an accelerated 
sustainable transformation of its energy, transport, and urban landscapes while contributing to economic 
recovery. A recent report prepared by the ILO shows that fostering of electric mobility can have a 
significant positive job impact primarily due to the induced impact of savings of consumers on petrol and 
maintenance resulting in increased spending on goods with a high income elasticity which tend to be 
 
51 https://www.who.int/airpollution/data/cities-2016/en/ 
52 https://www.who.int/gho/phe/en/ 
53 (Mitchell, 2013) 
54 (Clougherty, 2010)

=== PAGE 73 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 67 
 
 
 
 
labour intensive service-goods (ILO, 2020). A report of McKinsey also reveals that with the COVID 
pandemic the interest in EVs has risen amongst customers due to the fact seemingly that the pandemic 
has raised the awareness among people of the negative impact of fossil transport modes being suddenly 
being able to experience clean air during lockdowns55. 
 
Economic co-benefits 
 
The monetary value of reduced air emissions (GHG and air pollutants) has been calculated (see Annex 23 
for the methodology, dataset and results). The economic cost of pollution is calculated by assigning a 
monetary value to emissions of PM2.5 and NOx for each country based on local levels of pollution at the 
ground level and the impact on health and costs caused by this type of pollution per country. This is based 
on the exposure of the population to contamination and how increased pollution increases mortality risks 
using the World Health Organization's dose response functions to concentration. The greater risk of 
mortality or, more precisely, the cost of premature death is valued economically on the basis of stated 
preference studies as performed by the OECD.  
 
EVs are more energy efficient than fossil vehicles. PT is more energy efficient than usage of private 
vehicles. The energy savings resulting from the Program are estimated at 48,343 TJ over the lifetime of 
vehicles. 
 
The TA will include plans for a just transition to capture the potential positive benefits in terms of new jobs 
being created with e-mobility whilst enabling people which loose jobs e.g. in the maintenance area to find 
new jobs. 
 
Gender-sensitive development impact 
 
Gender issues and development impact will be described in the gender-disaggregated targets in projects’ 
targets. Each Program will be required to meet AFD’s policy on gender equality. Individual projects will 
also be guided by the gender action plan (GAP) as included in Annex 8. The GAP includes actions and 
targets (i) on improved access for women to public transportation; (ii) improved sex-disaggregated data 
collection on urban transport; (iii) Improved women’s access to jobs; (iv) interventions including awareness 
raising and infrastructure to reduce sexual harassment in public transport; and (v) trainings and workshops 
to disseminate best practices for reducing sexual harassment in public transport. 
 
On a cross-sectoral level the GAP has as target to integrate gender considerations in transport sector 
activities. This includes (i) the usage of a gender lens when designing the roadmap for different EV 
segments and EV support policies; (ii) Increased awareness about gender equality gaps and opportunities 
in transport through capacity buildings and training; and (iii) Increased knowledge in how to include gender 
equality in Urban Transport projects through specific training materials.

55 Mobility investments in the next normal | McKinsey

=== PAGE 74 ===

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 68 
 
 
 
 
The market study (Annex  2) identified the following barriers hindering an take-up of EVs in the countries 
involved: 
• 
High upfront investment; 
• 
Lack of profitability of EV investments; 
• 
High risk of EV investments (financial and technical risks); 
• 
Lack of know-how on appropriate system design, business models, financial models and technical 
know-how; 
• 
First-mover problem as no commercial EV fleets yet operate in the country;  
• 
Lack of an enabling (policy) framework for sustainable mobility and EV deployment. 
AFD has also realized market costing studies on the different vehicle segments for different price ranges 
and countries (see Annex 2). The commercial attractiveness of EVs versus fossil vehicles has been 
assessed based on parameters such as the Total Cost of Ownership (TCO), FIRR, capital and equity 
requirements, cash flow and risks. This again serves as base to structure the financial instruments 
required to resolve the barriers to commercial viability of EVs in the selected vehicle segments. The 
conclusions from the market study that upfront financial support is required combined with policy advice to 
create a favourable e-mobility ecosystem is supported by the benchmark study (Annex 22) which shows 
that countries which have followed this path are succeeding in deploying on a mass scale EVs whilst 
others remain in the pilot stage. 
Contrary to renewable energy generation that is increasingly cost competitive, EVs are not yet 
commercially viable and remain largely underfunded. The market and benchmark studies show that, if 
unaddressed, commercial EV deployment on a mass scale will take place in a delayed manner. This will 
delay the transformation towards low carbon transport systems in the Program countries.  
The EV sectors targeted have (i) limited access to commercial funding, (ii) early-mover costs and 
disadvantages including high performance risks (iii) lack of sufficient commercial viability including 
profitability and acceptable risk levels. Therefore financiers are not in a position to fund such activities with 
commercial loans at the current stage.  
Governments and transport operators also lack capacity and know-how on the appropriate technology 
choices and the most appropriate and conducive policies and business models to foster in a cost-effective 
manner EV deployment. This is clearly evident from public statements in favour of electric mobility which is 
however not followed up with concrete actions and implementations.  
D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Section B1 already includes a short overview of countries including the carbon grid factor, population and 
vehicle data, energy prices and major EV policies. This section therefore focuses on the climate policies of 
the involved countries. The majority of countries have included in the NDCs electric mobility as a core 
mitigation measure to reduce transport emissions and have formulated concrete electrification targets 
through regulations, policies or EV strategies. The project supports the countries in implementing the 
following measures and targets: 
In the framework of the elaboration of the E-Motion Concept Note until today, the French Development 
Agency and its partners (Proparco, CAF, KfW, GIZ) have hold meetings with the NDAs of the countries 
concerned by the Program and key actors of the e-mobility sector to ensure that the FP answers to the 
needs of the countries (See Annex 7). These actors include development banks, private banks, 
municipalities and regional states, power utilities, transport operators etc. The following table gives an 
overview of consulted stakeholders per country and the identified potential investment projects per country. 
With all the listed stakeholders virtual and in many cases physical meetings were organized. With many 
stakeholders multiple meetings were realized. The Program also made per country virtual kick-off meetings 
with stakeholders, a large amount of individual meetings and a discussion of final results. The FP, the

Argentina 
 
Argentina’s total GHG emissions are estimated at 361 MtCO2e in 2020 (excluding LUCF) of which the 
transport sector 39 MtCO2e.63 In its NDC (Nationally Determined Contribution, 2015) Argentine commits to 
an absolute net emission target of 359 million tCO2e by 2030. The updated NDC of 10/2021 includes that 
electric mobility is a core mitigation measure in the transport sector. Argentina launched the development 
of its National Electric Mobility Strategy in May 2018 with the support of UN Environment. Argentina is 
currently formulating specific legislation on electric vehicles, establishing the conditions for the installation 
or operation of charging centers, and at the same time, exploring possible options for the local 
development of the electric mobility industry. Decree 51/2018 establishes a tariff reduction for the import of 
electric buses up to a certain level.  In terms of electric vehicle charging infrastructure, provision 283/2019 
regulates the service of electric charging at fuelling stations and defines safety specifications on the 
installation and registration of charging stations. 
Brazil 
 
Brazil’s total GHG emissions in 2020 were 1.06 GtCO2e (excluding :LUCF) and transport emissions 190 
MtCO2e64. In its updated NDC of 11/2023 Brazil commits to reduce GHG emissions by 53% by 2030 
compared to the year 2005 with the goal of achieving climate neutrality by the year 2050. The NDC indicates 
that the transportation sector shall promote efficiency measures and improve urban public transportation 
infrastructure to achieve mitigation targets. Brazil has already established various tax incentives for the 
import and purchase of EVs. There are 21 bills pending in the Chamber of Deputies on electric mobility that 
seek greater incentives for electric vehicles (Promobe, 2019). Resolution 97/2015 reduces the import tariff 
from 35% to 0% for electric vehicles and fuel cells. Senate Bill No. 454/2017 which seeks to prohibit the sale 
of internal combustion vehicles by 2060, is currently under discussion in the Federal Senate. 
Colombia 
Colombia’s  GHG emissions for 2020 are estimated at 187 MtCO2e (excluding LUCF) Transportation 
emissions for the same year are 28 MtCO2e65. Colombia's NDC Update estimates that according to the 
reference scenario for 2030 emissions would reach 346 MtCO2eq.  Within the mitigation goals Colombia 
commits to emit a maximum of 169 MtCO2e in 2030 (equivalent to a 51% reduction of emissions). The NDC 
contemplates for the transportation sector amongst others to achieve 600,000 registered electric taxis, 
buses, light commercial vehicles including small trucks and official vehicles. The Electric Mobility Law has 
managed to provide for measures in public transportation services such as compliance with a minimum 
quota of 30% of EVs in new acquisitions or contracts, taking into account the commercial offer in 
Colombia. According to the same law, the goals for the incorporation of EVs in the acquisition of the fleet 
of zero-emission mass transportation systems must follow the scheme of minimum proportions of 10% in 
2025, 20% in 2027, 40% in 2029, 60% in 2031, 80% in 2033 and 100% in 2035 (Congress of Colombia, 
2019). As a complement to the Law, the National Government has developed the National Strategy for 
Electric Mobility (ENME), which aims to promote the electrification of the transportation sector. In addition 
to the above, the National Energy Plan (PEN) 2020-2050 presents projections for the incorporation of EVs,

under the scenario of meeting the GHG reduction commitments (20% by 2030) (UPME, 2019). Projections 
for 2030 include 630,000 electric motorcycles, 370,000 electric light vehicles, 40,000 e-taxis and 20,000 
electric urban freight vehicles. 
Costa Rica 
 
Total GHG emissions of Costa Rica are estimated at 10.9 million tCO2e in 2019 with land transport being 
responsible for more than 50% of emissions. Emissions under a BAU scenario are expected to increase by 
45% by 2050. The updated NDC of Costa Rica includes as target net emissions of 9.1 MtCO2e by 2030 
and holds on to the net zero target by 2050. Greening the transportation sector is key to achieving these 
targets. Electrifying mobility is considered as essential and a national priority. The updated NDC has 
quantified 2030 electric mobility targets for public transport, passenger cars and fleets (8% of the vehicle 
stock). For other vehicle areas e.g. motorcycles targets and measures shall be developed to migrate 
towards EVs. Costa Rica has also developed a national plan for electric transport which includes concrete 
steps towards electrification of vehicles and has approved 2018 the law on incentives and promotion of 
electric transportation which includes targets for EV penetration, the establishment of a public charging 
infrastructure as well as important tax incentives for private EVs. 
Dominican Republic 
 
The estimated GHG emissions of the Dominican Republic in 2020 were 37 million tCO2e (excluding LUCF) 
of which 7.2 million tCO2e from the transport sector66. The updated version of the Nationally Determined 
Contribution NDC-RD 2020 (Gobierno de la República Dominicana, 2020) has as target a 27% reduction 
in GHG emissions in relation to a Business as Usual (BAU) scenario by 2030. In terms of electric mobility, 
the NDC-RD proposes (i) Electrification of the fleet of diesel buses; (ii) Renewal of public transportation 
vehicles, such as cabs and "conchos" with electric and hybrid vehicles; (iii) Introduction of electric buses 
for school transportation service; (iv) Regulation and creation of policies to encourage the transition to 
electric and hybrid mobility for private usage. In 2020 the Dominican Republic has adopted a National 
Strategic Plan for Electric Mobility (INTRANT, 2020) developed by INTRANT in collaboration with the IDB. 
It provides for a short, medium and long term transformation of the transportation sector. The plan is 
aligned with both the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 2030 and the Sustainable Development 
Plan 2030 of the Dominican Republic (Ministerio de Economía, Planificación y Desarrollo , 2012) and aims 
at all modes of road transportation. Targets of the plan are 30% of official vehicles and public buses shall 
be electric by 2030, whilst the target for the private sector is 10%. 14,000 public chargers shall be 
operational by 2030. By 2050 the respective goals are 100% EVs for official vehicles and public buses and 
70% of all private vehicles shall be electric. For the freight sector the target for 2050 is 50% of all units to 
be electric.  
Mexico 
 
The GHG emissions of Mexico for 2020 are estimated at 592 MtCO2e(excluding LUCF) with the transport 
sector contributing 109 MtCO267. The baseline scenario for 2030 projects GHG emissions of 973 MtCO2e of 
which 266 MtCO2e from the transport sector (Gobierno de México, 2015). The Government of Mexico 
committed in its NDC to reduce its emissions by 22% by 2030. For the transport sector the target is set at 
18%, reaching 218 MtCO2 by 2030. The Climate Change Strategy to 2050 published in 2016 established 
that within 10 years the use of EVs in public transport shall be common and within 40 years in all types of 
transport (SEMARNAT-INECC, 2016). In addition to the national instruments, the federal states 
 
66 Climatewatch data; in 2020 COVID-19 resulted GHG reductions of the transport sector of 9% compared to 
pre-COVID whilst total GHG emissions dropped by 7%. 
67 Climatewatch data; in 2020 COVID-19 resulted GHG reductions of the transport sector of nearly 30% 
compared to pre-COVID whilst total GHG emissions dropped by only 10%.
