Secretariat’s assessment of FP237 
Proposal name: 
E-Motion: E-Mobility and Low Carbon Transportation
Accredited entity: 
Agence Française de Développement (AFD) (French Development Agency) 
Countries:  
Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Mexico, Peru 
Programme size:   
Large 
I.
Overall assessment of the Secretariat
1.
The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following
remarks:
Strengths 
Points of caution 
The programme consistently scores medium–
high or high against GCF’s investment criteria, 
and is consistent with GCF’s aim of supporting 
a low-carbon transformational shift and 
innovation in low-carbon transport.  
The risk of limited commercial uptake of 
electric vehicles after the programme 
investments is being mitigated in the project 
design, but it needs to be carefully monitored 
to potentially take corrective action.  
GCF’s concessional finance will accelerate an 
e-mobility market transition in Latin America.
GCF and Agence Française de Développement
will be providing essential financial assistance
to (i) de-risk transactions; and (ii) boost the
market in both the public and private sectors.
In order to assure strong co-financing and 
parallel financing to materialize and support 
the market transition, the entities need to 
continue the strong country engagement 
demonstrated through the funding proposal 
development phase. 
The programme provides technical assistance 
to develop conducive policy frameworks and 
identify innovative business models to create 
synergies with other investment platforms in 
the region, which is crucial for e-mobility 
success in a region with a potential for rapid 
growth in electric vehicle uptake.  
The programme needs to carefully balance 
public and private investments during 
implementation to react to developing 
market opportunities due to the rapidly 
evolving nature of the public/private e-
mobility market.  
The programme demonstrates strong country 
ownership by aligning with nationally 
determined contributions and low-emission, 
urban, and climate policies.  
2.
The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the
term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity, and, if considered
appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in Annex II of document GCF/B.39/02.
II.
Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment
2.1 
Programme background

=== PAGE 122 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 122 
 
 
 
 
3. 
The programme objective is to accelerate electric vehicle (EV) deployment and enable a 
large-scale regional transition towards electric mobility in Latin America through financial and 
technical assistance. The programme will implement interventions to kick-start EV mass 
deployment significantly earlier than under a business-as-usual (BAU) scenario by reducing the 
risk profile of investments and utilizing comprehensive technical assistance.   
4. 
The key strategic value of the programme is that it functions as a market accelerator, 
enabling a faster uptake of e-mobility than under a BAU scenario, thus avoiding a lock-in of 
long-lived path dependencies and allocations of assets into fossil fuel technology pathways. The 
programme fills the gap between initial pilots already existing in the region and long-term 
transformational targets. These interventions are made now, at a time when e-mobility is not 
yet commercially viable and thus initial investment support is required, as is the case in all 
countries that have a significant uptake of e-mobility.  
5. 
Latin America has strong enabling conditions for clean transport and e-mobility. The 
region has one of the cleanest electricity generation portfolios in the world with the programme 
countries having a median carbon grid factor of 0.252 kilograms of carbon dioxide equivalent 
(CO2 eq)/kilowatt-hour, thus enabling e-mobility to have a significant greenhouse gas (GHG) 
mitigation impact. The adoption of e-mobility strategies further enables countries to move 
towards a decarbonization pathway. The updated nationally determined contributions (NDCs) 
of programme countries all include measures to reduce transport emissions. In their NDCs, the 
majority of countries have prioritized e-mobility as a core mitigation measure to reduce 
transport emissions and have formulated concrete electrification targets through regulations, 
policies or EV road maps, which are also supported through the present funding proposal.  
6. 
The main motivating forces to foster e-mobility in the countries included in the 
programme are (i) reduce GHG emissions; (ii) improve air quality and reduce health problems 
associated with air pollution; (iii) reduce fossil fuel dependency on imported fuels and exposure 
to external price shocks; (iv) achieve positive economic impacts related to reduced health costs, 
reduced fuel import bills, and job creation; (v) participate in the new growth industry of EV and 
battery manufacturing; and (vi) increase the attractiveness of public transportation vis-a -vis 
private motorized transport.  
7. 
The programme financial envelope is EUR 307.31 million (of which EUR 210.98 million 
and USD 103.90 million), utilizing loan and grant instruments. The GCF contribution to the total 
is 37.6 per cent (EUR 78.33 million and USD 40.26 million). This consists of EUR 56 million and 
USD 40.27 million in concessional loans and EUR 22.3 million in grants for technical assistance. 
The direct mitigation impact over the programme lifetime of 25 years is 4,334,051 million 
tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MtCO2eq). 
8. 
GCF categorizes the programme as B (medium) for environmental and social safeguards. 
2.2 
Component-by-component analysis 
9. 
The programme is structured in three components:  
(a) 
Component 1: Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging 
electrification and shift to public transport (technical assistance);  
(b) 
Component 2: Investments into electric public transport mode shift (financial 
assistance); and  
(c) 
Component 3: Deployment of EV fleets (financial assistance).  
Component 1: Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and a 
shift to public transport (total cost: EUR 34 million; GCF cost: EUR 21 million in grants)

=== PAGE 123 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 123 
 
 
 
 
10. 
The outcome of component 1 is improved public and private sector capacity and an 
improved regulatory environment for e-mobility. Component 1 is linked with output 1.1: 
Establishment of a sustainable mobility ecosystem encouraging electrification and a shift to 
public transport.  
(a) 
The activities of Component 1 are: 1.1.1 – Implement a local e-mobility-conducive 
framework; 1.1.2 – Implement a national e-mobility-conducive framework; 1.1.3 – 
Implement a regional e-mobility conducive framework; and 1.1.4 – Realize interventions 
with a gender perspective; 
(b) 
Component 1 is technical assistance only.  
Component 2: Investment measures for public transport mode shift (total cost: EUR 27 million and 
USD 15.8 million; GCF cost: EUR 8.5 million and USD 6.1 million in loans)  
11. 
The outcome of component 2 is reduced GHG emissions from the transport sector. 
Component 2 is linked with output 2.1.: E-mobility is combined with public transport measures 
to foster mode shift to low-carbon transport.  
12. 
The activities of component 2 are: 2.1.1 – Implement urban mobility measures to foster 
public transport in two countries. 
13. 
This component is focused on lending and financing activities only.  
Component 3: Deployment of EV fleets (total cost: EUR 143 million and USD 88 million; GCF cost: 
EUR 47.5 million and USD 34.1 million in loans)  
14. 
The outcome of this component is reduced GHG emissions from the transport sector. 
Component 3 is linked to output 2: EV fleets are deployed with innovative business models to 
enable their mass application.  
15. 
The activities of component 3 are deployment of e-buses; deployment of charging 
infrastructure for e-buses; implementing bus depot upgrades for e-buses; and implementing 
grid upgrades for connecting e-bus chargers.  
16. 
This component is focused on lending and financing activities only.  
Project management (total cost: EUR 7 million; GCF cost: EUR 1 million in grants) 
17. 
Activity 1.1.5. refers to project sourcing, structuring and monitoring. This includes 
technical, financial and institutional advisory services and the full feasibility of the project 
including economic, environmental, social, gender and legal aspects. This includes defining a 
project contractual scheme, ensuring a proper risk allocation among key stakeholders, assuring 
the bankability of the project, and securing a proper allocation of GCF concessional resources. 
This support is key as innovative business models are often needed to bridge the cost gap 
between EVs and internal combustion engines. Project preparation can also support verification 
of the conformity of e-buses at the supplier end, including the bidding documents and test 
developments. Projects sourced and designed under this activity will be financed within 
components 2 and 3 and result in outputs 2.1 and 2.2. Project monitoring is also part of this 
activity, which will contribute to a proper structuring of the projects, an adequate risk allocation 
and bankability, and the deployment of innovative business models.  
III. 
Assessment of performance against investment criteria 
18. 
The programme consistently scores medium-high or high against GCF’s investment 
criteria. It is also consistent with GCF’s aim of supporting a low-carbon transformational shift 
and innovation, in this case in e-mobility and low-carbon transport. The programme is well

=== PAGE 124 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 124 
 
 
 
 
suited to the needs of the recipient countries. A detailed assessment of the programme’s fit with 
GCF investment criteria is provided below. 
3.1 
Impact potential                                                             Scale: Medium to high 
19. 
The impact potential is expected to be 4,334,051 Mt CO2 eq of GHG emissions avoided. 
This is based on an initial conservative estimate related to an indicative pipeline of subprojects 
in four countries receiving investment support.  
20. 
GHG emission calculations for e-buses have been made for potential e-bus investment 
projects per country. While baseline emissions per vehicle category are comparable for different 
countries (variations are due to vehicle mileage, fuel type used and, to a lesser extent, different 
specific fuel consumption values), the carbon grid factor per country influences the emissions of 
the EVs and therefore the emission reductions.  
21. 
In addition, GHG emissions include calculations for public transport measures. This 
includes GHG reductions from measures to increase patronage. The methodology used for 
calculating GHG reduction is based on the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate 
Change (UNFCCC) methodology ACM0016 and the UNFCCC methodological tool “Baseline 
emissions for modal shift measures in urban passenger transport”. GHG reductions are due to 
additional passengers who, in absence of the electric bus option, would have used other modes 
of transport with higher emission factors. Measures which increase public transport usage 
include infrastructure investments in bus lanes and bus stations, smart ticketing, improved 
connectivity and intramodality, modernized bus fleets, and related measures to promote public 
transport systems in urban settings.  
22. 
The emission factor per passenger-kilometre of private means of transport is two times 
higher than for public transport. The assumed lifetime of the programme is 25 years in 
accordance with infrastructure lifespan.  
3.2 
Paradigm shift                                                                                    Scale: High  
23. 
The programme will lead to transformation of urban EV transport systems and 
penetration via enabling policies, infrastructure investment, and technology and knowledge 
transfer for sustainable EV market development across the region. 
24. 
 The programme has potential for upscaling and replication at the national and 
international level through capacity created in the e-mobility ecosystem, successful business 
models, improved profitability and reduced risks of EV investments and knowledge products.   
25. 
It also has high potential for knowledge-sharing and learning through the technical 
assistance component at regional, national and local levels. In addition, the programme also to 
contribute to the creation of an enabling framework. Technical assistance includes the support 
of enabling policies for EV deployment. Technical support will aim to set up, improve and 
enforce enabling public policies and legal, regulatory, fiscal and normative frameworks for e-
mobility. 
26. 
The programme also aims to have an overall contribution to climate-resilient 
development pathways consistent with national climate change adaptation strategies and plans. 
It will lead to significant GHG reductions in the transportation sector. Without this contribution, 
the countries will not be able to achieve their decarbonization plans. Fostering e-mobility and 
kick-starting EV deployment is consistent with relevant national climate-resilient development 
pathways.

=== PAGE 125 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 125 
 
 
 
 
3.3 
Sustainable development potential                          Scale: Medium to high 
27. 
The programme contributes to the following key Sustainable Development Goals 
(SDGs): SDG 3 (Good health and well-being), SDG 7 (Affordable and clean energy), SDG 9 
(Industry, innovation and infrastructure), SDG 11 (Sustainable cities and communities), and 
SDG 13 (Climate action).   
28. 
The main environmental co-benefits are reduced emissions of pollutants and reduced 
noise emissions. The major concern for air pollution in the cities is PM2.5 and NOx emissions. 
29. 
 The major social co-benefit is improved air quality, plus reduced noise. Air pollution is a 
major problem in most Latin American cities, with levels seriously affecting human health. 
According to the latest urban air quality database, 98 per cent of cities in low- and middle-
income countries with more than 100,000 inhabitants do not meet World Health Organization 
(WHO) air quality guidelines. The WHO estimates that annually 4.2 million deaths result due to 
excessive exposure to fine particulate matter, equivalent to 7.6 per cent of all deaths.  
30. 
The programme also delivers economic co-benefits due to the monetary value of 
reduced air emissions (GHG and air pollutants). The economic cost of pollution is calculated by 
assigning a monetary value to emissions of fine particulate matter (PM2.5) and nitrogen oxides 
for each country based on local levels of pollution at the ground level and the impact on health 
and costs caused by this type of pollution per country.  
3.4 
Needs of the recipient                                                                      Scale: High  
31. 
GCF concessionally is expected to address financial and technical capacity barriers, de-
risk and leverage public-private capital for the EV sector, support public finance flows and 
incentivize a shift to EV in urban contexts. 
32. 
The majority of countries have included electric mobility in their NDCs as a core 
mitigation measure to reduce transport emissions and have formulated concrete electrification 
targets through regulations, policies and/or EV strategies. The programme’s activities in each 
country are fully contextualized in the country plans, as further described in the funding 
proposal and the feasibility study. 
33. 
The accredited entity (AE) Agence Française de Développement (AFD) has also followed 
a robust stakeholder consultation process, which includes the national designated authorities 
(NDAs) in each country as well as development banks, private banks, municipalities and 
regional states, power utilities, transport operators, etc. 
34. 
The EV sectors in targeted countries have (i) limited access to commercial funding; (ii) 
early-mover costs and disadvantages including high performance risks; and (iii) lack of 
sufficient commercial viability including profitability and acceptable risk levels. Therefore, 
financiers are not in a position to fund such activities with commercial loans at the current 
stage. The programme countries depend on technical assistance that serves to enhance the 
viability of the subprojects and leads to long-term transformational impacts. The programme 
countries would not be able to source the required public funds on its own due to current fiscal 
realities (e.g. economic weakening, coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) recovery, high 
inflation). The GCF grants are therefore essential for the successful implementation of the 
required technical assistance activities. 
3.5 
Country ownership:                                                      Scale: Medium to high 
35. 
No-objection letters have been issued by all programme countries.

=== PAGE 126 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 126 
 
 
 
 
36. 
The programme is well aligned with countries’ policy objectives in low-emission 
transport, urban development, climate change commitments in NDCs and sectoral plans, which 
include concrete e-mobility targets. All countries have also established/are formulating EV 
policies and legislation. Some of the countries have established specific targets for the EV sector, 
to which this programme will contribute. 
37. 
The design of the programme, from the concept note stage to funding proposal 
development, was based on a detailed stakeholder assessment, which included multiple 
meetings with the NDAs and stakeholders in the public and private sector relevant to the 
programme, as well as countries’ diagnostics and market studies. 
38. 
The EV sector in programme countries have (i) limited access to commercial funding; 
(ii) early-mover costs and disadvantages including high performance risks; and (iii) lack of 
sufficient commercial viability including profitability and acceptable risk levels. Therefore, 
financiers are not in a position to fund such activities with commercial loans at the current 
stage. Governments and transport operators also lack capacity and know-how on the 
appropriate technology choices and the most appropriate and conducive policies and business 
models to foster cost-effective EV deployment. 
3.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness                                       Scale: Medium to high  
39. 
The total programme cost is EUR 210.98 million and USD 103.90 million, with a GCF 
contribution of EUR 78.33 million and USD 40.26 million, resulting in very efficient mitigation 
costs of EUR 26.7/tonne of carbon dioxide (tCO2) and a leverage ratio of 1:1.66.  
40. 
The economic internal rate of return (EIRR) is estimated at 18 per cent, including the 
measures for e-buses and public transport (in two countries), against the total programme cost, 
including technical assistance. The EIRR is above the social discount rates calculated by the 
Secretariat for the programme countries (between 5 to 8 per cent). 
41. 
The analysis was also conducted on the average financial internal rate of return (FIRR) 
for e-buses without concessional GCF finance (1 per cent) and with GCF concessional finance (9 
per cent). Without concessional finance, the FIRR is below the weighted average cost of capital 
(WACC) (8 per cent), while it is above the WACC with concessional finance. 
42. 
The mitigation cost is EUR 27/tCO2, which is in the medium to higher range of the GCF 
portfolio, reflecting the higher average GHG abatement costs of transport interventions. 
43. 
The AE has provided a comparison with eight other countries, where costs ranged 
between USD 97/tCO2 (China) to USD 476/tCO2 (Poland), with a total average of USD 227/tCO2 
across the eight countries. The programme therefore has an efficient and effective cost 
structure.  
44. 
The long-run viability of the programme is based on proven business models and the 
decreasing technology cost in the future. The sensitivity analysis of the financial model shows 
that if the capital expenditure for e-buses drops by 10 per cent, the FIRR is in most cases higher 
than the WACC without GCF. In summary, as the capital expenditure of e-buses drops, the 
subprojects will be financially viable with attractive returns in all the countries. 
45. 
The AE’s financial analysis shows that GCF concessional financing improves the FIRR of 
subprojects. In Brazil and Mexico, GCF provides a significant profitability enhancement by 
enabling the FIRR to improve above or close to the WACC of 8 per cent. In Colombia and Peru, 
however, even with GCF support, the subproject FIRR remains at 6 per cent, which is slightly 
below the target of 8 per cent. As AFD has presented an indicative pipeline of subprojects, the 
Secretariat does not have certainty of the exact amount of GCF resources to be allocated to each 
programme country. To mitigate the risk of country concentration, AFD has set a maximum of

=== PAGE 127 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 127 
 
 
 
 
35 per cent of the total resources per country. The financial analysis supports the need for 
concessionality in aggregate. 
46. 
Grant instruments will be used for technical assistance measures that cannot be 
financed by the private sector, but are necessary to ensure the replication impact of the 
programme, increase the attractiveness of the public transport system and ensure the intended 
shift towards e-mobility.  
IV. 
Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  
4.1 
Environmental and social safeguards  
47. 
Programme Background. The programme is part of the regional efforts that aims to 
accelerate EV deployment and enable a large-scale transition towards electro-mobility in 7 
countries in Latin America. The programme intends to invest the deployment of e-buses and 
upgrading the public transport systems. The AE indicates 4 investment projects in the 
programme pipeline, with focus on financing: (a) urban mobility measures such as 
pedestrianization and bus lanes and route restructuring, which include infrastructure 
connecting public transport systems to alternative modes of urban mobility; and (b) 
deployment of 850 e-buses, the related charging infrastructure, bus depot upgrades, and grid 
upgrades for connecting e-bus chargers. Among the key co-benefits of the programme includes 
the reduction of air pollution, noise levels and GHG emissions, and subsequently improving 
public health. Main social co-benefits include increased road safety as non-motorized transport 
and micro-mobility options tend to lower accident rates, lesser road congestion, and improved 
the quality of life for urban dwellers with more resilient urban transport infrastructure. 
48. 
Environmental and Social Risk Category and safeguards instrument. The AE 
assigned the programme low to moderate environmental and social (E&S) risk category that is 
equivalent to Category B as per the GCF’s E&S risk categorization. This is rationalised by 
financing activities with potential limited adverse environmental and/or social risks and 
impacts that are few, generally site-specific, largely reversible, and readily addressed through 
mitigation measures. No activities with potential significant adverse environmental and social 
risks and impacts that would fall under Category A will be supported under the programme. The 
Secretariat confirms the E&S risk category and that this is within the AE’s risk accreditation 
level. The AE has provided an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) 
applicable to all the programme components to ensure safeguards compliance. 
49. 
Compliance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards. The 
paragraphs below describe the programme’s compliance with GCF’s ESS standards: 
50. 
ESS 1: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and 
Impacts. The ESMF provides an analysis of the potential E&S risks based on the characteristics 
of the programme, suggests potential mitigation measures focusing on EV deployment and the 
construction of charging infrastructure, grid connections and bus depot upgrades, and outlines 
the eligibility criteria, exclusion list, and the E&S screening checklist for subproject due 
diligence. All subprojects will be required to prepare an ESIA and ESMP. Where gaps and 
moderate risks are identified as a result of the AE’s subproject E&S due diligence, an E&S 
commitment plan (ESCP) will be required and be included as part of financing agreement. All 
subprojects under the programme are required to comply with the applicable national and local 
E&S laws and regulations in the respective target countries as well as AE’s E&S Safeguards that 
are aligned with the International Finance Corporation (IFC) Performance Standards on 
Environmental and Social Sustainability.

=== PAGE 128 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 128 
 
 
 
 
51. 
ESS 2: Labor and Working Conditions. The programme identifies potential risks and 
adverse impacts on occupational health and safety for workers involved during the 
construction, implementation and operations of EVs, charging stations, grid connection and bus 
depot upgrades. The ESMF triggers the AE’s E&S safeguard on working conditions and considers 
the terms and conditions of employment, non-discrimination and equal opportunity, worker’s 
organizations, labour protection and the prevention of child labour and forced labour, etc. as 
part of the E&S due diligence. The programme will exclude any activities involving the use of 
forced labour or child labour. The programme will promote the employability of local labour in 
the construction area with gender focus, encouraging hiring service providers locally. 
52. 
ESS 3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention. The programme is expected of 
minor short-term impact of natural resources due to construction activities. ESMF is guided 
with the aim of avoiding and minimizing negative impacts on human health, biodiversity, and 
ecosystems through the prevention of pollution. Activities under the programme will potentially 
generate wastewater and hazardous materials as regards the use, replacement, reuse and 
recycling, and disposal of EV batteries (primarily lithium batteries and the peripheral 
components). Additional impacts can be attributed to small-scale construction activities of grid-
connected charging stations and bus depot upgrading, which may generate noise, dust particles, 
solid and liquid waste, and soil and water contamination from and scrapping of out-of-use 
vehicles, such as oils and lubricants, lead-acid batteries, tires and scrap metals. The programme 
will exclude the production, trade, storage or transport of large volumes of products hazardous 
chemicals, or commercial scale use of hazardous chemicals, and provide technical assistance to 
the targeted countries and requires an integral EV Battery Waste Management Program must be 
developed together with the ESMP, which will need to comply with current or proposed new 
regulatory requirements established by each target country for the management, treatment and 
disposal of End-of Life EV Batteries. The programme envisions that necessary legal framework 
and capacities on EV battery management will be developed and deployed during the 
implementation of the programme. ESMF specifies that if the local legislation does not have 
specific regulations in this regard, the best international practices must be applied. Given the 
regulatory framework on the proper management of EV batteries in the targeting countries are 
yet to be developed, the AE is recommended to further elaborate the guideline on EV battery 
management and detail the best practice and technical standards to be followed at the 
subproject level. 
53. 
ESS 4: Community Health, Safety and Security. The investment under the programme 
could potentially adversely affect the community health and safety. These could include 
infrastructure and equipment safety risks during the construction, maintenance and operation 
of the EV charging stations and upgrading related systems; increased accidentality as the low or 
non-existent noise of EVs are less noticeable during operation; potential for community 
exposure to hazardous materials and chemical substances related to EV battery disposal and 
scrapping conventional vehicles; and conflict risks between institutions, companies and 
community during the installation and cabling activities which may fluctuate the electric power 
service and cause power outrage in the affected communities, therefore, the community may 
have power outage. The ESMF has provided measures to manage these risks and required 
further risk assessment will be conducted at subproject level. The AE is recommended to 
incorporate measures to avoid and minimize community exposure to hazardous materials in the 
EV battery waste management plan at subproject level. 
54. 
ESS 5: Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement. The programme will exclude 
subprojects that involves land acquisition and involuntary resettlement through the exclusion 
list. As part of the subproject due diligence process, the programme will consider minimizing 
resettlement through altering project design, compensation and benefits for affected persons, 
community engagement, physical and/or economic displacement, and collaboration with other 
responsible agencies or sub-national jurisdictions. The programme will construct small-scale

=== PAGE 129 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 129 
 
 
 
 
urban charging stations and new connecting power lines located on lands of public domain and 
property of municipal or state authorities. The programme will build e-bus charging stations on 
sites belonging to the bus companies. 
55. 
ESS 6: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural 
Resources. The AE’s does not consider any eligible projects will pose risks on any modification 
to protected areas, critical habitats and or sensitive areas or affect any modified, natural and 
critical habitats or legally protected and internationally recognized areas. Hence the ESMF does 
not trigger AE’s safeguard in this regard. The Program will exclude projects that involve any 
modification to natural and critical habitats or legally protected and internationally recognized 
areas. As part of the subproject due diligence, the programme will exclude any operation 
leading to or requiring the destruction of critical habitat, or any forestry project which does not 
implement a plan for improvement and sustainable management. 
56. 
GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples.  All of the countries 
the project will work in have Indigenous Peoples.  As the project activities focus on accelerating 
Electric Vehicle deployment and a large-scale regional transition towards electro-mobility in 
Latin America through financial and technical assistance, the management of batteries for 
electric vehicles or of the hazardous waste from disposal of batteries may affect Indigenous 
Peoples.  Furthermore, there are no specific regulations or policies for the management of 
batteries for electric vehicles or of the hazardous waste from disposal of batteries in the 
countries that are part of the Programme.  Accordingly, the AE has included an Indigenous 
Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) as an annex to the ESMF.  The contents of the IPPF need to 
meet the requirements of the Green Climate Fund Indigenous People Policy.  In line with its 
roles and functions, the GCF Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice 
to the AE who is also encouraged to share emerging good practices and success stories with the 
Group. 
57. 
ESS 8: Cultural Heritage. The ESMF establishes specialized requirements for 
subproject specific ESMP, when warranted, must contain measures to ensure the protection of 
the cultural heritage from loss or damage and support its preservation. Per the AE’s assessment, 
eligibility projects under the program do not expect to affect properties and sites of 
archaeological, historical, cultural, artistic, and religious significance. The Program will exclude 
projects that affect cultural heritage. As part of the due diligence, the programme will confirm 
the identification and legal protected cultural heritage areas. The project will exclude Any 
activity leading to an irreversible modification or significant displacement of an element of 
culturally critical heritage or any activity leading to any risks and impacts on cultural heritage. 
58. 
Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (SEAH) safeguarding. 
The AE’s has identified potential SEAH risk related to the deployment of EVs in the public 
transport sector and in an low gender-based violence (GBV) and SEAH during the construction 
of small-scale EV infrastructure works. The AE will develop a SEAH action plan following if any 
questions will be identified. Potential mitigation measures include training transport operating 
companies to detect SEAH incidents, raising awareness and creating spaces free of gender-
based violence (GBV) and SEAH, and supporting the establishment of an adequate regulatory 
framework for public transport operating companies to implement gender policies preventing 
SEAH and GBV. All subprojects will comply with the requirements established in the GCF 
regarding Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment. 
59. 
Implementation arrangements. The AE will monitor and supervise the environmental 
and social performance and compliance with contractual requirements of the specific 
subprojects financed by programme. A project management unit (PMU), composed of 
professionals from the AE and the Executing Entity and supported by an external E&S and 
Gender expert, will be established and will support early E&S screening and to ensure the day-
to-day implementation and monitoring of the programme's impacts. The PMU’s E&S specialists

=== PAGE 130 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 130 
 
 
 
 
will also review E&S due diligence, ensure E&S supervision of al subproject. The AE will also 
ensure E&S monitoring. Three steps are determined for the appraisal at the project level, being: 
identification, instruction and commitment; Early E&S screening verifications, requirements 
and categorizations carried out by the project owner and PMU specialists; elaboration of ESDD 
to be conducted under the responsibility of the project owner with review of the PMU’s E&S 
specialist, elaboration of the ESCP having the project owner responsible for including E&S 
specifications from the ESMP into procurement documentation and contract works. Regarding 
the supervision component, the implementation of the ESMP and ESCP measures conducted by 
the project owner and PMU’s E&S specialist, in coordination with AFD. The evaluation step is 
defined by an environmental and social ex-post evaluation conducted by AFD or external 
consultants. 
60. 
Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure. The development of the 
programme has evolved based on consultations conducted with various stakeholders including 
national government representatives such as from National Designated Authorities, national 
companies in charge of electric energy, municipalities of the main cities in targeted countries, 
and main actors including those involved in capacity building and financing EV, charging 
infrastructure and in the battery value chain. The programme also provides for engagement of 
stakeholders during the implementation of the supported subprojects and capacity building 
activities through an ongoing process involving stakeholder analysis and planning, disclosure 
and dissemination of information, consultation and participation, grievance mechanism, and 
environmental and social performance reporting to affected communities. The ESMF provides 
an indicative outline of the stakeholder engagement plan required by all the subprojects. The AE 
will also disclose environmental and social documents at project level, in the same manner, and 
time frame as the safeguards documents, a summary of the activities, along with the 
environmental/social information. 
61. 
Grievance redress mechanism. The programme provides access to grievance redress 
mechanisms at the AE, implementing partners and subproject levels. The AE will use its own 
E&S complaints mechanism to ensure the independent handling of complaints. The project 
owner will design a GRM to receive and facilitate concern or conflict resolution in relation with 
E&S risks and impacts, with the purpose of resolving concerns or conflicts through an 
understandable and transparent consultative process consistent with project and/or local 
context. A project-level grievance redress mechanism will also work in conjunction with AFD´s 
Environmental and Social Complaints Mechanism. So far, the AE does not have a specific 
procedure to manage the risks related to GBV and SEAH. This is however covered by the 
operating procedures, which aims to identify, prevent or mitigate environmental and social 
risks and impacts and human rights violation that could result from AFD-funded activities. In 
particular, the AE relies on and applies the principle of the World Bank Environmental and 
Social Framework, which includes risk management measures related to SEAH. The AE is 
recommended to keep all relevant stakeholders informed once its corporate GRM is established 
and keep the ESMF and guidance to subproject GRMs updated. 
4.2 
Gender policy 
62. 
The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and 
therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. 
63. 
The gender assessment (using secondary data) provided an analysis of gender equality 
and women’s empowerment and how they link with the transport sector in the targeted 
countries of Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Mexico and Peru. All 
these countries have unique situations, and while the assessment has put a spotlight on most of 
their gender dynamics, country-level and transport sector-specific gender assessments are

=== PAGE 131 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 131 
 
 
 
 
required to comprehensively unpack these gender issues and tailor the countries’ gender action 
plans (GAPs) in alignment with the gender/social issues and the funding proposal. The AE has 
committed (in the GAP) to conducting additional sector-specific and country-level gender 
assessments with their accompanying GAPs, one for each targeted country. 
64. 
The gender assessment report also presents initiatives and good practices emerging 
from the targeted countries and region on gender equality and urban transportation. In general, 
for the Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) region, GBV remains a challenge and is 
underreported.  Before 2006, no country in the LAC region had established femicide as a crime, 
while by 2015, 16 countries had done so. Likewise, in many parts of the world, civil society has 
become strongly activated against GBV, and Argentina, Chile, Mexico, Peru and Uruguay have 
marched under the motto “Ni una menos” (Not one woman less). 
65. 
While the programme countries have laws and regulations that foster women’s access to 
economic opportunities, studies have shown that there are still constraints on women’s 
emancipation in many areas, including: access to productive resources (jobs, equal 
pay/benefits, etc.); constraints on women’s mobility (usage, fare schemes, time/commute 
factors); and safety (exposure to harassment and/or sexual violence and risks involved).The 
gender gaps and inequalities in the transport sector are enormous and require robust 
programming that is gender-responsive and potentially transformative in the long-run. The AE 
has put in place several activities to be implemented as outlined in the GAP. The implementation 
of the GAP will be led by the Deutsche Gesellschaft fu r Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ); it 
is important that the AE is clear on the modalities for implementation and ensures that the 
gender specialists to be recruited have practical gender experiences in the local countries. The 
AE is reminded that gender accountability is critical in achieving the gender results/outcomes 
presented in the funding proposal and annex 8 in particular.   
66. 
The programme will also integrate gender components in the projects financed by the 
programme, and this will be done through the inclusion of gender considerations in the 
investment plans; improved sex disaggregation of data collection on urban transport; improved 
women’s access to jobs; increased awareness on sexual harassment in public transport through 
capacity-building; promotion of gender equality at the workplace; and capacity-building for the 
staff of public transport projects on gender equality and work-life balance issues. The AE is 
required to ensure that the grievance mechanisms of the programme are accessible to both 
women and men.  
4.3 
Risks   
4.3.1. 
Overall programme assessment (medium risk)   
67. 
GCF is providing a senior loan of EUR 56 million and USD 40.27 million and a grant of  
EUR 22 million to accelerate EV deployment in seven countries in Latin America. The total co-
financing is estimated at EUR 133.5 million and USD 63.6 million from different co-financers 
such as AFD, Corporacio n Andina de Fomento (CAF), GIZ and KfW , and private and public 
investors in a form of grants, in-kind contributions, equity and loans. The exact allocation of 
each co-financier is yet to be determined, and the currency of the co-financing is indicative.  
68. 
The programme allows for the provision of financing for both public and private sector 
entities. The level of support and a business model per country will be determined during the 
implementation period on a “first come, first served” basis, depending on the needs of the 
subproject in each country and their level of maturity at the time of availability of GCF proceeds. 
The GCF grant will only be used to provide technical assistance (component 1) that cannot be 
financed by the private sector. All the subprojects must be aligned with the eligibility criteria in 
the term sheet.

=== PAGE 132 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 132 
 
 
 
 
4.3.2. 
Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme 
(low risk) 
69. 
AFD is the AE and co-executing entity (co-EE) (components 2 and 3) for this 
programme. In addition, GIZ will be acting as co-EE for the programme. AFD will be responsible 
for the implementation of eligible investments in the public sector. Proparco will act as an AFD 
agent and be in charge of the implementation of financing activities and the technical assistance 
measures of the programme in cases where the project owners belong to the private sector. GIZ 
will be implementing some of the technical assistance activities (activity 1.1, 1.2, 1.3 and 1.4 (i) 
to (v). 
4.3.3. 
Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)  
70. 
Delays in implementation and impact risk: The programme is planning to implement 
different business models (e.g. public sector-led direct/indirect delivery, public/private sector-
led indirect delivery) in multiple countries. The final financial structure and business model will 
be subject to due diligence by the AE and agreement with the borrower during the 
implementation. This great flexibility and complex implementation arrangement may pose a 
risk of delays in the programme. The current impact potential at the end of the programme 
(year 7) accounts for ~18 per cent of the total lifespan impact potential. This assumes that there 
is no delay in implementation. The success of the programme will depend on the ability of the 
AE/executing entity (EE) to generate the pipeline that aligns with the eligibility criteria and 
arrange the appropriate modality in a timely manner. 
71. 
Credit risk: GCF will be exposed to the credit risk of borrowers. GCF loans may be 
provided to sovereign with sovereign guarantee, municipalities without sovereign guarantee, 
and to the private sector. The sovereign credit rating of the participating countries ranges from 
non-investment grade to investment grade (from Ca to Baa1 by Moody’s). The projects are 
mainly targeted for intermediate cities, which may not always have sovereign guarantee. The 
credit risk of different borrowers varies depending on the countries and borrowers. GCF will 
rely on AFD’s due diligence to assess the borrower’s ability to service the loan. GCF and AFD 
funding will be provided in two separate tranches. Comfort may be drawn from the fact that the 
credit risk will be shared between GCF and AE on a pari passu basis and all the other unsecured 
and unsubordinated creditors. In addition, no more than 35 per cent of GCF funds will be 
invested in one specific country.  
72. 
Co-financing risk: The total co-financing amount is indicative. The equity contribution 
from the government or sponsor and other co-financing are not confirmed. Instead, the project 
eligibility criteria in the term sheet sets a minimum co-financing of 40 per cent in each 
subproject and a minimum co-financing of 60 per cent at the overall programme level. The 
covenants include a specific co-financing ratio for GCF reimbursable funds in component 2 
(1:1.9) and 3 (1:1.8) and GCF non-reimbursable funds for technical assistance (1.2:1).   
73. 
FX risks: As the GCF reimbursable funds will be provided in hard currencies (in EUR 
and USD), the volatility of the local currencies in participating countries could potentially affect 
returns of subprojects, and consequently the borrowers’ ability to repay. If the GCF proceeds 
approved by the Board become insufficient to complete the funded activities resulting from the 
exchange rate fluctuations, GCF shall not bear any responsibility of providing additional 
financing.  
4.3.4. 
Project viability and concessionality  
74. 
The programme enables a commercial uptake and mass deployment of EVs without 
further support from the GCF at the end of the programme in the targeted countries. It is

=== PAGE 133 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 133 
 
 
 
 
expected that commercial investors will be crowded in to the e-buses once the framework 
condition (e.g. concession contracts) and the performance risks of e-buses are manageable. 
However, the current term sheet does not have a split allocation between public and private 
sector projects. There is no specific target or indicators to track the likelihood of commercial 
uptake of EVs in the funding proposal. 
75. 
The interest rates of the GCF senior loans are differentiated by the public and private 
borrower. While the pricing to the public sector is fixed, the pricing to the private sector will be 
determined by the AE/EE on a case-by-case basis with different spread. The AE/EE will ensure 
that it follows the minimum concessionality principle and contractually ensure that the GCF 
concessionality passes down to the final borrowers. The pricing to the private sector will not be 
lower than the pricing for the public sector.  
4.3.5. 
Compliance risk (medium risk) 
76. 
Following a thorough assessment of various risk factors outlined in the proposal, the 
Secretariat has determined a compliance risk rating of ‘medium’ for the project. Upon 
evaluation, we have identified several key points warranting attention and consideration. 
77. 
First, regarding risk assessment, it is noted that due diligence of counterparts is 
contingent upon the project’s structure. Given the current status of preparation of subprojects 
(pre-feasibility stage), the organizational structure remains fluid, posing challenges to 
conducting comprehensive due diligence at this stage. It is essential to differentiate between 
eligibility criteria and due diligence questions, ensuring that the latter focuses on 
implementation and organizational issues. 
78. 
Second, concerns surrounding sanctions and prohibited practices have been addressed 
with assurances that project activities will not take place in jurisdictions affected by UN 
Sanctions List and that measures are in place to prevent involvement with sanctioned 
individuals or entities. Additionally, mechanisms are established to mitigate risks related to 
illicit practices through internal controls and adherence to anti-corruption policies. 
79. 
Third, the importance of robust counterparty due diligence is emphasized, with 
adherence to accredited procedures and regular assessments of technical capacity and fiduciary 
risks. Similarly, the anti-money-laundering and countering the financing of terrorism policy 
ensures compliance with integrity principles and due diligence at the project level. 
80. 
Additionally, stringent measures already in place to address E&S risks can also serve to 
address potential concerns regarding money-laundering, terrorist financing, or prohibited 
practices. GRMs, including stakeholder engagement plans and an established mechanism for 
handling complaints, underscore the commitment to transparency, accountability, and 
stakeholder engagement. 
81. 
 In summary, the Secretariat has conducted a review of the proposal in accordance with 
relevant Board-approved policies and does not find any material issue or deviation with respect 
to compliance issues. Based on available information for this funding proposal, the Secretariat 
has determined a risk rating of ‘medium’ and has no objection to this proposal proceeding at 
this time.

4.4 
Fiduciary  
83. 
The AFD is both the AE and the EE for the programme. GIZ is also the EE for the project. 
84. 
The programme will be implemented by three development banks that are members of 
the International Development Finance Club. These are AFD Group (including Proparco), CAF, 
KfW Development Bank, and a bilateral technical cooperation agency (GIZ). The roles of each 
are illustrated as follows: AFD, as AE, EE and co-financier for the financial assistance targeting 
public sector project owners (components 2 and 3), for the technical assistance dedicated to 
project preparation, and for the GAP activities related to the investment projects; Proparco, as 
an agent of AFD and co-financier for the financial assistance targeting private sector project 
owners; GIZ, as EE for technical assistance activities; and KfW Development Bank and CAF, as 
co-financiers for the financial assistance. 
85. 
A funded activity agreement (FAA), subsidiary agreement and co-financing agreement 
will be signed with each relevant party. A financing agreement will also be made between AFD 
or Proparco and the beneficiaries, which will describe the provisions and modalities for 
disbursing and using the funds for the purpose of implementing the projects, and will be 
developed and negotiated according to AFD Group templates and procedures. 
86. 
AFD will hire consulting companies to implement the technical assistance dedicated to 
the preparation of the projects and to support the Programme Management Unit activities. AFD 
will also hire external consultants as part of the Programme Management Unit. 
87. 
The programme will have a steering committee with the EEs (AFD, CAF, GIZ, KfW 
Development Bank and Proparco) as representative members. The steering committee shall 
notably review and approve the programme’s annual budget and work plans, discuss 
implementation issues and identify solutions, and ensure coordination and communication 
between EEs. 
88. 
The Programme Management Unit will ensure coordination between the partners as 
well as proper implementation of the programme. 
89. 
During the disbursement period, the AFD Group will receive regular reports from the 
beneficiaries in order to track progress and identify potential issues as well as improvement 
opportunities. In this document, the beneficiary will report on performance indicators, as well 
as qualitative information, as defined in the financing agreement signed with AFD Group. An 
annual performance report will be submitted to GCF following reporting requirements under 
the accreditation master agreement (AMA) and FAAs, in parallel with performance and financial 
information at country and borrower level. 
90. 
Audits will be performed as per AFD’s procedures and as per the AMA. AFD 
procurement will follow the procurement guidelines for AFD-financed contracts in foreign 
countries. 
4.5 
Results monitoring and reporting 
Compliance 
Medium

=== PAGE 135 ===
GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 135 
 
 
 
 
91. 
The E-Motion programme by AFD is a low-emission transport programme with an 
expected emission reduction impact of 4.3 MtCO2eq over a lifespan of 25 years. The overall 
programme targets seven countries in the LAC region; however, it is essential to note that the 
mitigation impact calculated for this programme is generated from only four countries. 
92. 
The theory of change adequately explains how the results chain will cascade from the 
goal statement of the programme to the proposed activities and also identifies the linkages 
between outputs, barriers and risks. Articulating the goal statement and outcomes helps 
understand the intervention’s climate impact and ancillary benefits (co-benefits). 
93. 
The logical framework has been well designed per the Integrated Results Management 
Framework. The paradigm shift potential indicators and enabling environment indicators 
describe the current scenario well as well as the potential target scenario, which rests on the 
successful implementation of EV deployments through new business models and a conducive 
policy framework for market adoption. 
94. 
Section E.3 of the logical framework has been designed with relevant details, including 
reliable means of verification and reporting on the appropriate core and supplementary 
indicators for the targeted results area. The annex on the mitigation impact calculation provides 
required information on the relevant clean development mechanism methodologies being 
applied to calculate the estimated impact. A separate monitoring manual has been submitted 
that explains the approach and how the various parameters related to EVs and public transport 
measures will be monitored. The monitoring and evaluation budget of the programme is within 
the range specified in the evaluation policy and provides the required details in the relevant 
annexes.  
4.6 
Legal assessment 
95. 
The AMA was signed with the AE on 11 November 2017 and became effective on 16 
January 2018. The AE’s five-year accreditation term lapsed on 15 January 2023 but was 
extended by decision B.37/18, paragraph (q), until the earlier of three years from the date of 
lapse of the five-year accreditation term or the date on which a revised accreditation framework 
is adopted by the Board. The Board approved the re-accreditation of the AE pursuant to 
decision B.36/10, and the negotiation of the amended and restated AMA is ongoing. 
96. 
The AE has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all 
internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme.  
97. 
The proposed programme will be implemented in seven countries: Argentina, Brazil, 
Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Mexico and Peru. GCF is not provided with privileges 
and immunities in these seven countries. This means that, among other things, GCF is not 
protected against litigation or expropriation in these countries, which risks need to be further 
assessed. The Secretariat has engaged with the relevant countries as follows: 
(a) 
Argentina: The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and 
immunities and a background note to the Government of Argentina most recently on 7 
February 2023. The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the 
Government of Argentina on 16 March 2023; 
(b) 
Brazil: The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities 
and a background note to the Government of Brazil most recently on 1 February 2023. 
The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government of Brazil 
on 22 February 2024; 
(c) 
Colombia: The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and 
immunities and a background note to the Government of Colombia most recently on 8

GCF/B.39/02/Add.12/Rev.01 
Page 136 
 
 
 
 
May 2024. The latest communication was sent by the GCF Secretariat to the Government 
of Colombia on 8 May 2024; 
(d)

made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and
